There are many elliptical and short speakings in Scriptures: so that the interpreter must necessarily insert the word, or words there understood, as Luke 7. 11. ἐν τῷ ἑξῆς understand ἡμέρᾳ : which the Syriac inserts; or καιρῷ as it seems Theophylact does, reading ἐν τῷ ἑξῆ : deinceps , as chap. 8. 1. ἐν τῷ καθεξῆς . So John 5. 36. I have witness , μείζω τοῦ Ἰωάννου ; that is, μείζω τῆς τοῦ Ἰωάννου as the learned Beza notes: the full speech must have been μείζω τῆς μαρτυρίας τοῦ Ἰωάννου : greater than that testimony of John. So Acts 15. 5. ἐξανέστησαν δέ τινες , there εἶπον ( they said ) is understood. So Mat. 21. 30. The second son said, ἐγώ ; understand ἀπέρχομαι , or ποιήσω , I will go, or will do so. So Mat. 1. 6. ἐκ τῆς τοῦ Οὐρίου where γυναικὸς and the word γενομένης are to be understood: Uriah's wife, which became David's. So Rom. 11. 4. τῇ Βάαλ , that is, τῇ εἰκόνι τοῦ Βάαλ . So Mark 14. 43. παρὰ τῶν Ἀρχιερέων : understand πεμφθέντες or ἀπεσταλθέντες , sent from the high Priests. See Acts 9. 11.
6. That the article ὁ , is sometimes put to signify that which is, or is called something notable, Mark 15. 16. ὅ ἐστι πραιτώριον , which is called the Pretory: sometimes that word is expressed: as, Luke 21. 37. τὸ ὄρος τὸ καλούμενον ἐλαιῶν , that is, τὸ ὄρος τῶν ἐλαιῶν : as, Mark 11. 1. Luke 22. 39. of which ellipsis of the word הר , we read also 2 Sam. 15. 30. Sometimes the Greeks double the article, and propose it to both names, John 1. 42. John 6. 69.
7. For conclusion then (seeing all the Scripture is infallibly true, and no jot thereof may pass away) we must understand that (whereas Luke here names Cainan, between Arphaxad and Sala, which Moses in the same genealogy does not, Gen. 10. 24.) Moses in that epitome of History, saying Arphaxad ילד jalad 70. ἐγέννησε begat Sala, must be no otherwise understood, than Mat. 1. 8. Joram ( ἐγέννησε ) begat Ozias : not immediately, but as one of his progenitors: as 1 Chron. 3. 11. etc. or as the whole series of this genealogy is elliptical υἱοῦ being so often understood: So may we here understand καλουμένου ; as if we read Ἔβερ τοῦ Σαλὰ , τοῦ καλουμένου Καινάν . Eber the son of Sala, who was also called Cainan ; and so this article τοῦ may here be understood, as set to distinguish this surnamed Cainan, from his ancestor of the same name mentioned, Gen. 5. 9, 12, 13, 14. and here verse 37. or if we understand here λεγομένου , it may only point out the vulgar error, which it may be the mistake or corruption of the 70. Interpreters brought in; that being then the vulgar and commonly received translation of the old Testament into the Greek tongue; and so may the Syriac reading here be understood, shala בר קינן bar Kainan , that is, דמת קרא בר קינן demeth kere bar Kainan , which is called the son of Cainan: ever necessary in difficult genealogies, is that rule of the Apostle, 1 Tim. 1. 4. that we give not heed to endless genealogies, which minister questions, rather than godly edifying which is in faith : the end of the commandment being charity out of a pure heart, and of a good conscience, and of faith unfeigned. See also Tit. 3. 9. These things are written that we may believe, that Jesus is the Christ the Son of God , and that believing, we may have life through his Name. John 20. 31.

*{The son of Sem}* Gen. 11. 10. etc.

**V. 37. ***{the son of Methusala}* Gen. 5. 21. etc.

**V. 38. ***{the Son of God}* God is the universal Parent, and Creator of all men. See Deut. 32. 6. 18, 19. But Adam's parentage extended to God his Maker, who had not (as all other mere men) any human Parent: therefore the Syriac reads *דמן* *אלהא* *demen elho*, who was of God.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1. ***{Jesus being full of the Holy Ghost}* Whose fullness dwelt in him from the time of his conception: but the graces thereof were now more fully revealed, when he began to execute the office of the Messiah: and hence we all receive, John 1. 16.

*{from Jordan}* After his baptism there, Matthew 3. 13. etc. Mark 1. 9.

*{led by the Spirit}* See on Mat. 4. 1. Mark 1. 12. Christ's temptation and fasting presently follow, and are adjoined to his baptism; as to teach us to expect Satan's fiercest charges when we give our names to Christ, and set our hearts to seek the Lord in newness and holiness of life; so how we must prepare and arm ourselves to resist temptations. See Mat. 26. 41. Mat. 17. 21.

**V. 2. ***{Forty days}* See on Mat. 4. 2. Mark 1. 13.

*{tempted of the devil}* Mat. 4. 3. He was in the wilderness forty days, where the devil tempted him: he was manifested that he might destroy the works of the devil: therefore he presently began his combat with him that he might overthrow his kingdom: he would not exceed the fasts of Moses and Elias; some think it was that Satan might not suspect him to be greater than they; *Theophylact, Euthymius*, lest otherwise, says *Chrysostom*, *ἄπιστος* *ἔδοξεν* *εἶναι* *ἡ* *τῆς* *σαρκὸς* *ἀνάληψις*, his assumption of flesh, should seem incredible. So also *Theophylact*, on Mat. 4.

*{eat nothing}* See Mat. 4. 2.

**V. 3. ***{If thou be the Son of God}* Thus Christ presently after his baptism is tempted, showing us therein, that Satan's temptations most rage in the sanctified; *quia victoria ei est magis exoptata de sanctis. Hilar. in Matth. canon.* 3. because he most desires victory over the Saints: others, *ut captivos & victos contemnit & praeterit, &c. Cyprian.* l. 1. ep. 1. he passes by as captives and conquered people, of whom he is already secure and assured.

*{command these stones, &c.}* See on Mat. 4. 3.

**V. 4. ***{it is written}* Deut. 8. 3.

*{word of God}* The meaning is by the Ordinance and Providence of God; whereby he enables the creature to nourish man. See Mat. 4. 4. 1 Tim. 4. 5. Gen. 1. 29.

**V. 5. ***{taking him up &c.}* Mat. 4. 8. That he showed Christ here, all the kingdoms of the world in a moment, declares that this showing, was by a vision: otherwise all could not have been under view in a moment, as here it was.

**V. 6. ***{that is delivered unto me}* This he spoke of his own, as a lying spirit, John 8. 44. for he can do no more than God permits him, (Rev. 13. 7.) neither in giving nor taking away. See Psal. 75. 6, 7. Mat. 6. 27.

**V. 7. ***{worship me}* Or, *fall down before me*: this the sacrilegious impudent spirit says, that failing in his other assaults, *nunc vel ambitione corrumperet, Hilar. can.* 3. he might (as he did the first Adam) corrupt him by ambition: it was ambition which cast him out of heaven; in which sin he still continues; affecting the honor due to God alone, in that he would have us serve and worship him; as idolaters do, 1 Cor. 10. 20. 2 Chron. 11. 15.

**V. 8. ***{get thee behind me}* Avoid and get you gone.

*{it is written}* Deut. 6. 13. & 10. 20. And so must we learn to quench all the fiery darts of the wicked, and to resist Satan with the sword of the Spirit, which is *the word of God*, Ephes. 6. 16, 17.

*{him only shalt thou serve}* Christ interprets Moses' words, by adding *him only, &c.* because this is the true worship of God, to serve him only, and no creature with him. See Rev. 19. 10. and 22. 8, 9. Mat. 4. 10. this is an excellent shield against ambition *caret enim ambitione religiosa devotio, Ambros. in Luc.* l. 4. c. 4. religious devotion is free from ambition.

**V. 9. ***{brought him to Jerusalem}* See on Mat. 4. 5.

*{cast thyself down}* Thus he tempts: persuading men to hurt themselves, because without their own consent, he can neither hurt them nor compel them to do any evil: the consent of the corrupted will is in every act of sin.

**V. 10. ***{For it is written}* Psal. 91. 11. Thus the devil (as impious heretics his disciples) perverts Scriptures, either citing part, and leaving out the rest, as here he leaves out (*to keep thee in all thy ways*: not in any evil way) or misinterpreting and misapplying them to their own destruction, 2 Pet. 3. 16. The Scripture says not, *cast thyself down*, this Satan adds. *Irenaeus* l. 5. c. 3. See *Nicet. com. in Gr. Nazian. orat.* 40. n. 11.

**V. 12. ***{Thou shalt not tempt the Lord}* Deut. 6. 16. Matthew 4. 7.

**V. 13. ***{for a season}* For he set upon him furiously in his passion. See Luke 22. 53. John 14. 10. in which last assaults, he came not (as *Ambros.* says) *tentaturus, sed aperte pugnaturus*; not to tempt, but only to fight.

**V. 14. ***{in the power of the Spirit}* That is, supported in that journey by divine power: so that he came speedily, by the same spirit, by which he was sustained without meat, forty days in the wilderness; and with which he was abundantly furnished for the execution of his Ministry.

**V. 15. ***{in their synagogues, &c.}* That is, in their Churches, wherein they met to hear the Lectures of the Law every Sabbath-day, Acts 15. 21. Jerusalem was the place which God had chosen for his Temple, sacrifices, and other public worship, there only to be performed.

*{glorified of all}* *δοξαζόμενος*, That is, magnified, admired, and praised, as for his doctrine delivered with divine power and authority, beyond the manner of all other teachers, Mat. 7. 28, 29. and here, verse 32. so also for his miracles, whereby he confirmed the same: by doing good to all, and in this (*all*) we must understand the most part: or excepting those, who out of envy, and the spirit of contradiction, distasted anything that was good: all others glorified him: the present praised him, and the absent celebrated his fame.

**V. 16. ***{on the sabbath-day}* See note on Mat. 12. 1. and Luke 6. 1. Mat. 13. 54.

*{and stood up for to read}* As the manner was to read some portion of the Law and the *Prophets every sabbath-day*. See Acts 13. 15. and 15. 21.

**V. 17. ***{There was delivered unto him}* By some officer of that synagogue; according to the custom, to show (says *Ambros. in Luc.* l. 4. c. 4.) that it was himself who spoke in the Prophets; and to repel their sacrilegious perverseness, who say that there was one God of the old Testament, and another of the new, etc.

**V. 18. ***{the spirit of the Lord is upon me}* See Isaiah 61. 1. The fullness of the spirit; all the gifts thereof: not by measure, as it was on the Prophets and Apostles, and is on the faithful Ministers, in some good measure to enable them to their office and ministration. See Isaiah 11. 2. John 3. 34. Col. 2. 9.

*{because he hath anointed me}* That is, endued me with graces, and assigned me thereto; the spirit resting on Christ, appeared by the effects to us-ward, and of whose fullness all we have received, *grace for grace*, John 1. 16.

*{to the poor}* He calls them poor, who had not yet tasted of the riches of his grace, and the poor in spirit; as Matth. 5. 3.

*{to heal the broken-hearted}* Those who have deep remorse for their sins, and terror of conscience to repentance: such he calls to him, Mat. 11. 28. See Isaiah 63. 2. Psal. 147. 3.

*{deliverance to the captives}* To those who were held captive of Satan and sin (such as we all were by nature, Ephes. 2. 3. Rom. 7. 14.) this he did when he preached and proclaimed the new covenant of God, that is, free remission of sins to those who believe in him: that he has fully satisfied God's justice for us: this whosoever truly believes, is by the same spirit (working faith and obedience in him) manumitted, and set at liberty from the kingdom of sin and Satan; although yet they exercised some tyranny over him in this present life. See John 8. 36. Rom. 6. 13, 14, 18.

*{recovery of sight to the blind}* That is, spiritual illumination to them who were as yet ignorant of Christ the true light: see chap. 1. 78. Mat. 4. 16. for so he discovered himself in deeds, that they might see him present in his works, of whom they used to read in the books of the Prophets. *Hilar. can.* 3. *in Matth.* See also Mat. 11. 5.

*{to set at liberty them that are bruised}* The meaning is, to free them who are burdened with sins, who wrestling under their heavy weight, have not of themselves, strength to shake them off: who are thoroughly humbled with the sense of their sins, and miseries: see Mat. 11. 28.

**V. 19. ***{the acceptable year}* He alludes to the year of Jubilee, Levit. 25. 10. etc. signifying the time of grace, and reconciliation, wherein God sets us at liberty in Christ, and shows his special goodness on mankind.

**V. 20. ***{he gave it again to the Minister}* Of that synagogue, of whom he had received the same. It is said here, *πτύξας* *τὸ* *βιβλίον*—closing the book; that is, rolling it up again; for they then used to write their books in vellum or parchment, which they rolled up. See hereof on Mat. 1. 1.

*{and sat down}* It seems it was then in use for teachers to sit, or stand, as occasion required, Acts 13. 16.

*{were fastened on him}* With desire to hear how he would interpret the place of Scripture which he had read unto them: as also for the fame they had heard of him; who without study, and ordinary means, became so incomparable a teacher. See John 7. 15.

**V. 21. ***{this Scripture is}* Or *has been fulfilled in your ears*; meaning, that the same scripture which by his reading, had sounded in their ears, was fulfilled, in his preaching: he being that person of whom the Prophet there spoke.

**V. 22. ***{all bare him witness}* All approved and commended his preaching, as extraordinary and admirable.

*{gracious words}* Or *words of grace*: which by a divine sweetness drew their affection, with secret influence, and persuasion of the Holy Ghost wherewith he was filled, John 1. 14. see Psal. 45. 2. Cant. 4. 3. Isaiah 50. 4.

**V. 23. ***{Physician heal thyself}* If you can do anything, show your power here in your own country; for he had done no miracle there: see Mark 6. 5. and here verse 25.

**V. 24. ***{No Prophet is accepted}* See Mat. 13. 57. Mark 6. 4. John 4. 44. As if he had said; as the Prophets were contemned in their own country: so am I here, and therefore, wonder not that I do fewer miracles here than in other places; you may thank yourselves for this: you contemn me and my ministration. This shows that God there withholds his graces, where his Ministers are contemned or undervalued.

**V. 25. ***{many widows}* See 1 Kings 17. 9.

*{shut up}* When it rained not in three years and six months, Jam. 5. 17. that which is said, 1 Kings 18. 1.—*the word of the Lord came unto Eliah in the third year, &c.* understand of the third year of his living at Zarephath: for it rained not on the earth by the space of three years, and six months, Jam. 5. 17.

**V. 26. ***{unto Sarepta}* God oftentimes prefers strangers before them of the household; for he is debtor to no man; therefore the men of Nazareth could not reasonably claim any privilege in Christ, because they believed not as others did.

**V. 27. ***{and many lepers were in Israel}* See 2 Kings 5. 14. This appears in those four lepers whom hunger emboldened to venture upon the tents of the Assyrians beleaguering Samaria, in the Prophet Elisha's days, 2 Kings 7. 3. etc.

**V. 28. ***{were filled with wrath}* As commonly it comes to pass among formal hypocrites, when their sins and punishments are touched and spoken of.

**V. 29. ***{and rose up}* *ἀναστάντες*, rising up, and dismissing the assembly: going out of the synagogue, they thrust him out of the City.

*{unto the brow of the hill}* To the cliffside, there to destroy him, by casting him down.

**V. 30. ***{But he passing through the midst of them, &c.}* He escaped miraculously out of their hands, which probably he did, not by making himself invisible, or any ways changing the essential properties of his body; but by his Divine power, making himself way through the midst of his enemies, See Psalm 18. 29. and that as says *Theophylact*, *οὐ* *τὸ* *παθεῖν* *φεύγων*, *ἀλλὰ* *τὸν* *καιρὸν* *ἀναμένων*; not to avoid suffering (for which he came into the world) but waiting for the appointed time, for his hour was not yet come; John 8. 59.

**V. 31. ***{He came down to Capernaum}* Being so evilly and unworthily entreated at Nazareth he did not command fire from Heaven, nor pestilence to destroy them; but leaving all to him that judges righteously, in pursuit of his ministry he goes to preach the Gospel at a neighboring city.

**V. 32. ***{for his word was with power}* That is, full of majesty and convincing authority: so that it smote the consciences of the hearers, with an awful reverence and fear: as Matthew 7. 29. Mark 1. 22.

**V. 33. ***{and in the Synagogue, &c.}* See Mark 1. 23.

**V. 34. ***{let us alone}* *ἔα*, Away, Syr. *sine me*; suffer me, or let me alone.

**V. 35. ***{had thrown them in the midst}* Had thrown the possessed man in the midst of the people present.

**V. 36. ***{what a word is this?}* *τίς* *λόγος*, etc. what thing is this? what power is it, which with a word can do these things? See Matth. 8. 27. Mark 1. 27.

**V. 37. ***{the fame of him went out}* One telling another what admirable things they had seen him do: and thereby God confirmed the Gospel, and drew men to Christ, that they might believe and be saved.

**V. 38. ***{and he arose out of the Synagogue, &c.}* See on Matt. 8. 14. Mark 1. 30.

**V. 39. ***{stood over her}* He stood by her; and took her by the hand to lift her up, whom he immediately cured.

**V. 40. ***{When the Sun was setting}* See on Matthew 8. 16. and on Mark 1. 32.

**V. 41. ***{and the devils}* Mark 1. 34.

*{thou art Christ the Son of God}* The devils confessed him in hope thereby to bring him into suspect with the multitude, therefore Christ forbade him to testify of him: for as *Tertul. advers. Marcion.* l. 4. c. 8. says, Christ would have men, not unclean spirits to acknowledge him to be the Son of God: he had sent those before him, who were to manifest him, *utique digniores praedicatores*; that is, more worthy Preachers.

*{to speak}* Or *to say they knew him to be Christ*.

**V. 42. ***{when it was day}* See Mark 1. 35.

**V. 43. ***{I must preach}* Mark 1. 37. shows the occasion of these words; *all men seek for thee*, said the Disciples to Christ: therefore he said unto them, *I must preach, etc.* he was not sent for one City only, but to be a Savior of the world.

**V. 44. ***{in the Synagogues of Galilee}* Herein he gives us an example of obedience to God; in that he, who might have lived in honor and ease in Capernaum, would with much labor and hazard, travel about to preach the Gospel, and gain souls to God.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 1. ***{And it came to pass}* See Matthew, 4. 18. Mark 1. 16.

*{the people pressed on him}* *ἐπικεῖσθαι* sometimes imports to be instant, or importunate: as Luke 23. 23. where it is said of the multitude of the Jews, requiring Christ to be crucified, *οἱ* *δὲ* *ἐπέκειντο*—*at illi imminebant, &c.* yet here it signifies a pressing near to, or thronging upon him to see and hear him.

*{lake of Gennesaret}* Which for the greatness, was called the sea of Galilee Matt. 4. 18. Mark 1. 16. Genesareth was a City in the tribe of Nephtali; which Herod beautified with munificence in buildings, and called it Tiberias, in honor of Tiberius Caesar: hence the adjacent region near Capernaum and Bethsaida, Mark 6. 53. as also this lake was so called. John 6. 1. It was anciently called *כנרת* *Cinneret*, and *כנרות* *Chinneroth*, Deut. 3. 17. Numb. 34. 11. Josh. 12. 2. and Chalde *גינוסר* *Ginosar*. from the corruption of both words, it was called Genesareth. See on John 21. 1.

**V. 2. ***{standing by the lake}* That is, lying near the shore: this manner of speaking is usual with Seamen, concerning a ship under sail; wherefore they say, she stands for the shore; or such and such a place: and the Latins used to call a road, where ships used to lie at anchor, *stationem, statio male fida carinis*.

*{and were washing, &c.}* To lay them up to some other time: as being for present without hope of taking anything after so much labor lost.

**V. 3. ***{And taught the people}* This Evangelist says not what he taught, but Matt. 4. 17. It appears that the sum was, *repent, for the kingdom of heaven is at hand*.

**V. 4. ***{Let down your net, &c.}* By this miracle, he will show his newly called Disciples his power, that they might not be offended at his poverty and form of a servant, which others contemned and despised.

**V. 5. ***{Master, we have toiled, &c.}* *ἐπιστάτης* properly signifies him that is chief governor or ruler.

*{at thy word}* According to your word: at your command, because you would have us so to do.

**V. 6. ***{A multitude of fishes}* So Christ blessed their labor; to teach them to depend on him, and his commands.

**V. 7. ***{Began to sink}* That is, as the Syriac reads: they were near sinking, here appears an evident motive and cause of the Disciples so soon leaving their ships, nets and callings to follow Christ, for they heard his admirable Doctrine, which they thus saw confirmed by miracle. See verse 9. 10.

**V. 8. ***{Depart from me}* This spoke he out of conscience of his own vileness, and the Divine presence of Christ. See 1 Kings 17. 18.

**V. 9. ***{Astonished, and all that were with him}* Not only Peter, but the rest were amazed to see the draught of fishes; apprehending a divine power in that work: therefore feared they the presence of Christ, by whose command all was done.

**V. 10. ***{Fear not, from henceforth thou shalt catch men}* To comfort them, he declares what shall be the effect of his presence; that they should thereby be enabled by preaching the Gospel, (which is that great draught-net of God's Kingdom, Matthew 13. 47.) to draw men out of the bitter floods of sin to life everlasting; hereto Christ enabled them as he promised, Matt. 4. 18. all our sufficiency is of God. 2 Cor. 3. 5. who therefore did call, and so now can all ignorant men, to an effectual and powerful Ministry of the Word to save souls: but whom he thus calls, he does miraculously make able Ministers of the New Testament, 2 Cor. 3. 6. which when the false Apostles, (who pretend to extraordinary calling) can make appear, that God has wrought in them, we shall believe they are indeed called to preach the Gospel: one thing is certain, Christ never called any idiot or unlearned man (to this Office) to leave him such, but to show a miraculous power in enabling him to that, to which he called him, though Christ said this to Peter, (who it seems out of deeper sense of his own sins, and the Majesty of Christ shining in this miracle, prayed Christ to depart from him) yet it was a common grace here promised to them all; and so no prerogative of Peter; though Christ addressed this comfort by name to Peter whom he saw most afraid.

**V. 11. ***{Forsook all and followed him}* They were called to be Disciples before, John 1. 39. but now to be Apostles: therefore, though after their first hearing Christ, they returned to their calling again; yet now they leave all, and follow Christ in the Ministry of the Gospel.

**V. 12. ***{And it came to pass}* See Matthew 8. 2. Mark 1. 40.

*{full of leprosy}* This disease was somewhat common in these Eastern parts, and very hard to be cured: so that when Christ (by his Word and putting forth his hand to touch him) cured it immediately, the power of his Godhead appeared, which could as easily repair, as create by his Word: See Matthew, 8. 3. etc.

**V. 13. ***{And touched him}* For, as *Tertullian* says, *adv. Marcion.* lib. 4. cap. 9. he touched him *à quo etsi homo inquinari potuisset, Deus utique non inquinaretur*. By whom though man might be made unclean, God could not.

**V. 14. ***{Charged him to tell no man}* For the time was not yet come that Christ would be glorified, being now in the form of a servant: and lest men who were not yet well acquainted with his Doctrine, should too much depend upon miracles.

*{goe and shew thyself to the Priest}* He would not dissolve the Law, yet in force, but leave them more inexcusable, in that seeing the miracle, they would not believe in Christ, See Leviticus, 14. 4. 10.

**V. 15. ***{So much more went there a fame of him}* They the more admired, and spoke good of him, because they perceived that he did these miracles, neither for gain nor applause of men.

**V. 16. ***{Withdrew himself}* He withdrew himself from the multitude which followed him, lest he might seem to affect popular applause, or to move or occasion sedition against the civil magistrate; as also sometimes to rest from labor of preaching and going to and fro to heal the sick and infirm.

*{and prayed}* Thus he manifested himself God in his miracles, and man in performance of his mediator's office in preaching to, and praying for his.

**V. 17. ***{The Pharisees, &c.}* See on Chap. 14. 1. See Matthew 3. 7.

*{the power of the Lord was present to heal}* That is, at this time the power of the Lord showed itself in healing the sick: it was ever with him; but it did not always show itself, as now when he preached the Gospel, and for the confirmation thereof, wrought these miracles. See Mark, 6. 5.

**V. 18. ***{Men brought in a bed a man, &c.}* See on Mat. 9. 2. and Mark 2. 3.

**V. 19. ***{Let him down through the tiling}* *διὰ* *τῶν* *κεράμων* through the pavements: for their houses were flat roofed, and railed, or battlemented according to the law. Deut. 22. 8. to secure them from falling off; these pavements laid with terrace, or the like material might keep out all wet, these they dug or broke through, Mark 2. 4. this sets out the great faith of these men, and their confidence of Christ his power and will to heal their sick: as concerning such pavements, See *Plin.* lib. 36. cap. 25.

**V. 20. ***{When he saw their Faith}* That is, discerned it, the faith of the sick, and the bearers.

*{man, thy sins are forgiven thee}* Meaning both guilt and punishment: intimating, that all misery of body and soul, is an effect of sin, this sick man believed, and is to be reckoned among them whose faith our Savior saw, and therefore he pronounced his pardon, and healed him.

**V. 21. ***{And the Scribes and Pharisees}* See on chap. 20. 1.

*{who is this that speaketh blasphemies?}* Thus they blasphemed in accusing him of blasphemy.

**V. 22. ***{Why reason you in your hearts}* Why think you such things? by discovering their thoughts, he declares himself to be God, the *καρδιογνώστης*; Acts 15. 8. searcher of hearts, and therefore that he alone has power to forgive sins.

**V. 23. ***{Thy sins be forgiven thee}* His Divine Power appeared in the miracle, which when they either knew not, or maliciously dissembled, he repeats the man's pardon, that thence, and upon their confession of that Principle, that none but God could forgive sins, they might conclude, and acknowledge him to be God and Man, and therefore the Savior of the world.

**V. 24. ***{Power to forgive sins}* See on Matth. 9. 6.

**V. 25. ***{He rose up}* See on Matt. 9. 7.

**V. 26. ***{And glorified God}* See on Matt. 9. 8.

**V. 27. ***After these things he went forth, &c.* See on Matt. 9. 9. Mark 2. 14.

*{sitting at the receipt of Custom}* That is, the Custom House, or place where they received Customs, or Imposts due to Caesar.

**V. 28. ***{He left all, rose up, &c.}* See Matt. 9. 9.

**V. 29. ***{A great feast}* Thus he expressed the joy of heart conceived from his present calling to follow Christ.

**V. 30. ***{Murmured}* Calumniating him, as if he were a friend of, and companion to the wicked: thus they hoped to discourage his newly called Disciples from following him.

**V. 31. ***{They that are whole need not the Physician}* See on Matt. 9. 12. hereby he declares their irrational malice: they murmured that he conversed with sinners: this was as *Augustine* says, *de verb. Apost. ser.* 8. to find fault, *quod in domum aegroti intravit medicus*, that the Physician went to the sick man: Christ came that he might dissolve sins, the work of man; and save man, the work of God.

**V. 32. ***{I came not to call the righteous}* Those who seem so to themselves: or it may be understood simply; before Christ called and justified them, none were righteous; had any been such, Christ needed not have died for their Redemption. See Matthew, 9. 13. 1 Tim. 1. 15. *Fulgentius de incarn. & gra. D. n. Jesu*, c. 22. says well: if that man were not by any means curable, the Heavenly Physician would not have come to recover him: again, if he could cure himself, *coelesti medico non egeret*, he should not have needed a Heavenly Physician, if he could by any means have cured himself.

*{but sinners to repentance}* See Matth. 9. 12. That is, to the acknowledgment, confession and detestation of their sins, and to newness and Holiness of life: for Repentance is not only in saying, I repent, God be merciful unto me, but in changing our minds, evil purposes and courses of life, and forsaking our sins: so the Syriac interprets it *לתי* *בותא* *latteio butho, ad conversionem*, rightly, for as *Tertullian* says, *advers. Marcion.* l. 2. c. 24. *poenitentia nomen, non ex delicti confessione, sed ex animi demutatione compositum est*: the name of Repentance (*μετάνοια*) is not composed, or derived from Confession of sin, but change of the mind: so the Verb *μετανοέω*, whence it comes properly signifies, *post factum sapio*, or I so grieve for my error or offense, that I will endeavor to mend it for the time to come.

**V. 33. ***{And they said unto him}* See Matt. 9. 14. Mark 2. 18. The Scribes and Pharisees ask this question, v. 30. When their cavil about his company washed off, they bring a second against his feasting: as if they had said, Why do you and your Disciples feast? Why do you not rather keep some fasting days? the Disciples of John came to Christ with the like question: it is likely, that the Scribes and Pharisees fomented the controversy on purpose to make dissention between John's Disciples and Christ's: this is the disposition not only of Hypocrites, but also of some infirm men, John 3. 26. to desire to draw all men to their way, and to obtrude that for a Law to others, which pleases themselves, specially in the placing sanctity in things Ceremonial and external: so that Scribes and Pharisees, and John's Disciples moved this question, and so they are united, Mark 2. 18.

*{and make prayers}* They mean some solemn, extraordinary, and long prayers, such as Pharisees used in the streets and open places, Matt. 6.

**V. 34. ***Can ye make the children of the Bride-chamber fast, &c.* Meaning, that now was their time of rejoicing, not of mourning, while Christ their Savior was with them; and that he would not burden his Disciples with too much rigor, for which they were not yet seasoned, nor more able to bear, than old Vessels to hold new Wine.

**V. 35. ***{Shall be taken away from them}* He means, that he would not be bodily present with them, after his ascension into Heaven.

*{then shall they fast}* He intimates the afflictions which his Disciples should endure after his ascension.

**V. 36. ***{A parable unto them}* See Matt. 9. 17. Mark 2. 22. *παραβολὴ*, a comparison or similitude, it is of *παραβάλλω*, *comparo*, it is here a comparing of Heavenly things with earthly, See on Matt. 13. 3. 18. etc.

*{no man putteth a piece of a new, &c.}* See on Matthew, 9. 16. Mark 2. 21.

**V. 37. ***{No man putteth new Wine}* See Matthew, 9. 17. Mark, 2. 22.

**V. 38. ***{New Wine must be put into, &c.}* See Matthew, 9. 17. Mark 2. 22.

**V. 39. ***{No man having drunk, &c.}* The sum and meaning is; that he disallows not seasonable Fasts, (For himself fasted) if they be without Hypocrisy, superstition, and vainglory; but would show, that men cannot so suddenly bear a change of life, from joy to sorrow; but, must be brought to it by little and little.

*{the old is better}* Commonly wholesomer, and to them that are accustomed thereto, more pleasing: so the rule of teaching and living, which Christ prescribed his, was of the Eternal will of God, and accommodated to his Disciples: but the Fasts of the Scribes and Pharisees, as their other external rites, were later inventions of their Elders.

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1. ***{And it came to pass}* See Matthew, 12. 1. Mark, 2. 23.

*{on the second Sabbath}* At the feast of Pentecost, when corn was ripe, Leviticus 23. 15. 14, 15. some think; others at the Passover: for it is said, Leviticus 23. 15. *ye shall count unto you from the morrow after the Sabbath* (70. *ἀπὸ* *τῆς* *ἐπαύριον* *τῶν* *σαββάτων*.—*unto the morrow after the seventh Sabbath, shall ye number*) *fifty days, &c.* See also Deuteronomy 16. 9. so that they understand here the morrow after the first Sabbath, or Passover: but these Feasts contained divers days, and so contained two Sabbaths: so that the Sabbath here termed *δευτερόπρωτον*, was the Sabbath next after the sixteenth of Nisan, or *אביב* *Abib*, (which signifies an *ear of corn*, for herein their corn began to ripen, and this was their first month, and contained part of our March, and part of April:) the Jews counting the forementioned fifty days, by numbering the weeks from the *δευτέρα*; whence the Feast of weeks was named, according to the number of the Sabbaths following the *δευτέρα*: so the first Sabbath following was *δευτερόπρωτον* *σάββατον*: the second *δευτεροδεύτερον*: the third *δευτεροτρίτον*, etc. See *Scaliger. de emend. temp.* l. 6. *Jo. Drus. praeterit.* lib. 3. *Is. Casaubon. Exercit.* 14. n. 1. D. *Godwin. Moses and Aaron.* lib. 3. cap. 5. *The. Beza*, on this place.

*{plucked ears of corn}* This was lawful for any to do, Deut. 23. 25. and for them, because they were hungry, Matt. 12. 1. as it was for David in his necessity to eat of the showbread, 1 Sam. 21. Rites and Ceremonies may not stand against Charity; which is the end of the Law, 1 Tim. 1. 4.

**V. 3. ***{What David did}* See on verse 1. and on Matthew, 12. 4.

*{they which were with him}* For though he went alone to the Priest, yet had he attendants staying for him, 1 Sam. 21. 2, 4.

**V. 4. ***{Which is not lawful to eat, &c.}* See Leviticus 24. 9.

**V. 5. ***{The Son of man, &c.}* He that gave this Law interprets the works of mercy, and necessity to be no breaches of the Sabbath day, Son of man often imports man, as Psalm 8. 5. Isaiah, 56. 2. so says *Athanasius. de com. essent. patris*, etc., *ἀνθρωπότητα* *αὐτοῦ*, *υἱὸν* *ἀνθρώπου*, his Human nature, the Son of man. Christ conversant as man with man, in the form of a servant: the Hebrews called obscure and vulgar men *בני* *אדם* *bene adam*, and the more illustrious and honorable *בני* *איש* so Psalm 49. 2. which give low and high. Ezekiel, is often styled *בן־אדם* Son of man, c. 2. 1. & 3. 1. etc. Christ willingly professed Humility, who could have styled himself the Son of the Most High, intimating his Glory: but thus he would for a time be clouded under the veil of his flesh, in which he came to suffer for men, that he might make them sons of God.

**V. 6. ***{On another Sabbath}* Matth. 12. 9. Mark 3. 1.

*{was withered}* After his former works, and doctrine concerning the Sabbath, he now proved by the testimony of God the Father, whose power concurred in his following miracle also, that he taught the truth: this poor man's hand was dead and useless, before Christ healed it: so Jeroboam's hand was withered, 1 Kings 13. some take it for the disease which the Greeks called *ἀτροφίαν*, a *tabes*, pining, drying up, and withering for lack of natural moisture to nourish and strengthen the disaffected part.

**V. 7. ***{Scribes}* See on c. 20. 1.

*{Watched him}* Seeing they could not fasten their calumny on the Disciples, they watched an occasion to accuse Christ of Sabbath-breaking: malice is as restless as the Devil.

**V. 8. ***{Knew their thoughts}* Whereby his Divine knowledge might appear: for God only knows the thoughts of men, 1 Kings 8. 39.

*{stand forth in the midst}* That the miracle might be evident, in that all present, seeing his withered hand suddenly changed, and recovered, only by his Word, might be convicted, or confirmed by the sight of that cure, which none but God could work.

**V. 9. ***{I will ask you one thing}* The Scribes and Pharisees had questioned, whether it were lawful to heal on the Sabbath. Matthew 12. 10. Now Christ asked them a question; *is it lawful on the Sabbath days to do good*: taking that for granted which all would confess, that it is indisputably good to show mercy and to heal: the whole controversy was concerning the Circumstance of time; now indeed as *Prosper* says, *male celebrat Sabbatum, qui ab operibus bonis vacat*, he mis-celebrates the Sabbath, who rests from good works, and he here puts *ψυχὴν*, for life: (for concerning saving of Souls on the Sabbath, there was no question) So *נפש* is often used for the life, though it properly signify the soul, Genesis 9. 4. Leviticus 17. 11. Lamentations 5. 9. 1 Chron. 11. 19. etc. and sometimes a Person Genesis 12. 5. Genesis 46. 15. etc.

**V. 10. ***{Looking round}* Upon his convicted adversaries, as it were to see, if any would answer, he looked with anger. See Mark 3. 5.

**V. 11. ***{Filled with madness}* That is, extreme anger and impatience; such is the condition of carnal and proud hearers, when anything spoken crosses their affection, or opinion, though never so erroneous.

**V. 12. ***{Continued all night, &c.}* To teach us vigilance and perseverance in prayer: what ought not you to do for your own salvation: seeing Christ spent whole nights in prayer for you? *Ambros. in luc.* l. 5. c. 6.

**V. 13. ***{And of them he chose twelve}* Of them that followed him he chose, according to the number of the Tribes of Israel and the Patriarchs. Matt. 10. Mark 3. 14. etc. & 6. 7.

*{Apostles}* The word signifies Ambassadors, or Messengers, See 2 Corinth. 5. 20. So *Clemens Romanus* said well, *οἱ* *ἀπόστολοι* *ἡμῖν* *εὐηγγελίσθησαν* *ἀπὸ* *τοῦ* *Κυρίου* *Ἰησοῦ* *Χριστοῦ*, etc. the Apostles being sent by Christ Jesus preached the Gospel to us, as Christ did being sent of God: *ad Corinth.* ep. pag. 54. he therefore chose those Apostles, (being to ascend into Heaven) that he might commit the charge of preaching the Gospel unto them; and that he might commit the charge of preaching the Gospel unto them; and that he might teach men by man's Ministry, sufficiently confirmed by the efficacy of his Spirit and power of miracles.

**V. 14. ***{whom he named Peter}* John 1. 22. Matthew, 10. 2.

**V. 15. ***{Simon called Zelotes}* Or *the Canaanite*, Matthew, 10. 4.

**V. 16. ***{And Judas Iscariot}* See on Matth. 10. 4. and John 6. 71.

*{Judas}* The traitor is chosen unto this Office, *non per imprudentiam, sed per providentiam*: not by ignorance, but by providence, *Ambros. in Luc.* l. 5. c. 6. How great is the mercy of Christ, how great his love to us? He took on him the infirmity of man, and therefore refused not these parts thereof: *Voluit deseri, voluit prodi, voluit ab Apostolo suo tradi, &c.* He would (for our sakes) be forsaken; he would be betrayed by his own Apostle; to the end, that if you be forsaken of friends, or betrayed by those you trusted; you may patiently bear the error of your judgment, and loss of your love and good opinion placed on the unworthy.

**V. 17. ***{came down with them}* That which Luke here records, seems some part of that which Christ taught, Matthew 5. 1, etc. though there it be said, that he went up into the mountain, and sat and taught: for he first went up and stood, and taught a smaller company. See Mark 3. 13. but perceiving greater confluences of people coming to hear, he descended from the top, for their better convenience of hearing him; and sat down upon the edge of some Plain, which was more capacious.

**V. 18. ***{they were healed}* Therefore descended he, that he might teach and heal, *καὶ* *διπλῶς* *εὐεργετήσῃ* *Theophylact*, *and bestow a double benefit* on their souls and bodies.

**V. 19. ***{there went virtue out of him}* He had power of healing all that touched him by Faith, because he was God and Man: the Prophets and other Saints had the gift of healing, but virtue did not so go out of them, for they were not *πηγαὶ* *δυνάμεως*; *the Fountains of Virtue, Theophylact.* See on Mark 5. 30. and Luke 8. 46.

**V. 20. ***{on his Disciples}* For, them chiefly would he instruct, encourage, and fit to be teachers of the Word: though not them only, See verse 27.

*{poor}* *Poor in spirit*, Matthew, 5. 3. That is, you, who being humbled with external or spiritual wants or pressures, wholly betake yourselves to God's mercy and providence. See on Matthew 5. 3.

**V. 21. ***{hunger now}* Isaiah 65. 13. Whose present afflictions being sanctified unto you, cause you to aspire to a better state in God's Kingdom, and to set your affections on things above, which cause you to hunger and thirst for righteousness, Matth. 5. 6. however these may seem to ignorant men, *non possunt tamen esse aliud quam beati, Salvian. de gub. Dei* l. 1. yet can they be nothing less than happy.

*{that weep now}* Isaiah 61. 3. In this present life: but he speaks all this concerning the Elect, which shall Eternally be comforted: neither the wicked poor, nor sorrowful, can be blessed, See Psalm 126. 1.

**V. 22. ***{and cast out your name, as evil}* This further expresses what he meant by separation, or casting out of their Synagogue, John 16. 2. when they expunge and put out your names from the Register of God's people: This is a dreadful sentence, and the *thunder of the Church*, when denounced on just cause, and by the true Ministers of Christ: if otherwise, whatever men think, they are blessed who keep not company with the wicked. Psalm 1. 1, etc. *nemo enim alieno censu miser est sed suo, Salvian* q. s. therefore can they not be unhappy in the false judgment of any man, who are truly blessed in their own conscience.

*{for the Son of man's sake}* That is, falsely calumniating you for your profession of Christ: this is to bear his reproach; Hebrews 13. 13. if any suffer deservedly for his faults, what thanks shall he have, 1 Pet. 4. 13, 14, etc.

**V. 23. ***{leap for joy}* As men surprised with abundant joy: intimating the certainty and fullness of their joy in Heaven, who here suffer any injury for Christ his sake; Greek *σκιρτήσατε* *σκιρτάω, Salio, salto*, it signifies such a leaping as wanton cattle use, and thence is taken to express such a rejoicing as so affects one, that he cannot but leap for joy, and sometimes spoken of things inanimate; as Psalm 114. 4, 6. *τὰ* *ὄρη* *ἐσκίρτησαν* *ὡσεὶ* *κριοὶ*—the mountains skipped like Rams—So the Hebrew word *רקד* signifies *exilivit, exultavit prae gaudio*. So Michal saw David at the coming of the Ark to Bethel *מרקד* *saltantem*: 1 Chron. 15. 29. which 2 Sam. 6. 14. is given, *in morem caprae saltabat Ad Mont.*

**V. 24. ***{Woe unto you that are rich}* Meaning those who trust in riches and serve them. Amos 6. 1. James 5. 1. or those who being rich are unthankful to God, or uncharitable and evil to men: *non census igitur, sed affectus in crimine est, Ambros. on Luc.* l. 5. c. 7. wealth is not in fault, but the affections of men: such have had their consolation here, and cannot reasonably expect it hereafter, who trust in them, make them their God, or any ways abuse them to sin.

**V. 25. ***{Woe unto you that are full}* See Isaiah, 32. 9. Isaiah, 65. 13. Amos 6. 1. Isaiah, 28. 7, 8. Ezekiel, 16. 49.

*{you that laugh}* That live at ease, and in pleasures, disengaging you from the service of God.

**V. 26. ***{when all men speak well of you}* Either, when wicked men speak well of you, which shall then only be, when you are like them: or when you affect the praise of men, and give not God the Glory in all things: or because it is very hard to hold Humility in general applauses: For the first, *Augustine* says, truly, *nemo enim laudat, nisi quod ei placet*; in Psalm 148. for the last, *Ignatius Epist. ad Tral.* says, *μὲ* *ἐπαινοῦντες*, *μαστιγοῦτε*; they that praise me, scourge me: and *Augustine To.* 4. *de serm. Domine in mont.* *Si ergo inter quos vivis te recte viventem non laudaverint, illi in errore sunt: si autem laudaverint, tu in periculo*: if your neighbors among whom you live well, praise you not: they are in error, but if they do, you are in danger.

**V. 27. ***{For I say unto you, &c.}* See Matthew, 5. 44. Romans 12. 20.

**V. 29. ***{unto him that smiteth thee, &c.}* See Mat. 5. 39. Meaning, be more ready to bear new injuries, than to avenge the old: it is not bearing, but doing injuries which makes a man unhappy; See 1 Cor. 6. 7.

**V. 38. ***{ask them not again}* That is, not by any violent, or unlawful means of recovery: otherwise, the use of Law and Magistrates is not denied Christians.

**V. 31. ***{as yee would that men should doe to you, &c.}* See Matt. 7. 12.

**V. 32. ***{For if you love them, &c.}* See Matthew, 5. 46.

*{what thanks have ye?}* What reward can you expect from God? it is no good work which only self-love, carnal respects, or desire of like favor from others causes.

**V. 34. ***{And if ye lend}* See Matt. 5. 42.

*{for sinners}* Such as have no fear of God to lead them to these civil offices.

**V. 35. ***{Love your enemies}* Matt. 5. 44.

*{hoping for nothing again}* This is to be understood with respect to men's abilities to give to the poor, as also that verse 30. So that if our neighbor's necessities require, and we be able, we must lend without Hope of being repaid by them: and in other cases we must not violate Charity, nor give scandal in requiring our debts. See Isai. 58. 3.

*{your reward shall be great}* It may be men will requite us with injuries, or be unthankful: it is sufficient that God will reward; if our labor be lost on the wicked, yet shall not our reward be lost with a just God.

**V. 36. ***{as your Father, &c.}* See on Matt. 5. 48.

**V. 37. ***{Judge not, and, &c.}* See on Matt. 7. 1.

**V. 38. ***{with the same measure}* See Matt. 7. 2.

**V. 39. ***{blind, lead, &c.}* Matt. 15. 14.

**V. 40. ***{the Disciple is not, &c.}* Matt. 10. 24.

**V. 41. ***{a mote in thy, &c.}* A small fault in your brother, and cannot see your own great sins. See on Matt. 7. 3.

**V. 42. ***{hypocrite}* See on Chap. 13. 15.

*{cast out first}* First amend your own faults, and then shall you be a more competent and fit reprover of other men's; See on Matthew, 7. 4.

**V. 43. ***{a good tree, &c.}* As if he said, deceive not yourself, you are not good, when you do evil; See Matt. 7. 16.

**V. 44. ***{for every tree}* Matt. 12. 33.

*{of thorns}* Matt. 7. 16.

**V. 45. ***{A good man}* Matt. 12. 35.

*{of the abundance}* Matt. 12. 34.

**V. 46. ***{Why call ye me Master, &c.}* See Matt. 7. 21. Mal. 1. 6. The meaning is, you do in vain profess yourselves hearers of my Word, except you do indeed that which I command therein.

**V. 47. ***{and doth them}* See on Matt. 7. 24.

**V. 48. ***{when the flood arose}* He means by this metaphor to declare, that temptations will show how every man has profited in hearing the Word, and that they shall put a manifest difference between the Hypocrite, and the true Disciple of Christ.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1. ***{He entered into Capernaum}* Matt. 8. 5.

**V. 2. ***{a certain Centurion's servant}* A captain of an hundred; he is not named: but it is likely that his company was in garrison at Capernaum.

*{sick and ready to die}* Matth. 8. 6. The disease is specified; it was a paralysis or palsy, which oftentimes bereaves of sense and motion.

**V. 3. ***{he sent unto him the Elders, &c.}* Matthew says he came unto him: whether it imports, that Matthew attributed to him, that which was done in his Name, and by his request: these whom he sent, were not vulgar or ordinary messengers, but Elders: so the Jews called their Senators and Magistrates of Cities; such as for years and authority were most reverenced: probably he had heard of so great a Prophet risen among them, who could, and readily would cure all diseases: therefore first he sent, then came himself to Christ, Matt. 8. 13.

**V. 4. ***{saying he was worthy}* This makes nothing for merits of works before God (our merits are *dona Dei*, the gifts of God, as *Alcuinus hom.* 8. *post. pentecost.* well observes after *Augustin. ep.* 105.) well do these men acknowledge this benefit received, commending the Centurion's love to their Nation, and beneficence towards them, as intending this for a motive whereby to entreat Christ to help him, though he were a Heathen, and a Soldier.

**V. 5. ***{a synagogue}* Or *Church*: it seems he was a Proselyte; this word is from the Greek *συνάγω*, to gather together: and *συναγωγὴ* though it be used to other matters, yet here, as commonly in the Gospel, it is taken for a house dedicated to the service and worship of God, as preaching, prayer, etc. the Hebrews called it *בית* *הכנסת* *beth hacneseth*, *the house of assembly*: so the Syriac here calls it *בית* *כנישתא* *beth cenushetto*: it comes of the Hebrew word *כנס* Syr. *כנס* *canas, cenas*, or *כנש* *cenash*: which signifies to assemble or gather together, Hist. 4. 16. etc. the Temple at Jerusalem was as the Cathedral Church, the Synagogues as parish Churches, for the ease of the people, who could not come every Sabbath-day up to Jerusalem: there they offered sacrifices, but in their Synagogues they might not.

**V. 6. ***{sent friends unto him saying}* That is, to say in his Name.

*{trouble not thyself}* Weary not yourself in taking so much pains to come to an unworthy man. See Mat. 8. 8.

**V. 7. ***{but say in a word}* Command that it be so, and I am confident, it shall be done. See Psalm 44. 4.

**V. 8. ***{a man set under authority}* See Matt. 8. 9.

**V. 9. ***{marvelled}* Matt. 8. 10. This is not to be understood of admiration properly taken, which is from ignorance of the cause, but of affecting and declaring a thing which was notable, and admirable to common reason; as that this Centurion being but an alien, should excel all the Israelites (which had hitherto believed in Christ) in his profession of Faith: thus would Christ provoke them to believe. See Rom. 10. 19.

**V. 10. ***{found the servant whole}* This, Matt. 8. 11, 12, 13. is more largely Historified.

**V. 11. ***{Naim}* Which was a town of Galilee, in the tribe of Issachar, not far from Tiberias; at the foot of mount Hermon; about four miles from Capernaum.

**V. 12. ***{There was a dead man carried}* So soon as he had cured the sick, he presently does a more admirable work in raising the dead: all things were possible to him, and he omitted no opportunity of doing good.

**V. 13. ***{Weep not}* Not forbidding natural affection, but allaying her sorrow, by comfort offered, as, 1 Thess. 4. 13. and not only in word, but in taking away the cause and occasion of her present sorrow.

**V. 14. ***{touched the bier}* Or *coffin*, wherein the dead body was borne toward the grave: this probably he did, that they which bore it might stay; and that the sorrowful mother, and others present might be the more attentive to observe the admirable power of his Word, restoring the dead to life again, that they might believe in him to Eternal life, who is the resurrection and the life. See John 11. 25.

*{I say unto thee}* I command you by that power which I have in Heaven and Earth, over the living and the dead, that you live and arise to life again: these examples of Christ's power, are our assurances of his raising up the dead in the general resurrection.

**V. 15. ***{sat up and began to speake}* This declared the evidence of the miracle, and a preludium of that which Christ says, John 5. 28, 29. *the hour is coming, in which all that are in the graves shall hear his voice, and shall come forth, &c.*

**V. 16. ***{there came a fear}* Because of their sense of a Divine presence showing itself in his raising the dead: it intimates a more thankful and more reverend Opinion, which they began to have of Christ, in whom they saw so divine a power.

*{hath visited his people}* The present state of the Jews was calamitous, and servile as if God had forsaken them: their comfort was, that he would send the Messiah to repair them: therefore from this which they saw done by Christ, they conclude that God now began to look upon them, in that (as they conceived) he had sent them a great Prophet, as a forerunner of their expected deliverer: so weak and perplexed was their Faith, in expectation of a glorious Temporal Messiah, and an Earthly Monarchy. See Chapter 1. 68.

**V. 17. ***{This manner of rumour}* That is, that God had now visited them, and raised them up a great Prophet.

**V. 18. ***The Disciples of John shewed him, &c.* See Matt. 11. 2. They probably were moved with some envy and emulation; as, John 3. 26. supposing that the fame of Jesus somewhat obscured the honor of John, whom they might possibly hope to be the Messiah and restorer of Israel.

**V. 19. ***{he that should come}* The expected Messiah and Redeemer; which John was assured of: but this he did to instruct and confirm his Disciples in the Faith of Christ, by their own experience of his power working such miracles, as none but God could do; and it is very likely, that they out of infirmity and perverse zeal to John's honor, might cause him to send them to Christ to be fully satisfied, in that which he said unto them, John 3. 27. 28, etc.

**V. 21. ***{in the same hour he cured many, &c.}* At the very time that these messengers of John came to Christ, the providence of God so disposed, that they should see many cured of such several and desperate maladies, that their incredulity might be convinced, not by John's words only, but also by the Divine works of Christ: and it is observable, that by the same providence, John Baptist, greater than a Prophet, should yet herein be less than divers of the Prophets that he did no miracle. John 10. 41. while Christ (as also the Apostles afterwards) did many that John's Disciples might the more easily be transmitted to Christ; who possibly might otherwise have been too tenacious of that suspicion which then amazed most men, chap. 3. 15.

**V. 22. ***{tell John what things ye have seen, &c.}* Without any ostentation of himself, he appeals to their own senses and reports, that they might conclude by that which was done and said, that those things which the Prophets long before spoke concerning the Messiah, were now done by Christ. See Isaiah 35. 5, 6. Isa. 61. 1.

*{to the poor of the Gospel, &c.}* That is, such as are of obscure and poor condition in the world: who feel their own misery and disability to stand in God's judgment.

**V. 23. ***{offended in me}* Who shall not stumble at my state of Humiliation as a Rock of offense: See Matth. 21. 42. Acts 4. 11. Romans 9. 33. 1 Cor. 1. 23. 1 Pet. 2. 7, 8. Psalm 118. 22. See on Matth. 11. 6.

**V. 24. ***{he began to speak unto the people}* How John's disciples behaved themselves toward Christ, or what they answered, none of the Evangelists record: but Christ addressed his discourse to the people present, to let them know that John Baptist (one of his witnesses) was above exception, and constant for the truth, lest they should think him imprisoned for any evil by him committed.

*{a reed shaken with the wind}* That is an inconstant man, or a thing of little moment.

**V. 25. ***{a man clothed in soft raiment}* See on Mat. 11. 8. a libertine or voluptuous liver! these interrogations have the value of a certain appeal to their own knowledges; as if he had said, did not yourselves ever find him a man of a most temperate, stayed, and austere life, void of all pride and delicacy, or ambition?

**V. 26. ***{more than a Prophet}* That is, an extraordinary or excellent Prophet: for he had the honor to be the immediate forerunner of the Messiah, to prepare his way, and to show him present. See on Mat. 11. 11. 9.

**V. 27. ***{Behold I send my &c.}* Mal. 3. 1.

**V. 28. ***{there is not a greater}* In office; because he did not (as the former Prophets) only tell of Christ to come, but pointed him out. John 1. 29. See on Mat. 11. 11.

*{in the kingdom of God}* See on Mat. 11. 11. The meaning is, in the new state of the Church, under the perfecter light of the Gospel, in which the glory of God shines: the meanest minister of Christ, who preaches the fulfilling of those things which John saw not, is, in that regard greater than John: so that here is not a comparison of the persons, but of their kind of doctrine. Or if we understand it of the kingdom of glory, the least therein is greater than the greatest mortal man.

**V. 29. ***{justified God}* Meaning they gave witness to the truth of God; and acknowledged him just, gracious, and merciful according to the preaching of John, whose doctrine they had embraced.

**V. 30. ***{the Pharisees}* See on chap. 14. 1. The proud and arrogant Pharisees (as many now do) thought themselves the most knowing men, and too good to be bettered with any teaching: therefore they *rejected the counsel of God, &c.* So the Syriac interprets with us: but the word *ἠθέτησαν* here used has a more emphatic signification: *ἄθετος*, is *irritus, ineptus, inutilis*, and *ἀθετέω* imports to abolish, as Gal. 2. 21. to despise, John 12. 48. Jude 8. to abrogate so, that it seems they rejected the ordinance of God, baptism, and the word declaring his counsel for man's salvation by Christ, as a vain foolish and unprofitable thing. See 1 Cor. 2. 14.

*{against themselves}* To their own loss and destruction, which they did *in themselves*, by their unbelief and contempt of the Gospel, which declares the counsel of God for man's salvation. See Acts 20. 27.

**V. 31. ***{Whereunto then}* See on Mat. 11. 16. This double query imports a vehemency of our Savior's speech, reproving the Jews' contumacy, and invincible hardness of heart, willfully rejecting the grace of God, who used all means to reduce them, but they would not. This manner of speaking then concludes neither ignorance, nor any hesitancy in Christ, as if he knew not to whom readily to compare or liken them, but a certain appeal to themselves; as if he had said, do you know anything in the world so perverse and peevish as this generation? not that he condemns all, (God had his elect among them still) but the Pharisees and Lawyers; and those their followers who rejected the grace and mercy of God offered them in the Gospel of Christ. See chap. 13. 18. 20.

**V. 32. ***{children sitting in &c.}* See on Mat. 11. 15. The meaning is, that whatever means God proposes to save the wicked, and however mercifully he complies with their infirmities, yet their obstinacy will find some let to their reception of the Gospel.

**V. 33. ***{Neither eating bread}* He lived not according to the ordinary custom of men; but alone in the wilderness. Matth. 3. conversing with few men, being of a most abstemious and austere life and conversation.

**V. 34. ***{Eating and drinking}* That is, conversing friendly with men: John was a retired man: but Christ used a more gracious familiarity, and they, who then most professed sanctity and religion, calumniated both: and would not believe their preaching.

**V. 35. ***{but wisdom}* But yet they who are truly wise, embrace that as the wisdom of God to salvation, which carnal men account the foolishness of preaching. 1 Cor. 1. 21. Rom. 1. 16.

**V. 36. ***{One of the Pharisees desired him}* See Mark 14. 3. & Luke 14. 1. it appears hereby that this Simon, though a Pharisee, was yet none of Christ's violent enemies, neither any despiser of his doctrine; and though not yet fully understanding the end of Christ his coming into the world, to call and save sinners (1 Tim. 1. 15.) yet had he a good esteem of his person, and reverend esteem of his doctrine, therefore Christ went to his house.

*{and sate down to meat}* That is, according to their manner, lying on pallets covered like tables, and leaning on the left elbow. See *Is. Casaubon exerc.* 16. n. 26.

**V. 37. ***{Behold a woman}* The circumstances show, that this was not she who is historified. Mat. 26. 6. and John 11. 2. who anointed the Lord with ointment; for that was a little before his passion; but this was a good time before it.

*{which was a sinner}* An harlot: a woman of notorious levity, a bad liver, it is likely that she had heard John Baptist, or Christ preaching repentance, grace, mercy and free remission of sins by faith in Christ; and seeing, or hearing of his works; believed him to be the very Savior of the world: therefore came she, as the sick to the Physician.

**V. 38. ***{At his feet behind him}* They lying down to meat as ver. 36. she had convenience to weep upon his feet.

*{and anointed them}* See on ver. 46.

**V. 39. ***{He spake within himself}* That is, he thought, as *Fulgentius ad Monim.* l. 3. interprets; *nihil aliud est apud se dicere, quam apud se cogitare*.

*{what manner of woman}* The Pharisee had respect to the law, concerning their pollution, who touched the polluted, Num. 19. 22. he knew not the gracious mercy of God in Christ. Here was Simon's failing: he thought the stains of sin so absolutely indelible, that what this sinner had been, she must be reputed still, not considering the power and mercy of God, converting sinners; making their scarlet sins white as snow; and them, new creatures, and therefore he concluded that it became not a Prophet to permit such a one to touch him: hence proceeded another foul error, that Christ did not know what condition she was of, and therefore that he was no Prophet, he might have considered that God reserved many things from the knowledge of the Prophets, but errors are fruitful.

**V. 40. ***{Jesus answering}* That is, said: the Hebrews so speak of him that begins a discourse: but here it properly betokens, that Christ being truly God the searcher of hearts, answered to the Pharisee's prejudicate thoughts, and so showed himself to be not only a knower of the external condition of others, which Prophets may be, but a knower and discerner of the thoughts of the heart, which only God can be.

**V. 41. ***{A certain Creditor}* The scope of this parable is, by proportion to show. 1. That, what, or how little soever a man owes to God, he is ever unable to make payment, and so satisfy his justice. 2. That as the debtor (whether of a greater, or smaller sum) is absolutely discharged of the whole debt, if his creditor (that is God) forgive him all; and therefore are no more to be condemned as sinners: such are all they who truly believe in Christ for their justification. See Rom. 5. 1. Rom. 8. 1. 33, 34. this, Simon who looked only on the law, knew not.

**V. 43. ***{Thou hast rightly judged}* From this principle of common reason Christ would make him understand, and condemn his own rash judgment: and therefore spoke he in a parable, that he might behold, as it were the sun under a cloud.

**V. 44. ***{Seest thou this woman}* Whom you contemn, and condemn as unworthy of this presence: yet she has done more than yourself, who have neglected some small and easy things in your entertainment.

*{thou gavest me no water}* It was their manner of entertaining strangers in those hot regions to give them water for their feet, to wash and cool them. Gen. 18. 4. & 19. 2. Judg. 19. 21. this Simon neglected: but the woman washed his feet with tears of repentance, most acceptable to God, who would not the death, but conversion of sinners; at which, the Angels in heaven rejoice, chap. 15. 7.

*{and wiped them with the hairs of her head}* This was a token of her repentance: the unchaste women are very careful and industrious in adorning their heads, which this convert so much neglected now, that she cast off all dressings, and used her disheveled hair to the meanest office, the wiping of his feet which she had wet with tears.

**V. 45. ***{Thou gavest me no kiss}* Which was the usual manner of salutations with the people of those parts. Rom. 16. 16. 1 Cor. 16. 20. 2 Cor. 13. 12. 1 Pet. 5. 14.

*{to kiss my feet}* This showed her humility, the surest ground of true conversion to Christ: God gives grace to the humble.

**V. 46. ***My head with oyl, &c.* That is, with ointment made of oil, and other sweet ingredients: it seems their custom was, at solemn feasts, to pour sweet ointment on the heads of their guests, this Simon neglected; but this convert anointed his feet, thus use the proud to condemn others, of whom themselves come short in duties to God.

**V. 47. ***{Her sins which are many}* Many, that is, as *Euthymius* well interprets, all: for where God forgives one sin, he forgives all.

*{are forgiven her}* Here is no satisfaction on her part, but free remission on God's: to v. 42. *ἐχαρίσατο*, he frankly, and freely forgave both.

*{for she loved much}* As this forgiveness of so great a debt of sin, is a just cause of much love; so was that so great love a certain Argument from the effect, of her sins' remission: for where God remits great sins, he also sheds abroad great love in the convert's heart: so that Christ alleges her love to God, not as a Cause, but as a certain consequence of her sins' remission, and so was she (as *Nazianzen* said of *Cyprian, orat.* 18. *πολὺς* *ἐν* *ἀσεβείᾳ*, *καὶ* *πλείων* *ἐν* *εὐσεβείᾳ*, abundant in sin, but more in sanctity.

**V. 48. ***{Thy sins are forgiven thee}* She came with faith, but wanted comfort for her afflicted soul (which her abundant tears declared) as appears in his pronouncing peace to her, verse 50. and thus she hears the judge's voice absolving her, whose preventing free grace gave her confidence to fly to the sanctuary of his mercy.

**V. 49. ***{to say within themselves}* *ἐν* *ἑαυτοῖς* may be as much as *πρὸς* *ἀλλήλους*, among themselves; it skills not much whether of these we here understand: they thought what they said, and Christ equally knew both. See on ver. 39.

*{Who is this?}* As in contempt of his person, bearing the form of a servant. Phil. 2. and truly resolving, that none can forgive sins, but God only. Mark 2. 7. sale of indulgences and pardons of sins by men, is but beguiling of ignorant souls.

**V. 50. ***{Thy faith hath saved thee}* To confirm her and to stop the gainsayers' mouths, he commends her faith, which alone can justify us before God, and bring us assurance of our reconciliation with him; this is to be understood of faith which works by love, Gal. 5. ever fruitful of good works: neither is our peace and salvation in the merit of faith, but in the sole merit of Christ apprehended by faith.

*{go in peace}* As chap. 8. 48. peace of conscience; which ever goes with remission of sins and justification (Mark 5. 34. Rom. 5. 1.) both which he specified, *ut de praeterito sit solicita, & secura de futuro*, solicitous for her sins past (that she neither relapse, nor become proud in forgetting them) and secure for the future; (that he who had once freely pronounced her pardon, would never recall it) *Bernard. Serm. in fest. B. Magd.*

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1. ***{Every city and village}* For being assigned of God, to the office of a Savior, to seek that which was lost, he would not expect that sinners should seek him: but as a good shepherd, he is found of them that sought him not, offering mercy and preventing with his grace. See Rom. 10. 20. Isai. 65. 1.

*{the glad tidings of the kingdom}* Of the kingdom of grace here, and glory in the world to come, that is, of man's reconciliation to God by Christ, free pardon of sins, justification, and salvation of those, who were exiles and aliens from his kingdom. See Mat. 3. 2. Rom. 14. 17. Col. 1. 13.

**V. 2. ***{Mary Magdalene}* This other name he adds to distinguish her from that Mary which was Martha's sister, chap. 10. and others of the same name, mentioned in the Gospel: she seems to have had this name, as *Suidas*, and some others say *ἀπὸ* *τόπου* from the place: some say from a village called Magdala, (which seems probable from chap. 24. 10. where the denominative is first put, *ἡ* *Μαγδαληνὴ* *Μαρία*. *Magdalen Mary*; as it were *Mary of Magdalen*) or from a castle or tower, which the Hebrews called *מגדל* *migdal*, Chalde. *מגדל* *magdal*. Genes. 11. 4. etc. others reject this opinion as improbable; see the learned *Is. Casaubon. Exercit.* 16. n. 1. That Christ cast seven Devils out of her, and a legion out of others, we may without controversy conclude the Divine Power of Christ, out of whose hands no power of hell can take his elect, John 10. as also our comfort in that Christ redeems sinners out of conditions hopeless and desperate, as was this woman's.

**V. 3. ***{Herod's steward}* *ἐπιτρόπου* *Ἡρώδου*, *procuratoris Herodis: Pomponius Laetus* says, that unto Caesar's steward was all his personal estate committed: there was also an officer so called, whose power and trust was such, as that what he did, stood ratified, as if Caesar had done or confirmed it under his own hand, *Fenestella de Magistr. Roman.* c. 26. this our Evangelist here mentions to show, that God can, and sometimes does call some out of the most impious societies of men. See Phil. 4.

*{of their substance}* In token of their gratitude to him: this received he (though he that could feed so many thousands with few loaves and fishes; and cause the fish to bring him tribute money; could as easily have rather given them riches and all other necessaries) that their reward might be the greater: that he might leave an example, that it is but just for those who administer spiritual things to receive the temporal of their hearers: he pronounces the laborer worthy of his hire; he threatens them who receive not, and relieve not his Ministers.

**V. 4. ***{Much people}* See Mat. 13. 2.

**V. 5. ***{A sower went out}* See on Mat. 13. 3.

*{by the ways side}* See on Mat. 13. 4.

**V. 6. ***{Some fell upon a rock}* See on Matthew 13. 5. Greek *ἐπὶ* *τὴν* *πέτραν*—on the rock, which Mar. 3. 5. is, *ἐπὶ* *τὰ* *πετρώδη*—upon stony, or rocky places; both to the same sense, as the Syriac gives it in both places by, *cecidit supra* *שועא* *Petram* or *Saxum*, the scope and purpose is to show, that hardness of auditors' hearts causes that so few of them bring forth fruits to faith, conversion, and perseverance to maturity of good resolutions, which the word works in them sometimes for a little season and then they wilt away again and come to nothing; and also why the word preached which works powerfully on some that hear it, nothing, to no good purpose, moves others; the reason is, because of the hardness of their hearts.

**V. 7. ***{Among thorns}* See on Mat. 13. 7.

**V. 8. ***{Other fell on good ground}* Mat. 13. 8.

**V. 9. ***{Ears to hear}* To understand and believe these things.

**V. 10. ***{In parables}* Mat. 13. 3. That is, dark speeches: the curse was on them. Isai. 6. 9. Mat. 13. 13. etc. because they would not understand and obey. God's word shall never want effect, it will be a savor of life to life, or of death to death to the hearers. 2 Cor. 2. 16.

**V. 11. ***{The parable is this}* That is, it signifies or represents this: and so understand the applications following, (*is*) does not always in Scripture signify an existence a literal or real being: but sometimes a *similitude, Sacramental* representation, and likeness: God sows when he causes his word to be preached by his Prophets, Apostles, and Ministers; see 1 Cor. 3. 5, 6, etc. the field represents the hearers, Mat. 13. 38. 1 Cor. 3. as there are divers veins of earth some fruitful, some barren; so is it with hearers: some bring forth to newness of life, and salvation: some not amending, grow worse, and nearer the curse for their contempt of God's word, Heb. 6. 7, 8.

**V. 12. ***{The word out of their hearts}* The meaning is; Satan took out of their hardened hearts the word which they heard; lest lying still in their memories, like seed in the ground, it should at last take root, and convert them.

**V. 13. ***{With joy}* Who take pleasure in hearing the promises in God's word concerning eternal and temporal blessings, in which they would be glad to share, though they hate to live the life of the righteous. See Numb. 23. 10.

*{for a while believe}* Because it was not rooted in God's truth and love, but on temporal ends, and respects merely mercenary; these failing, their seeming faith withered: and here, to the heat of the sun, is affliction, temptation and persecution resembled; for as the kindly heat and influence thereof makes fruitful, and ripens the well-rooted corn; but burns that which is not on good earth: so do afflictions, etc. More confirm, make fruitful, and ripe for God, the elect and true believers; but causes formal hypocrites to fall quite away: here appears the difference between temporary, or seeming faith, and that which is true and justifying, which never falls away, or is finally lost: because he that believes, has already everlasting life, John 3. 36. Again, the gifts and calling of God are without repentance, Rom. 11. 29. Again, Christ prays for the elect, that their faith may not fail. chap. 22. 32. so that though they who seem to believe, may, and do fall away; yet they who truly do believe, shall never, *vera fides remitti potest, amitti non potest*, true faith may sometimes have less evidence, but can never be quite lost.

**V. 14. ***{Go forth, &c.}* Return home to their affairs; and the cares and pleasures of this world, leave no place for the word of God to grow in their hearts, nor any leisure to think of it.

**V. 15. ***{Honest and good heart}* That which God has made so who takes away the stony heart, and gives a heart of flesh: so that here is not mentioned any cause, but a certain consequence, of a fruitful and good heart, it is true, that God only who gives the increase, gives faith and obedience towards the word: but wherever he gives such a heart, he also gives grace to retain and hold fast the word which shall bring forth good fruits.

*{with patience}* With perseverance unto the end: these bring forth fruits of sanctification to some maturity. Rom. 2. 7.

**V. 16. ***{No man lighteth}* See Matthew 5. 15. Mark 4. 21. Luke 11. 33. he warns his to do good with the light they have, and shall receive, that it might guide men according to the intent of the giver.

**V. 17. ***{For nothing is secret}* See Matt. 10. 26. Mark 4. 22. Luke 12. 2. 1 Cor. 4. 5. the meaning is, that though their preaching was yet but of obscure beginnings, and clouded by the malicious oppositions of adversaries, yet it should once gloriously break out and shine to the world, whereby the secret machinations of wicked and hypocritical enemies (who under pretense of religion persecuted the Gospel) should plainly appear.

**V. 18. ***{Take heed therefore how yee hear}* Take heed how you come affected to God's word (that it be with repentance, faith, and due preparation) how you behave yourselves in hearing (that it be with reverent attention) and how you practice when you have heard; that you may bring forth fruits accordingly: See on Mark 4. 24. of Israel's earrings, was the Idol made; Exodus 32. 2. etc. *qui male audit, conflare sacrilegium solet. Ambros. lib.* 7. *epist.* 36. sacrilege and most sins come of careless and evil hearing: as a defect or fault in the first digestion, causes the like in the rest, and consequently many diseases in the body; so is it proportionably in the soul: see more Mat. 13. 12. and 25. 29.

**V. 19. ***{Then came to him}* See Mat. 12. 46. Mark 3. 32.

**V. 20. ***{Thy mother and thy brethren}* See Mat. 12. 47. These were not any sons of Christ's Mother: nor the sons of Joseph by another wife (see *Jerom. contr. Helvid.* cap. 8. & cap. 9.) but were called his brethren according to the Custom of the Hebrews who so styled cousins: it may be they were sons of Joseph's brother, or Mary's sister; as was James.

**V. 21. ***{Mother, and my brethren}* Meaning that he would not neglect the work of God for any secular respect, but chiefly attend them, who hear the word of God and live thereafter.

**V. 22. ***{Now it came to pass}* Mat. 8. 23. Mark 4. 36.

**V. 23. ***{He fell asleep}* Into a sound, or very heavy sleep, as that doubled cry of his disciples awaking him imports, v. 24.

*{they were filled}* The ship began to founder, or to be filled with water, and so in danger of sinking.

**V. 24. ***{Master, Master, we perish}* Mar. 8. 25.

**V. 25. ***{Where is your faith?}* Mat. 8. 26. your confidence in me, which is an effect of faith.

**V. 26. ***{They arrived}* Mat. 8. 28. See on verse 27.

**V. 27. ***{A certain man, &c.}* Matth. 8. 28. this is called the country of the Gergesenes, which here, and Mark 5. is called the Country of the Gadarenes: it seems that Gadar and Gergessa, or Gerassa were two Towns, but both in the same continent, near the lake of Tiberias, over against Galilee. See note on Mat. 8. 28. and on Mark 5. 1. and whereas Matthew speaks of two men possessed meeting Christ, and here is but one mentioned, the order and circumstances, show it to be one and the same history, but where Matthew speaks of both the possessed, Luke and Mark speaks but of one of them, who either was better known, and more notorious, fierce, dangerous, or more cruelly tormented, by a whole legion of Devils.

*{but in tombs}* That is, (as the Syriac interpreter gives it) *in the place of Sepulchers*: among the graves of the dead: some think in caves, or arches built by, or over the Sepulchers.

**V. 28. ***{What have I to do, &c.}* The Devils usurping the parts and faculties of his body and soul whom they possessed, made the poor man cry this in his own name; whereas the presence of Jesus, was his happiness, but their torment, and so the spirit forced him to say this, in his own wrong, as they caused him to do many things.

*{torment me not}* Meaning, before the time, as Mat. 8. 29. for they knew they must not only be executioners of reprobates, but also be tormented themselves eternally, and with the greatest torments; for as much as their sin is greatest. 2 Pet. 2. 4. Jude 6.

**V. 29. ***{Caught him}* *συνηρπάκει*: snatched him up with violence.

*{therefore he was bound}* That is, the possessed man was bound, that he might do no harm to passersby. He was used to be bound, by the care possibly of his friends, or others whom it otherwise concerned.

*{brake the bands}* By the power of unclean spirits, he broke all chains and fetters: which shows that none but God was able to rescue this poor man out of their power.

*{driven of the Devil}* *ἠλαύνετο* signifies a violent and speedy driving: *ἐλαύνω*, *agito, stimulo incito, exagito, &c.* and that is here attributed either to one commander in chief; or it intimates that it was done by a common consent and instigation of all. Those spirits which possessed him.

**V. 30. ***{and he said Legion}* A legion consisted of 6100 footmen, and 726 horsemen: *Vegetius de re militar.* l. 2. c. 6. but here it seems put for a great indefinite number.

**V. 31. ***{into the deep}* That is, into hell; where the devils are in darkness. See Rev. 20. 3. 2 Pet. 2. 4. he calls that *ἄβυσσον*, which chap. 16. 23. the Evangelist calls *ᾅδην*, Hebrew *תהום* *tehom* a gulf, or depth without bottom: metaphorically, great, many desperate afflictions, in which men perish. Psal. 42. 7. or things unsearchable. Psal. 36. 6. literally for the depths of the seas: so some take it here: it is probable that the devils are confined to certain places; and permitted to infest some; but the sum of this petition was, that they might not be commanded to go out of this world, lest that thereby they should lose their opportunity of destroying, or hurting men.

**V. 32. ***{an herd of many swine}* Mark 5. 13. about two thousand: but why say some, did the Jews keep swine; seeing God's law made them unclean to them.
