Levit. 11. 7. we must know that Pompey had taken away Gadara from the Jews, which (it seems they had seized into their hands by force: and that the Jews, there or elsewhere inhabiting, which kept swine which themselves might neither offer in sacrifice, nor eat) did it to make gains thereof in selling them to the Romans or others.

**V. 33. ***{choked, &c.}* Drowned in the waters.

**V. 34. ***{they fled and went and told, &c.}* As being amazed, and afraid at that which was done, as also that they might acquaint the owners of the swine, of that which had befallen them.

**V. 35. ***{clothed, &c.}* Clothed with something which they who were present could spare him to cover his nakedness: for verse 27. he wore no clothes.

**V. 37. ***{back again}* Into Galilee.

**V. 38. ***{that he might be with him}* Mark 5. 18. The Gadarenes desired Jesus to depart from them; but he that had tasted of his mercy in delivering him from the devil's power, and tyranny, petitions Christ for this favor, that he might still be with him. Something hereto *Theophylact* says, possibly he feared, lest if he should be absent, or far from Jesus, the devils might invade him again: but it is very likely also that his love to Christ his deliverer; did draw his affection toward him, and the gracious doctrine which he likewise heard from him, in this compendium of History not specified; it being most improbable, that Christ would send away him, on whom he had showed so great mercy, without instructing him in the mysteries & doctrine of the Gospel: so they in and with whom the spirit of Jesus now lives, love his word and ordinances, in which he shows himself (Mat. 10. 20. Luke 10.) See Psal. 27. 4. others who have not tasted of God's mercy in Christ, and whom Satan commands, hate the ordinance & word of God, and if a Gadarene vote could prevail, would be no more troubled with Ministers, preaching, nor Christ himself.

**V. 39. ***{return to thine own house}* Christ would not have him depend on his bodily presence for protection against those spirits which had tormented him: but to know that he was able (as being God) to countermand the devils, and to save his: and also that he might testify to others, what God had done for him. Mark 5. 19.

*{throughout the whole city}* Of the Gadarenes which was one of the cities of Decapolis. Mark 5. 20. for Gadara was reckoned so. *Plin. nat. hist.* l. 5. c. 8.

**V. 40. ***{gladly received him}* Because they hoped that he would cure their sick; and many also desired to hear him.

**V. 41. ***{a man named Jairus}* Mat. 9. 18. Mark 5. 22.

*{a ruler of the synagogue}* Or *Church of the Jews*: See on chap. 13. 14. and on Mat. 9. 18. Acts 13. 15. the office of these rulers was to be wardens of the synagogues, and to take care that all things might be decently and orderly done there.

**V. 43. ***{all her living}* All that she had in bank, or all that she had to live on: for *βίος* signifies life, or livelihood, by which life is maintained; as, chap. 15. 30. etc.

**V. 44. ***{touched the border of his garment}* Mat. 9. 20.

**V. 45. ***{who touched me?}* Mark 5. 30. This he said, though he knew her thoughts, and that she was healed; *ne fructus fidei apud ignorantes latere videretur. Optatus* lib. 5. lest the fruit of her faith might otherwise have been concealed from them who knew not what was done.

**V. 46. ***{some body hath touched me}* He speaks of the end which some one of the multitude had proposed to herself, in assurance of being healed, if they could but come to touch his garments: this faith of the woman he manifests to the multitude, to invite them also to believe, that they might be healed and saved; therefore he asked who touched him, *ut confessionem certe provocaret*, says *Tertul. advers. Marc.* l. 4. to cause her confession.

*{virtue is gone out of me}* Understand here with *Fulgentius ad Thras.* l. 2. *divinae curationis effectum*: the effect of a divine cure: as if he had said, I have given virtue or power to heal, according to the faith of that party who desired to touch me.

**V. 47. ***{for what cause she had touched him}* For says Mat. 9. 21. *she said with herself* if I may but touch his garment I shall be whole: she was in modesty ashamed to tell men, but when she saw that she was not concealed, she glorified God in declaring to others the admirable cure she had: so, as *Optatus* said, l. 5. *invenit consilium tacita fides*; her silent faith found advice.

**V. 48. ***{thy faith hath made thee whole}* Not the merit or virtue of your faith, but his virtue upon whom your faith relied: he knew both her faith, and her cure.

*{go in peace}* See on chap. 7. 50.

**V. 49. ***{trouble not the Master}* This they said as ignorant of the power of Christ to raise the dead: as if they should have said, she is already past help, let him not lose his labor, for he comes too late.

**V. 50. ***{fear not}* Do not despair of help: he forbids not such fear as is necessary to beget a holy reverence; and to bring them for refuge and help to the power and mercy of God, who says, Hos. 5. 15. *in their affliction they will seek me early*, but would have them confident in his power and love: *sicut meliores sunt quos dirigit amor: ita plures quos corrigit timor. Aug. epist.* 50. as they are better whom love directs; so are they more whom fear amends.

**V. 51. ***{he suffereth none to go in}* Mark 5. 37.

**V. 52. ***{she is not dead}* Mat. 9. 24. Mark 5. 39. She is not so dead, but that she may and shall be raised: this he spoke to confirm their faith concerning the resurrection from the dead, intimating that it was as easy for him to raise the dead, as for one man to awake another out of sleep: and it is probable which *Euthymius* says; he permitted them to laugh, that they might be a manifest demonstration or witness of her being really dead and raised again: that they might not afterwards say that she was not dead.

**V. 54. ***{he put them all out}* All but those forenamed; verse 51. he would admit no profane scorners.

**V. 55. ***{give her meat}* That they might know that she was perfectly restored to life and health.

**V. 56. ***{tell no man what was done}* That verse 39. he bade the dispossessed man tell what great things God had done for him, and here forbade the parents to divulge what he had done for them, the reason may be, that though he would assign that man such an office, to express his gratitude to God, and to prepare the inhabitants of Decapolis for their conversion to the faith of Christ; yet being come from thence, and doing this miracle in Galilee, he would not have it noised abroad, as for that he would decline the importune malice of the Jews towards him; so also justly to punish these excluded scorners, by permitting them to their own unbelief.

## CHAPTER 9

**Verse 1. ***{Then he called his twelve disciples}* Mat. 10. 1. Mark 3. 13. and 6. 7. according to the number of the tribes of Israel: yet so far was the Priesthood of Christ, (after the order of Melchisedec. Hebr. 5. 6.) different from the priesthood of Aaron, that he chose not one Apostle out of the tribe of Levi, neither was he of that tribe, but of Judah.

*{authority over all devils}* That is; to cast any of them out of the possessed; which he did, as to give them confidence and assurance of his assistance, and their good success in preaching the Gospel: so to confirm the faith, and foment the obedience of their hearers that in both, God might be glorified, and the Gospel propagated.

**V. 2. ***{he sent them to preach}* In that he gave them power to do miracles, it shows his deity, for no mere man could give it: in that he sends them to preach as well as to do such works, it declares the principal end of miracles which is to confirm the doctrine preached; as also the difference between impostors and the ministers of Christ; they work lying wonders to gain credit to some falsehoods, but these do true miracles for the confirmation and manifestation of the truth: the Gospel having been confirmed sufficiently, needs no further confirmation: neither need the ministers thereof any miracles to justify them, when according to their lawful calling, they sincerely preach the truth of the Gospel.

**V. 3. ***{Take nothing for your journey}* Meaning that they should not be distracted with any cares, for God would cause those who received spiritual good of them, to administer temporal necessaries to them.

**V. 4. ***{and thence depart}* As you have occasion to go or stay.

**V. 5. ***{shake off the very dust}* Which was a sign of detestation, and their unworthiness to have the Gospel preached unto them. See Mat. 10. 14. Mark 6. 11. Luke 10. 11. Acts 13. 51.

**V. 6. ***{went through the towns, &c.}* According to his command who sent them.

**V. 7. ***{Now Herod}* See Mat. 14. 1. Mark 6. 14.

*{and he was perplexed}* This was Herod Antipas. Mat. 14. 1. etc. who was thus troubled at the fame of Jesus: the word here used is of *ἀπορέω* signifying not only to doubt as counselless, or ignorant which way to turn, but also to stick fast upon some inexplicable difficulties. Hebrew *ויתמה* *vajithmach* and he was amazed; Syr. *ומתדמר* *umethdammar*; and he wondered; but it signifies also *obstupuit*; as does also their *תמה* the same with the Hebr. *תמה* *tamah, attonitus fuit, obstupuit V. Shindl.* c. 24. 41. *Mart. Trostius.* Mar. 12. 17. & 16. 5. where we translate it *affrighted*: so indeed was Herod, (as it is commonly with men of blood and guilty consciences) affrighted, terrified and perplexed, at the sundry rumors which he heard concerning Christ; some saying John was risen again: some that Elias, or some other of the Prophets had appeared; there is no rest to the wicked, Isaiah 57. 20.

**V. 9. ***{desired to see him}* Not to be converted by him, but to satisfy his own curiosity; as, Luke 23. 8. in hope to see some miracle done by him.

**V. 10. ***{and he took them}* Mat. 14. 13. Mark 30. 32.

*{Bethsaida}* A man of Galilee near the lake of Genesarer, where Andrew, Peter, and Philip dwelt. John 1. to the desert hereto belonging he withdrew his disciples from the confluence of people, coming out of the near towns, that they might rest a while after their travel.

**V. 11. ***{received them}* That is, graciously entertained them: teaching and feeding them: so is he mercifully provident for the souls and bodies of those that seek him. See Psal. 34. 10. 1 Tim. 4. 8.

**V. 12. ***{When the day}* Mat. 14. 15. Mark 6. 35. John 6. 5.

*{began to wear away}* *κλίνειν*, decline, or shut up. When it grew towards night.

**V. 13. ***{except we should go and buy}* This manner of speaking shows that the Apostles thought it impossible to feed and satisfy so great a multitude, having so little provision, which sets off the truth and greatness of the miracle.

**V. 14. ***{for they were &c.}* That is, there were present so many. Five thousand men beside women and children. Mat. 14. 21.

**V. 15. ***{fifties in a company}* That which this Evangelist calls here *κλισίας*: the Syriac interpreter gives *discubitus*, and our translation *company*. Mark 6. 39. is given *συμπόσια*, *συμπόσια*, as it were by tables; that is, as many in a company as a table would hold: this was done for the better convenience of serving them all distinctly and orderly, which in a confused throng they could not so have done. As also that their number might more distinctly appear; to the greater evidence of the miracle, and God's honor.

**V. 16. ***{looking up to heaven}* As showing whence all blessings come and must be expected.

*{blessed them}* Gave thanks. John 6. 11. praying that God would bless and sanctify those gifts to the receivers. See 1 Tim. 4. 5.

**V. 17. ***{were all filled}* Whereby it appears that man lives not by bread only, but by the word and providence of God: and that our life is not in abundance, but in God's blessing, who can make a little sufficient.

**V. 18. ***{And it came to pass}* Mat. 16. 13. Mark 8. 27.

*{alone praying}* That is, apart from the multitude.

*{and he asked them}* That is, when he had done praying. Either he was praying alone, and his disciples afterward came to him, or this *κατὰ* *μόνας*, alone, is put in opposition to all other company but himself and his disciples: the former sense seems most probable.

*{Whom say the people that I am?}* This he asks, not being ignorant of the thoughts of men, but because the faith and confession of the Apostles, whom he appointed to be the light of the world, ought to outshine all vulgar faith and confession, he requires it of them, verse 20. and Peter as the speaker for the rest, answers, *the Christ of God*; that person whom God fore-promised, and anointed, above all those that (as his types) were anointed; in whom the offices of King, Priests, and Prophet met together, for the work of our redemption.

**V. 19. ***{John the Baptist, &c.}* See Mat. 16. 14.

**V. 20. ***{the Christ of God}* See on Mat. 16. 16. John 6. 69. Christ the Son of the living God; the anointed of the Lord: the Messiah and Savior of the world.

**V. 21. ***{tell it to no man}* Till the appointed time, when it was to be divulged to the world, Mat. 10. 27. which he says not, as if it did not highly concern all the Elect to know and confess the same: but because he would not yet have his glory manifested before he had accomplished the work of the cross, in suffering an ignominious death, to redeem us from death and hell; thus would he yet leave the devil in suspense: but principally, lest the faith of the disciples thus confessing, should be dangerously shaken in the sight of his bitter passion, and lest their auditors should have suspected the truth they preached (neither these, nor they yet fully understanding the mystery of Christ's death, resurrection, and ascension into heaven) and so the perversion of order in things opportunely to be manifested and known, should have proved, but as removing of the steps from them who were by such degrees to have ascended to that great mystery of godliness and salvation by Christ. He would therefore be declared *to be the Son of God by the resurrection from the dead*. Rom. 1. 4. after which the commission ran general. Mat. 10. 27. Mark 16. 15. *preach the Gospel to every creature*: this present restraint then, is limited; as that, Mat. 17. 9. *Tell the vision to no man, until the Son of man be risen from the dead*: and this the following words declare, which foretell of his passion.

**V. 22. ***{Son of man must suffer, &c.}* See on chap. 6. 5. He that was God and man in one person; in his Godhead impassible, was to suffer in his human body and soul, to redeem us, bodies and souls: to which God had assigned him from eternity: therefore as he is called *the Lamb of God which taketh away the sin of the world*. John 1. 29. So is he, *the Lamb slain from the foundation of the world*. Rev. 13. 8.

**V. 23. ***{he said unto them all}* Mat. 10. 38. Mat. 16. 24. Mark 8. 34. Luke 14. 27.

*{his crosse daily}* Meaning, if God should please to call us to daily afflictions, as it sometimes comes to pass. Rom. 8. 36. 1 Cor. 15. 31. we must yet endure and persevere unto the end. Mat. 10. 22. and 24. 13. we are therefore here admonished to be always prepared to bear afflictions for Christ's sake, and to follow him in obedience and holy imitation of his humility and patience: so must all they who will follow Christ, deny themselves, not regarding their own profits or lives, in respect of his will and glory: so Christ sought not his own, but his Father's will.

**V. 24. ***{will save his life, &c.}* That is, whosoever will set more by his own ease, safety, or life, than Christ the way, truth, and life, shall lose himself to eternity: but he that will expose himself to perils and death itself, where the honor of Christ shall require it, shall be saved: *Salvian* therefore advises well, *de gub. Dei* l. 5. *sine, repudia te, ut recipiaris a Christo*, renounce yourself, that Christ may receive you. You cannot by any of your endeavors save yourself; that Christ alone can do: if you will be sure of salvation, rely on Christ for it, not on yourself, or any creatures.

**V. 25. ***{For what is a man advantaged, &c.}* Mat. 16. 26. Mark 8. 36. Doubt not therefore to part with any worldly thing for your salvation: because, *si te amiseris omnia in te perdis, Timoth. ad Eccle. Cath.* l. 3. if you lose yourself, in that one loss you lose all; but if your soul be saved, in that one gain, you obtain all things desirable.

**V. 26. ***{Whosoever shall be saved, &c.}* Mat. 10. 33. Mark 8. 38. Luke 12. 9. 2 Tim. 2. 12.

*{when he shall come}* To judge the living and the dead, with glory, and many Angels attending his tribunal: then will not Christ own him, who shall here before men, deny him, for fear or favor of men, or for love of this world, and the things thereof; this does not only concern apostates openly denying Christ, but (as *Fulgentius* l. 1. *ad Thras.* well notes) him also *qui silentio firmat errorem*, who by his silence confirms error *silendo non adstruit veritatem*; and by holding his peace does not confirm the truth; *divinam contumeliam qui non refutavit, accumulat*. He that repels not God's dishonor (what he possibly can) adds to it; the negligent or sleepy sentinel betrays his Prince's Tents.

**V. 27. ***{But I tell you}* Matth. 16. 28. Mark 9. 1.

*{till they see the kingdom of, &c.}* Some present shall not die (or see death; as, John 8. 27. Hebrews 2. 9.) until they see the propagation of the kingdom of Christ, in and after his ascension into Heaven: and a preludium of his glory in his transfiguration, Matthew 17. 2. etc. and here verse 29. etc.

**V. 28. ***{it came to pass about eight days after}* Matth. 17. 1. Mark 9. 2. Matthew and Mark say six days after. So that Luke takes those six with either limit included, that is reckoning to the six, the day preceding the first, and the day following the last thereof, which the other Evangelists take exclusively.

*{these sayings}* Or *things*.

*{took Peter and John and James}* That they might first be spectators of his glory, whom he would afterwards make witnesses of his bitter Agony, and sad conflict with the pains of death and hell: what this mount was we may not curiously dispute, the Scripture not declaring it: the common opinion is, that it was mount Tabor in the midst of Galilee, specified, Psalm 89. Hosea 5. Peter calls it the holy mount, 2 Pet. 1. 18. See on Mat. 17. 1.

**V. 29. ***{his countenance was altered}* Gr. *ἕτερον* *another*, or changed from the ordinary appearance, beginning to shine with an admirable and divine beauty: and so his garments were light and glorious; and in heavenly glory shined in the Angels appearing, Matth. 28. 3. Revel. 4. 4. this light of Majesty which they now saw, was a slender ray of his Divine glory, but much more excellent, than that wherewith Moses' face shined, which the people were not able to behold, Exod. 34. showing us the mystery, the disability of men under the veil, to look unto the end of the law Christ Jesus, 2 Cor. 3. 13. by this they now saw, Christ would give them a taste of that glory, which they shall see when he shall appear *glorious in his Saints*, 2 Thess. 1. 14. Coloss. 3. 4. 1 John 3. 2. Phil. 3. 21.

**V. 30. ***{Moses and Elias}* These also appeared glorified; possibly to show these disciples, that Christ was not against the Law and the Prophets, as the unbelieving Jews would persuade men, for they gave testimony unto him: and because these two were the most famous in the Old Testament.

**V. 31. ***{of his decease}* Of his death and passion at Jerusalem.

**V. 32. ***{were heavy with sleep}* Either with joy, or fear and admiration, or a divine rapture: see Dan. 8. 18. and 10. 9. or overcome with watching, for they stayed there all night verse 17.

**V. 33. ***{not knowing what he said}* So the Spirit of God opened his understanding to know Moses and Elias, whom he never before had seen.

**V. 34. ***{overshadowed them}* Them, that is the disciples, by means whereof Moses and Elias were taken out of their sight, after this conference ended: it seems the Apostles, imagined that his kingdom was now established to him, and begun; or they spoke to him that he might avoid the suffering of which they conferred, by staying still in the mount.

*{and they feared}* Because it was an extraordinary cloud wherein the glory of God appeared: and the voice was presently heard sounding from heaven, 2 Pet. 1. 17. Mark 3.

**V. 35. ***{This is my beloved Son}* The only Son of God by nature, for whose sake we are beloved, Ephes. 1. 6. in whom are all the treasures of wisdom: therefore under pain of rejection from God, we must hear and obey him, Deut. 18. 18, 19.

**V. 36. ***{was found alone}* Having put off that glory, and resumed his form of a servant.

*{and told no man}* Until Christ was risen from the dead, for so he charged them, Mark 9. 9.

**V. 37. ***{and it came to pass}* See Mat. 17. 14. Mark 9. 17.

**V. 38. ***{look upon my son}* That is, have mercy upon him, and help him: for we willingly look on those whom we pity and would help.

**V. 39. ***{a spirit taketh him}* That is torments him, making him foam and tear himself, and dash himself against the ground. See Matth. 17. 16, 17. Mark 9. 20.

*{teareth him}* Mark 1. 26. & 9. 20.

**V. 40. ***{and they could not}* See on Mark 9. 18.

**V. 41. ***{O faithless and perverse}* Thus sharply does he reprove the Apostles' unbelief. See Matth. 17. 20.

**V. 42. ***{the devil threw him down}* Being enraged (as he uses to be) because the child was brought to Christ: and because he knew, that thereby he should be cast out of possession.

**V. 43. ***{at the mighty power of God}* Which appeared in his admirable work of Christ's, commanding the unclean spirits, out of their possession.

**V. 44. ***{let these sayings sink down into your ears}* That is, mark them diligently and remember them well; That is, lay them to heart, and remember what you hear, and see; that when you see me dying, you may not be scandalized, as if I suffered such things out of infirmity, or because I could not deliver myself; for in reason you may know, that he that has wrought such miracles *ἠδύνατο* *ἂν* *μηδὲ* *σταυρωθῆναι*: *Theophylact*; could, if he had pleased, not have been crucified.

**V. 45. ***{they understood not this saying}* They were so confident of Christ's temporal kingdom, that they could not understand him, when he spoke of his suffering death: after his resurrection they understood it, John 2. 22. now they feared to ask him, because he had a little before so sharply reproved Peter about it.

**V. 46. ***{which of them should be greatest}* Possibly the occasion of this question might now be his speech concerning his death; they desiring to know, which of them should be the chief or head, of his supposed earthly kingdom in his stead; for yet they understood not his spiritual kingdom: but here, as also chap. 22. Christ reproves their ambition.

**V. 47. ***{perceiving the thoughts of their hearts}* For he is truly God, as truly man: none can search and know the heart but God, Jer. 17. 10. the very Heathens knew this: therefore some of them dedicated to their supposed Gods *ὦτα* *καὶ* *τοὺς* *ὀφθαλμοὺς*, ears and eyes of some precious materials, intimating that God heard and saw all things. *Clem. Alexandr. strom.* lib. 5.

**V. 48. ***{he that is least among you}* True greatness before God, is in sincere innocency and humility, such as is commonly in little children.

**V. 49. ***{and John answered}* That is, thus began to say, Mark 9. 38. It is an Hebrew manner of speaking (See chap. 7. 40.) occasioned by the former passage, concerning their not casting out the devil.

*{he followeth not with us}* He follows you, not in our society. It might seem strange that any should cast out devils in the name of Christ, who did not follow Christ: but we must know, that though this power was principally given to the Apostles to confirm their preaching, yet was it not so confined to their office, but that Christ might give the same also to other believers. See Mark 16. 17.

**V. 50. ***{Is for us}* Though he be not with us in body, or external communion; yet he adheres to us in faith, affection, consent, in doctrine, and spiritual communion.

**V. 51. ***{received up}* By his ascension into heaven, after his death and resurrection.

*{he stedfastly set his face}* This is an Hebrew manner of speaking, signifying a resolving, and setting a man's self to do a thing, Jer. 42. 15. Ezek. 4. 3. God says here to the Prophet *והכינותה* *את* *פניך*, *vahachinotha eth phanecha*, *set thy face against it*: the word is of *כון* *Cun*, which among other things, signifies to be prepared, firm, and constant, resolved not to be diverted; or to be fitted, disposed, or intent to do something. So here, the time of Christ's passion drawing near, he resolved (whatever flesh and blood suggested; or Peter had persuaded to the contrary, Mat. 16. 22.) to go to Jerusalem. See on Mat. 19. 1.

**V. 52. ***{to make ready for him}* Which was needful; because Christ was attended, not only by the twelve Apostles, but many others who followed him, as disciples and hearers.

**V. 53. ***{his face was as though, &c.}* The Samaritans took special notice of any that went up to Jerusalem to worship, because they had a Temple of their own for their false worship on mount Gerizim, built in opposition to the Temple of God at Jerusalem. See John 4. 20. now it being near the Passover, the Samaritans took the more notice of their going that way, and therefore would not entertain them: moreover, the Jews having no commerce with the Samaritans. John 4. 9. there was the more need of forerunners to make provision for them, who could not in an ordinary, or then usual course, have expected any necessary entertainment there.

**V. 54. ***{even as Elias did}* See 2 Kings 1. 10. Possibly, that very Region put them in mind to desire fire from heaven, where Elias by fire from heaven obtained destruction of the two Captains with their fifties sent to fetch him to their King, thinking the Samaritans now despising Christ the Son of God, worthy of the like punishment: therefore they pretend Elias' example, not attending the difference of the case, without which, imitation of holy fathers may be very inconvenient and dangerous: So the foolish Samaritans alleged the example of the fathers worshiping in their mountain, John 4. 20. So in other cases do many more, as ill-advised now: but as a reverend Author says, they who without due choice thus do, are rather Apes, than imitators of holy fathers: that which they say, *wilt thou that we command fire, &c.* makes it doubtful whether they desired that Christ should give them that power they speak of, or that they were confident of their power, if they had but Christ his approbation and consent thereto.

**V. 55. ***{of what spirit ye are}* You do not know what spirit moves you herein: your counsel agrees not with your calling: you show a spirit of revenge, and therefore misapply the example of Elias: for I am come to save, not to destroy. And indeed as *Augustine ad Marcellin. ep.* 5. well says, *vindicta ultima juste poscitur, quando nullus jam restat correctionis locus, extrema sc. summoque judicio*, final revenge is justly required, when there remains no place for amendment, that is, at the last judgment. So dangerous is imitation of misapplied examples of Saints, especially to the violation of charity.

**V. 56. ***{is not come to destroy}* See 1 Tim. 1. 15. John 3. 17. He now came to show mercy, and to save the penitent, who will come again to judge and destroy the impenitent, and unbelievers: it was now a time for mercy, not judgment.

**V. 57. ***{and it came to pass}* See Mat. 8. 19.

**V. 58. ***{the Son of man hath not}* Knowing his thoughts, he intimated to him, that he should but deceive himself in following him for temporal gains, who now possessed nothing temporal, and came to propose spiritual and eternal gains.

**V. 59. ***{and he said}* Matth. 8. 21.

**V. 60. ***{Let the dead bury the dead}* Meaning the unregenerate who are dead in trespasses and sins, 1 Tim. 5. 6. Ephes. 2. 1. not that Christ disallows a decent and seasonable performance of that office of duty and piety, but that he prefers the advance of God's glory in preaching the Gospel, and saving of souls before it. See Mat. 8. 22.

**V. 61. ***{bid them farewell}* Mark 6. 46. the word *ἀποτάξασθαι* signifies also to order or dispose of: so that the sense may be, suffer me first to go home, and order or dispose of my estate and family: so that which the Prophet says, Isa. 38. 2. *Hezekiah*, *צו* *לביתך*, *tsav lebeitheca*, command or give order to your house; the 70 give *τάξαι* *περὶ* *τοῦ* *οἴκου* *σου*, dispose of your house: this probably was the main care of the man, to bid farewell, or take leave of friends; though an act of humanity, yet was it of less moment than to order and provide for them.

**V. 62. ***{and looking back}* This an Hebrew manner of speaking, signifying repentance of that one had begun. See Gen. 19. 17, 26. The meaning is, that no man dedicated to the ministry of the Gospel, having in his heart a resolution to forsake his calling, and to embrace the present world, is fit for that holy work: the *simile* is derived from plowmen, who looking backward, cannot make straight furrows. See 2 Tim. 4. 10.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1. ***{After these things}* See Matth. 10. 1.

*{other seventy also}* After that the twelve Apostles had preached in the towns round about, now the hearers multiplying, there was need of more preachers to confirm them: therefore he chose out of those who had heard and followed him, seventy more to send abroad to that office: so the Syriac interprets, *he separated of his disciples seventy other, and sent them*: the Greek word *ἀναδείκνυμι* here used signifies, to design or appoint: as *Budaeus* interprets out of *Plutarch, creare*, or make: as, *ἀναδεῖξαι* *ὕπατον*, to make one Consul, or to design one thereto: so to ordain or consecrate, as Christ here did. Their return from Babylon was a kind of type of their redemption by Christ: so here are appointed seventy, as they had their Synedrion or Council of seventy Elders or Judges after their return. So Moses appointed seventy Elders, or assistants to him, Exod. 18. 22.

*{whither himself would come}* Whither he would follow them, more fully to preach the kingdom of heaven, and also confirm the same with miracles: he sent them two and two, as to strengthen and comfort each other; so for the more certain testimony of that which they were to affirm.

**V. 2. ***{the harvest truly is great}* Matth. 9. 37. meaning a great number of people, which were ready to be converted, and gathered unto God. See John 4. 35. thus he encourages them.

**V. 3. ***{behold I send you, &c.}* Matth. 10. 16.

*{among wolves}* These are *tyranni, & raptores seculi, Bernard. ad Past. Ser.* tyrants, spoilers, bloody persecutors.

**V. 4. ***{carry neither purse}* Be not distracted with care concerning ordinary means to bear your charges, for clothes, or diet: he that sends you is able to provide necessaries for you; such ought our confidence to be in God's providence, that we may ever be assured, that he cares for us, while we are careful of his work; lest otherwise, if the mind be too intent on the things of this world, *minus aliis provideat aeterna, Greg. hom.* 17. *in Evang.* he should be less careful over others for things eternal.

*{salute no man}* See on 2 Kings 4. 29. this he speaks not forbidding comity and civility which he enjoins, verse 5. but distraction from, and hindrance in their duty and ministration: In the uncivil deportment of a late-grown-up monstrous sect among us, appears, how dangerous it is to take up the letter of the Scripture without due consideration of the sense and meaning thereof: It is a most necessary rule, so to interpret Scriptures, as that the interpretation of one place, may not cross the plain sense of another relating to the like purpose: as here; Some will not be civil to those they meet, or to whom they speak; because it is said, *Salute no man*: but they ought to consider, many places commanding to salute one another: whence they learn their rude salutes, of *thou, thou flesh, &c.* Let them consider; we have learned of S. Peter, ep. 1. c. 3. v. 8. *Be courteous*.

**V. 5. ***{into whatsoever house, &c.}* Matth. 10. 11.

*{peace be to this house}* See 1 Sam. 25. 6. It was their manner of salutation, whereby they wished all health, and happiness. See on John 19. 3.

**V. 6. ***{the son of peace}* That is, if any man capable of that blessing, and disposed to receive the doctrine of peace, which you preach, be therein.

**V. 7. ***{the labourer is worthy of his hire}* See 1 Cor. 9. 7. 1 Tim. 5. 18. From the common rule of equity, he shows that they shall by administering spiritual things, deserve of their hearers, things necessary for their sustenance; and therefore might not doubt, but that God would stir them up to relieve them therein, on whom he bestowed the riches of his grace in Christ, by his Gospel preached unto them.

*{go not from house to house}* In respect of the charge you put them to; for while you minister the gospel to them, you bring them greater gain.

**V. 8. ***{eat such things}* That is, be contented with such things as you find: which he says. First that they might not seek themselves, as they whose belly is their God, Phil. 3. Secondly, that they might know, that he who forbade them to carry purse or scrip, yet permits them necessaries in the execution of their ministry.

**V. 9. ***{nigh unto you}* See Mat. 3. 2. and 4. 17.

**V. 10. ***{out into the streets and say}* Publicly and openly for a testimony against them.

**V. 11. ***{dust of your city}* See Mat. 10. 14. Luke 9. 5. Acts 13. 51.

*{kingdom of God}* Christ the Messiah, King of glory, presented himself unto you by his messengers, to reign over you, and save you.

*{is come nigh unto you}* That is, against you, further to condemn you, who contemn it, as chap. 9. 5.

**V. 12. ***{more tolerable in that day}* In the day of judgment, See Mat. 10. 15.

**V. 13. ***{Woe unto thee Chorazin}* Mat. 11. 21. Chorazin was a chief City of Galilee, reckoned among the ten cities of Decapolis, that stood on the sea of Galilee, in a creek, over against Capernaum.

*{Bethsaida}* Another city of Galilee, which was also one of the cities of Decapolis, standing on a creek of the same sea.

*{Tyre}* Tyrus the chief City of Phoenicia, which in the division of the land, came to the tribe of Asher, Josh. 19. 29. but because they could not drive out the inhabitants, Israel never possessed it, but the Tyrians kept it, and continued still in Idolatry.

*{Sidon}* Another City of Phoenicia of the like condition, Josh. 19. 28.

*{sitting in sackcloth and ashes}* Which were signs of mourning and repentance. See 1 King. 21. 27. 2 King. 19. 1. Esth. 4. 1. Mat. 11. 21.

**V. 14. ***{more tolerable}* Because, as *Salvian* says, *de gub. Dei*, lib. 4. fine, *Minoris criminis reatus est, legem nescire quam spernere*: It is less sin to be ignorant of God's will, than to contemn it known: he that knows his Master's will, and does it not, shall have most stripes, chap. 12. 47. Jam. 4. 17. John 9. 41.

**V. 15. ***{and thou Capernaum}* This was another City of Decapolis, which was the Metropolis of Galilee, standing on the river Jordan, upon the borders of Zabulon and Nephtalim, that then flourished with great trade, riches, and pride.

*{which art exalted to heaven}* In regard of Christ preaching and working there: or in regard of their high ambition and prosperity.

*{down to hell}* *ἕως* *ᾅδου*: so in the Creed, *εἰς* is put elliptically for *εἰς* *οἶκον* *τοῦ* *ᾅδου*, to the house, or place of hell. See in cap. 12. 5.

**V. 16. ***{he that heareth you}* See Matth. 10. 40. John 13. 20. 1 Thess. 4. 8.

**V. 17. ***{through thy Name}* We using your authority, and calling on your Name.

**V. 18. ***{I beheld Satan}* Satan is styled Prince of the air, Ephes. 2. 2. ruling in high places, Ephes. 6. 12. whence he is said to be cast down, when his power is overthrown by preaching of the Gospel.

**V. 19. ***{I give unto you power}* See Matth. 16. 18. Acts 28. 5. Mark 16. 18.

**V. 20. ***{because your names are written in heaven}* Because by the effects it appears, that you are elected to life everlasting. See Phil. 4. 3. Rev. 20. 12. and 21. 27. This is a manner of speaking borrowed from the customs of men, whose names are registered, and enrolled in some public records, to keep in certain memory, and assure them of their freedom and privilege in that corporation.

**V. 21. ***{Jesus rejoiced in spirit}* Mat. 11. 25. being comforted in the success of the Gospel, and in the consideration of the performance of his Father's will.

*{unto babes}* *νηπίοις*, the word also signifies simple or weak men. *Hesiod.* *ἔργ*. *καὶ* *ἡμέρ*. *νήπιοι*, *οὐδ᾽* *ἴσασιν*. etc. fools or simple men, neither do they know, etc. See on Mat. 11. 25.

**V. 22. ***{all things are delivered}* Matth. 28. 18. John 3. 35. and 17. 2.

**V. 23. ***{blessed are the eyes}* See Matth. 13. 16.

**V. 24. ***{Many prophets}* Which he means not only concerning the bodily presence, and sight of things by him done; though that was much desired, that they might see the world's Savior present (as we shall do in his kingdom of glory) but also to understand, and to be more fully assured of the admirable work of God's mercy, and the accomplishment of their redemption by Christ.

**V. 25. ***{a certain lawyer}* One that professed the knowledge and interpretation of the law of Moses, though indeed he knew not the end of the law, which was to be a Schoolmaster to bring us to Christ, Gal. 3. 24.

*{Master, what, &c.}* Thus he calls Christ, who yet came not with a mind to learn, but to tempt, and to find some occasion of cavil: it seems that when he had heard Christ teaching, he rose up to propose this question, to try whether Christ would say anything against the law, or mention any other way to life everlasting.

**V. 26. ***{what is written}* Knowing his subdolous mind, he remits him to the law, who boasted of, and trusted in righteousness by the law.

**V. 27. ***{Thou shalt love, &c.}* See Deut. 6. 5.

*{with all thy heart}* As much as you can: *modus diligendi Deum est sine modo diligere. Bernard.* The measure of our loving God, is to love him beyond measure.

*{and thy neighbour as thyself}* Levit. 19. 18.

**V. 28. ***{This do and thou shalt live}* This answer is shaped according to the Lawyer's question, verse 25. *what shall I do to inherit eternal life?* because he sought eternal life by the works of the law, which he could not fulfill; he refers his conscience to the law, which then only showed his sin, Rom. 7. 7. & 3. 20. Gal. 3. 21, 22. knowing that it must either bring him unto Christ, or leave him inexcusable, Mat. 19. 17. otherwise when the keeper of the prison (far from all opinion of any merit of his own) asked; *What must I do to be saved?* Acts 16. 30, 31. *Paul and Silas answered, believe in the Lord Jesus Christ, and thou shalt be saved.*

**V. 29. ***{Who is then my neighbour?}* For they counted no man their neighbor but their friends and countrymen.

**V. 30. ***{leaving him half dead}* We may not so wrest these words in their application, as thence to conceive, that man in his fall, had left in him any life of grace, or ability to recover or raise himself to any moral good; seeing that the Scriptures so expressly declare, that before our regeneration and new birth by the free grace and mercy of God quickening us by his Holy Spirit, we are all *dead in trespasses and sins*, Ephes. 2. 1. See Rom. 7. 18, 24. 2 Cor. 3. 5.

**V. 31. ***{on the other side}* As if the distressed man had been unworthy of his care or pity. See Psal. 38. 11. Isai. 58. 7.

**V. 33. ***{but a certain Samaritan}* Whose nation was odious to the Jews. John 4. 9.

**V. 35. ***{took out two pence}* These circumstances are set to express the free mercy of God in our conversion; not only preventing us by his grace, but continually supplying us with necessary increase thereof, that we may persevere and be saved: according to which *Augustine de verb. Apost. serm.* 15. says, *non potes te promerendo accepisse, qui non promereres, nisi accepisses*; think not that you received by deserving, who had not merited, except you had first received: and again, you give (says he) altogether freely, you freely save, who find (in us) nothing to save us; you find much to condemn us.

**V. 36. ***{which now of these, &c.?}* Which of these did according to the law, which says, *thou shalt love thy neighbor*.

**V. 37. ***{Go, and do thou likewise}* Help him that has need of you, although he be your enemy, or you know him not.

**V. 38. ***{a certain village}* Bethania, John 11. 1.

**V. 39. ***{sate at Jesus' feet}* Here is not only historified her posture, but principally her attention, diligence, and composure of herself (all things else set aside) to hear Christ. See Acts 22. 3.

**V. 41. ***{thou art careful and troubled, &c.}* You are too much perplexed with these cares which hinder your hearing the Gospel, which is more necessary.

**V. 42. ***{Mary hath chosen that good part}* Martha's entertainment of Christ, was good and commendable: but her too much care distracting and hindering her from better, as also her censuring her sister's hearing Christ, was not good, *non tu malam, sed illa meliorem, &c. August. de verb. Dom. ser.* 27. You have not chosen a bad part, but Mary has chosen a better.

*{which shall never be taken away}* That which we here do for eternal life, *etiam post mortem servatur, Greg. hom.* 27. *in Evang.* is kept after death. Such is the kingdom of Heaven, *Unde nullus ejicitur beatus: quo nullus miser admittitur. Prosper. de vit. contempl.* l. 1. c. 2. whence no blessed one is cast out: into which none unhappy is admitted.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1. ***{One of his disciples}* All his Apostles were Disciples: but all his Disciples were not Apostles. Whether this were a Disciple but not one of the twelve Apostles, it is not certain: probably he was present when Christ taught this prayer to his Apostles, and the multitude, Mat. 6.

*{Lord, teach us to pray}* We know not what to pray as we ought, Rom. 8. 26. *Deus solus docere potuit, ut se vellet orari, Tertul. l. de orat.* c. 9. Only God could teach how he would be petitioned. Therefore himself ordained and ordered the duty of prayer; and from his own Spirit, even then when it proceeded out of his mouth, *animata suo privilegio*, being animated and enlivened with his privilege it ascended into Heaven, commending to the Father those requests which his Son has taught. Prayer is a principal part of God's worship: and there can be none other so good a teacher of prayer as Christ our Mediator and Redeemer, God and Man: who as he died to give us life, so does he sit at God's right hand, to present our petitions in Heaven, which his own Spirit indites for us here on Earth, that we may obtain the same. He therefore taught us to pray, and to avoid the vain repetitions and ostentation of Pharisees and Heathens, thinking to be heard for their much speaking. Mat. 6. 7. taught us this short form of prayer, in it comprehending all things necessary to be prayed for.

*{as John also taught}* By giving them some form of prayer: it seems, that because Religion was miserably corrupted by the false Glosses, and superstitious traditions of Pharisees, as also by a general depravation of manners, so that it was no marvel, if very few among them, either rightly practiced, or truly understood, that main part of God's worship, which is in devout and faithful prayer. Therefore whereas now the long promised redemption of the faithful drew near; it was necessary that their minds should be strengthened, and lifted up to the firm hope and confident expectation thereof, by earnest prayer: so that John Baptist, whose office it was to prepare the way of Christ, did, out of most evident places of Scripture thereto pertinent, compose his Disciples (whom he was to instruct and fit to receive Christ the Messiah) some private prayers suitable to their present condition, and the spiritual kingdom of Christ, which they were now to profess.

**V. 2. ***{when ye pray say}* Compare Matth. 6. 9. with this place, and the circumstances show, that Christ did twice at the least teach them this very form of prayer. And hence it appears, that as this is the most absolute and complete pattern of prayer, it comprehending all that we must ask, so is it the most exact and sacred form of prayer, indited and taught the Disciples (who were to teach the whole World the rules and practice of true Religion) by Christ himself, who is best able to teach his servants to pray.

*{Say}* Christ prescribed his Disciples this form of prayer to be used by them; not rejecting others, which his Spirit taught or teaches, but to abridge all necessary petitions, into this one Sum. See *Augustin. ep.* 121. c. 12.

*{Our Father}* Under one person of the holy Trinity named, we understand all three: according to that of *Tertullian de orat.* cap. 2. *In Patre Filius invocatur*, In the name of Father, the Son also (and the Holy Ghost) is invocated. This excellently agrees with the state of grace: first intimating our adoption in Christ; by whom we are to expect all that is profitable for us; as remission of sins, preservation, temporal and spiritual goods, all which God gives as a loving and gracious Father: also in this (*our*) we learn that the faith only which works by love, is available, Gal. 5. 6. So that as children of one merciful Father we must be unanimous, and mutually solicitous for each other's good: and lastly, it shows us with what confidence we must pray: for this appellation *& pietatis, & potestatis est, Tertul. qu. s.* is both a name of piety and power.

*{which art in Heaven}* Who possesses, rules, and disposes of all things in Heaven and Earth. Dan. 4. 34, 35. Filling Heaven which is your Throne, and Earth, which is your Footstool with your power and glory: comprehending all things, comprehended of none: It is to be confessed, says *Augustine, Dardan. ep.* 57. that God is everywhere, *per divinitatis praesentiam*, by or in respect of the presence of his Deity, but not so, *per habitationis gratiam*, in respect of his grace dwelling in, and manifesting himself to the holy. Such only can be the Father of all the Elect, in all ages and places: this shows us to whom our prayers must be addressed: that our God we call on is the true God: how we ought to lift up our minds in prayer, how we ought to live, if we call him Father, 1 Pet. 1. 17. Phil. 3. 20. and with what humility, sanctity, and reverence we must appear before him when we pray. See Eccles. 5. 2.

*{Hallowed be thy Name}* By Name, understand his Nature, Majesty, eternal Power, Goodness, Wisdom, Righteousness, Mercy, Truth, etc. By Hallowed, or Sanctified we pray that we, and all may, rightly know him, adore, reverence, praise, magnify, and set forth that his goodness which shines in all his works, that we may rightly worship and fear him, as *Tertullian* says, *de orat.* c. 3. *id petimus ut sanctificetur in nobis*, that it may be sanctified in us: that we faithfully endeavor, as much as in us lies, that all others may do the same: that his Name which is ever holy *etiam apud homines sanctum habeatur, Augustin. Prob. ep.* 121. may also be accounted holy of men. That we may think and speak of him with all reverence, and never take his holy Name in vain. That we may live to his glory, that his sacred Name be not blasphemed among the enemies, through our impiety. See Titus 2. 10. The sum of all is, that God would manifest himself to us, and give and bless the means by which all may come to the knowledge of him in Christ, that his Name may be magnified in our salvations.

*{Thy kingdom come}* The kingdom of God imports first, his absolute sovereignty and universal government, under which, are all good and bad, Psalm 103. 19. This is not here prayed for, as *Augustin qu. s.* says, *Quod seu velimus seu nolimus utique veniet*, which shall come whether we will or no, it being always everywhere and none can resist it, Dan. 4. 32. Secondly, his special kingdom, whereby he rules in the elect, working his will in them. This is 1. The kingdom of Grace whereby he governs the Elect by his word (the Scepter of his kingdom) and his Holy Spirit of sanctification, whereby he causes in them a voluntary subjection of the whole man body and soul, to his revealed will, Psal. 110. 3. Rom. 4. 17, 18. Psal. 2. 13. with which understand the revelation of Christ and all the means of salvation. 2. The kingdom of glory; that is, the eternally blessed estate of the Elect in Heaven, as *Augustine* says, *Ut nobis veniat, atque nos in eo regnare mereamur*, that it may come to us, and that we may reign therein. Neither may we conceive these to be two kingdoms of God: but one and the same, begun in the state of Grace, and perfected in the state of Glory: and it comes by certain degrees, as first, illumination by the gospel, and all other divine Ordinances, whereby God's grace and favor in Christ is revealed, and his kingdom advanced and maintained. Secondly, subjection of the whole man, body, soul, will, and affections to Christ. Thirdly, true renunciation of the World, Sin, Satan, and ourselves, in shaking off the kingdom and rule of sin, and not following the guidance of carnal wisdom and affection. Fourthly, affections set above, desires and conversations fixed on Heaven, Coloss. 3. Phil. 3. Fifthly, accomplishment of all God's promises to the Elect for the life to come; as God being all in all: perfection of every part and faculty of body and soul filled with glory and unspeakable joy and blessedness in the presence of God: so that the meaning of this petition is, as if we said, O Lord govern us by your word and Holy Spirit, that we may wholly and willingly obey you in all things: increase and preserve your Church, destroy the kingdom of Satan. Sin, and whatsoever power exalts itself against your Divine Majesty, Truth, and holy Ordinances: protect and preserve us in your kingdom of grace, and in your good time bring us into your kingdom of glory.

*{thy will be done, as in heaven, so on earth}* We are not here to pray that God would do what he will by his absolute will, whereby he irresistibly rules in Heaven and Earth; but forasmuch as we are yet partly flesh and partly spirit, Gal. 5. 17. And as *Tertul. de orat.* c. 4. says, *Earth and Heaven*; we pray that we may be enabled to do what God will for his glory and our salvation: we pray (says *August. ep.* 121.) for that obedience; that so his will may be done of us, as it is done by his Angels in Heaven. This will of his is first that we believe in Christ for our justification and salvation, see John 6. 40. Secondly, that we endeavor to the works of sanctification, 1 Thess. 4. 3. and true conformity to our holy Savior Christ. Thirdly, that we submit to a patient and cheerful undergoing all that which the good providence of God shall lay upon us. See Acts 14. 2. Acts 21. 13. 2 Sam. 15. 25. Mat. 26. 39. which we must do by renouncing our own wills (which are corrupt and unjust, ignorant, and commonly hurtful to ourselves) and submitting and rendering up ourselves wholly to the will and good pleasure of God: to this end, Christ proposes the pattern, *as in heaven*. The meaning is, that the Lord would frame us here on Earth to such a holy, ready, and cheerful obedience, as now his holy Angels show in the execution of his will, Psal. 103. 20. and we shall also do it in the kingdom of glory, in which we shall no more sin, Rev. 22. 3. The sum is, Lord give us grace to do your will (for that we may do his will, *opus est Dei voluntate, id est, ope ejus & protectione, quia nemo suis viribus fortis est, sed Dei indulgentia. &c. Cyprian. de orat. Dom.* (*si sit opus ejus*) We have need of God's will; that is, his assistance and protection; because no man has of himself ability thereto. Let your will revealed in the word for your glory and our salvation, be accomplished in our conversion, and mortification of our corrupt affections, (See Ezek. 33. 11.) denial of ourselves, relying wholly on our Savior Christ; sanctification (1 Thess. 4. 3.) and cheerful submission to your good pleasure in all estates prosperous and adverse.

**V. 3. ***{give us day by day our daily bread}* First, he teaches to pray for (as also to seek, Mat. 6.) the kingdom of God, and then things temporal, which may accommodate and serve us thereto. We must say, give, *Ne putetur esse ex nobis, Augustine ep.* 143. and, *us*, for he would not have our prayers such, *ut—pro se tantum precetur, &c. Cyprian qu. s.* the God of peace and teacher of concord, so instructed his for unity, that he would have everyone pray for all, *quomodo in uno omnes ipse portavit*, as he being one, bore, and took care for us all. *Day by day*, *τὸ* *καθ᾽* *ἡμέραν*, day by day, or for the day: every day as much as is sufficient. So the Syriac, *כל־יום* *culjum*, is rendered *quo opus habemus quotidie*. By *Bread*, understand all things necessary for the sustenance of this mortal life, meat, drink, clothes, house, utensils, peace, health, liberty, maintenance, and whatsoever God knows needful to our natures, callings, and conditions, and those whom he has given us. So the Wiseman prays, Prov. 30. 8. *Feed me with* *לחם* *חקי* *lechem chukki*, *bread of my allowance*, or *convenient for me*; as is expressed Genesis 47. 12. *לפי* *הטף* *lephi hattaph*, to the mouth of the little one: 70. *κατὰ* *σῶμα*, according to *their families*; we give it, according to everyone's eating. Christ does not hereby prohibit lawful care and labor in our callings to provide things necessary for ourselves and families, (see 1 Tim. 5. 8. Ephes. 4. 28. 2 Thess. 3. 10.) but 1. That care which distracts from God's service and dependence on his fatherly providence, who can bless the least, without whose blessing, the greatest store cannot profit us. 2. All fraud, oppression, injustice, and cruelty in getting. 2. Diffidence in God's providence for necessaries. 4. Covetousness and desire of more than enough, (see 1 Tim. 6. 9, 10.) 5. Discontent and repining at that measure which God's providence gives us, (see 1 Tim. 6. 8. Hebr. 13. 5.) 6. Intemperance in the excessive use of his creatures, making men unfit for the service of God, and works of their calling. 7. That ungrateful looking on second causes, and means of getting, which makes carnal men forget to praise God's providence, as if their own care and labors supplied them. The sum of this petition is; Lord God whose providence descends to the preservation and well-being of all your creatures, bestow on us and ours, all things necessary for our present life, that we may daily and thankfully acknowledge your fatherly providence, and faithfully and cheerfully depend thereon.

**V. 4. ***{and forgive us our sins}* In this petition Christ teaches us what we must ask, and what we must do if we will attain (*Ut qui a Deo pascitur, in Deo vivat, Cypr. qu. s.* that he who is fed by God, may live in God) we must ask remission of all our sins, guilt, and punishment, without which we must perish eternally: for that which S. Luke here calls *ἁμαρτίας*, Mat. 6. 12. is called *ὀφειλήματα*, *debts*: to intimate what punishment we owe to God's justice, except he remit and forgive it in mercy, (see Psalm 130. 3, 4. Psalm 143. 2.) in asking pardon, we acknowledge and confess our guiltiness, and disability to satisfy; as likewise our hope of mercy: thus he taught his Saints to pray; therefore as *Augustine ep.* 29. says, *quantum libet profecerimus*, how much soever we have profited, still we have need to say forgive us; for if we say we have no sin, *we deceive ourselves, &c.* 1 John 1. 8. The African Council *contr. haeres. Palag.* cap. 67. condemned them who said, that the Saints thus prayed in regard of others' sins, not their own, or rather in humility than truly: *Quis enim ferat orantem, & non hominibus, sed ipsi Domino mentientem, qui labiis dicit sibi dimitti velle, & corde dicit, quae sibi dimittantur, se debita non habere*; for who can endure one praying, and lying; not to men, but God himself: saying with his lips that he desires to be forgiven, but in his heart, that he has no sin to be forgiven? That which we must do, is to forgive our brethren who hurt us: which duty is not here annexed as a cause, but as a condition of our sins' remission in Christ. It is impossible to obtain remission of sins, without true faith, apprehending the merits of Christ for our justification: and as impossible for that to be without charity (Mat. 6. 14. & 18. 33. etc.) therefore Christ herein gave us a law, *Certa nos conditione & sponsione constringens*, binding us in a certain condition, bargain, or covenant, so to forgive as we desire forgiveness; *Cyprian. qu. s.* Here we see the necessity of that which Christ teaches, Mat. 5. 23. The sum is, O Lord forgive us all our sins, guilt and punishment (for your Son Christ his sake) as you have in your mercy given us a heart to forgive all others, who have done any injury to us.

*{Lead us not into temptation}* Mat. 6. 13. He teaches us to supplicate not only *de remittendis*, for remission, but also *de avertendis peccatis*, for aversion and prevention of sins, *Tertul. de orat.* cap. 8. *Lead us not*; that is as *Tertul.* and *Cyprian. qu. s.* interpret, *Ne patiaris nos induci, &c.* Suffer us not to be led into temptation: lest being destitute of his help, either deceived, we consent to; or afflicted, we yield to temptation, says *Augustine Epist.* 121. cap. 11. As if we said, Lord leave us not to the malicious assaults of Satan, nor to the frailty and corruptions of flesh and blood: We are of ourselves unable to resist the power of sin; and have by our many willful transgressions of your holy Commandments deserved to be given over and left to our own sinful wills and affections, and thereby to destruction of body and soul: but Lord forsake us not in any temptation, but give a happy issue; so upholding us with your merciful hand (who best know our weakness and necessities) that we may neither faint in our trials, nor yield to the power of sin or allurements of Satan; but may so resolutely stand, that, through your good Spirit strengthening us, we may at last obtain a perfect victory, through Jesus Christ our Lord.

*{but deliver us from evil}* *Serva nos a malo*, keep us from evil, the Interpreter of the Syriac gives it. Herein we pray against all those things which the Adversary practices against us in this world, who has (says *Cyprian qu. s.*) power granted him against us, either *ad poenam cum delinquimus, vel ad gloriam cum probamur*, to our punishment when we sin, or to our glory when we are tried. And we are here admonished, *Nondum nos esse in eo bono, ubi nullum patiemur malum, Augustine epist.* 121. cap. 11. that we are not yet in that good estate, where we shall suffer no evil: This prayer (says he cap. 12.) comprehended all that an afflicted Christian can pray for: So that it is free to pray *aliis atque aliis verbis*, in other words, but not for other things than those which Christ has taught us to pray for.

**V. 5. ***{Which of you shall have a friend}* The scope of this speech is to teach us fervency and constancy in prayer; by an argument from the less to conclude the greater: if men think no time unseasonable to help a friend in want, how much less will God who is ever ready, willing, and able to help his? and unto whom nothing good can be importune or unseasonable; there can be no lassitude in him whose word is his work, neither does he slumber or sleep, so that any untimely calling on him might break his rest, therefore *David* said, Psal. 34. 1. *I will bless the Lord at all times*, and Psalm 119. 62. *at midnight I will rise to give thanks unto thee*—and he is everywhere to be found of them that seek him faithfully; therefore the Apostle says, *I will therefore that men pray everywhere, lifting up holy hands*, 1 Tim. 2. 8.

**V. 6. ***{in his journey is come, &c.}* *ἐξ* *ὁδοῦ*, *out of his way*. Syriac *מן* *אורחא* *men urecho*, which word coming from *ארח* *arach, iter feci*, is put to signify, a journey, or way.

**V. 7. ***{trouble me not}* Which of you will be so uncivil as to put off an importunate friend, saying, *trouble me not*? but rather (as verse 8.) he will rise and give him, because of his importunity: how much more will the keeper of *Israel* (who neither slumbers nor sleeps: who is ever ready without any trouble to relieve, having in himself that eternal and unspeakable love to his elect, which can have no cause but itself, which needs none other solicitor, or motive to entreat pity towards us) readily hear and relieve us?

**V. 8. ***{because he is his friend, yet because, &c.}* Though he would not take so much pains for any love to this his friend, yet will he do it to avoid more trouble and disturbance, because he loves himself, and his own ease, quiet and rest, rather (says *Augustine ep.* 121. cap. 8.) *vitando taedium, quam benevolentiam cogitando*: avoiding trouble, than thinking of good will: that we may hence understand, if he who sleeping is awaked (awaked against his will) to give, how much more benignly, and willingly will he give us, who knows not sleep, and awakes us from sleep, that we may ask of him? This, as other similitudes, is not to be drawn beyond the intent of Christ, whose purpose is to show the excellent effects of fervency and perseverance in prayer. (See Luke 18. 1. Mat. 15. 27, 28. Isaiah 62. 7. Exod. 32. 10, 11. Exod. 17. 11. etc.) if importunity prevail so much with man, who loves it not, what shall it do with God, who loves it, commands it, and puts the issues and condition of obtaining upon it?

**V. 9. ***{And I say unto you, &c.}* See Mat. 7. 7, & 21. 22. Mark 11. 24. John 15. 7. Jam. 1. 5. 1 John 3. 22.

*{ask and it shall be given you}* Understand this promise to be made to those who ask according to his will: See Mat. 18. 19. Mat. 21. 22. Mark 11. 24. John 15. 7. John 16. 23. Jam. 1. 6. Jam. 4. 2. 1 John 3. 22. 1 John 5. 14. he will not frustrate our hope of obtaining; whose own Spirit both moves us to pray, and indites our prayers: so that when he commands us to pray, he commands, not because he wants our prayers, but because we want his blessings; So true is that of *Augustine de verb. Dom. serm.* 29. *illud quod hortatur, propter nos hortatur*, that which he exhorts, he exhorts for our sakes.

**V. 11. ***{if a son shall ask}* Here is another argument or motive to prayer, from the less to the greater from the worse to the better; if evil fathers will give their children nothing but good; how much more shall the good God (from whom comes every good gift, who can neither be deceived in his choice, nor fail in his will) give his children that which is best? it is impossible for him to deceive his children by giving them any evil, when they pray for good.

*{will he give him, &c.}* As if he said, I appeal to your own minds and affections: do but examine your hearts herein: would any of you natural parents, willingly hurt your own children?

**V. 12. ***{will he offer him a scorpion?}* Would any man abuse the simplicity, or betray the confidence of his infant asking an egg, by giving him a deadly poison? a scorpion is a venomous serpent which brings forth young, *ovorum specie*, like eggs. *Plin nat. hist.* lib. 11. cap. 25. that which the Hebrew calls *עקרב* *hacrab*. Deut. 8. 15. *עקרבין* Chald. 70. *σκορπίου*. So Ezek. 2. 6. *vid Tertul. advers. Gnostic.* cap. 1. *init.*

**V. 13. ***{the holy Spirit}* That is, the graces of the Holy Spirit, the most excellent gift, in which he instances to assure them that he who will give himself to them, will not let them perish for want of things earthly and temporal; He is therefore (as *Fulgentius ad Monim.* lib. 1. well says) to be praised for his grace and favor of either gift; both when he awakes us from sleep to seek him, and also when he gives himself to be found of us seeking him: who indeed, *nullatenus invenitur, nisi ipse nos excitet; ut quaeratur*, is by no means found, except himself excite us to seek him.

**V. 14. ***{casting out a devil}* See Matthew 9. 32. and 12. 22.

*{and it was dumb}* That is, it made the possessed dumb, or speechless, by some resolution of the nerves, or other obstruction or disaffecting the vocal instruments, so that the possessed could not speak.

*{the dumb spake}* He who made the mouth, and the dumb, Exod. 4. 11. can dispossess Satan, and restore his creature, he only can, *verbum proferre de clausis, vocem elicere de mutis. Ambros. in Luc.* lib. 5. cap. 6.

**V. 15. ***{he casteth out}* See Mat. 9. 34. & 12. 24.

*{through Beelzebub}* *ἐν* *Βεελζεβοὺλ*. Syriac *בבלזבוב*, by, or through *Beelzebub*. The Latins give it sometimes *Beelzebul, Tertul. advers. Marc.* lib. 4. cap. 26. and commonly *Beelzebub*, it is all one, they meant (as our Evangelist here interprets) the chief, or most wicked of the devils. So the Jews called that unclean spirit, in detestation of the Idol of that name; 2 Kings 1. 3. *בעל* *זבוב* *Bahal Zebub*: possibly not without a blasphemous allusion to the name of that spirit of the magicians, which was called *Eaalzebub, muscarum Dominus*, the god or lord of flies: because, as some report, they drove away flies by swarms. See *Pausan. Arcadic. Plin.* lib. 19. c. cited also by *Bodin. Daemonom.* l. 1. cap. 3. See *Shindl. Penteglot. in* *בעל* *Hanov.* 1612. pag. 222. B. C.

**V. 16. ***{a sign from heaven}* Mat. 12. 38. and 16. 1. It is likely, that seeing such miracles, and through infidelity doubting, whether all might not be some compact or collusion, they desired some such miracle, as they fancied would put them out of doubt; which was indeed to tempt God, by desire, to prescribe him: or it may be they desired to persuade the people, that except he showed them some such miracle, he had no authority from Heaven: that is, such as was in the Manna, thunder and showers in *Samuel's* time, etc. 1 Sam. 7. 10.

**V. 17. ***{knowing their thoughts}* As the searcher of hearts, knowing to what end they required some other signs from heaven, being not satisfied with all those signs which they had seen him do by a divine and heavenly power; knowing it was their infidelity, and impious mind to calumniate him.

*{every kingdom}* Division ruins kingdoms; unity establishes them: you see by woeful experience, the kingdom of Satan powerful among you, not only, not the wicked castaways, but in that which is more apparent, so many which are now possessed of evil spirits in Israel: therefore Satan's kingdom is not against itself. Devils conspire against all that which is good; but not against one another, or anything which is evil: they are cast out against their wills, chap. 9. 39.

**V. 18. ***{If Satan be divided}* See Mat. 12. 26.

**V. 19. ***{If I by Beelzebub, &c.}* Mat. 12. 27.

*{by whom do your sons, &c.}* That which you allow in your children, why reprehend you in me? We may understand this either of their Exorcists, or of some of these to whom God had formerly given this gift of healing.

**V. 20. ***{with the finger of God}* *ἐν* *δακτύλῳ* *Θεοῦ*, meaning by the power of God, Exod. 8. 19. it is called *אצבע* *אלהים* *ecsbah elohim*, 70. *δάκτυλος* *Θεοῦ*: which Matth. 12. 28. is called *the spirit of God*.

*{the kingdom of God is come upon you}* Meaning not only the preaching of the gospel of the kingdom; but also the power of Christ destroying the works of the devil, casting him out of his usurped possessions, and translating his elect from the powers of darkness into his kingdom of grace, Coloss. 1. 13. and marvelous light: this was come to them who would not believe; which was a sign and argument of their greater condemnation.

**V. 21. ***{When a strong man}* Matth. 12. 29; *ἰσχυρὸς*, strong or powerful. Heb. *בן* *חיל* *ben chaill, filius virtutis*, 1 Sam. 14. 52. 2 Kings 2. 16. etc. a son of power or strength; so used there to call the strong or mighty.

*{keepeth his palace}* He compares Satan in the heart of the unregenerate, to some usurping prince, or powerful tyrant, or *Goliath*-like giant, domineering in confidence of his own strength; and himself to be conqueror (who bore his type) till Christ come to conquer and cast him out; Satan rules, as it were a king, in his palace, commanding all parts and faculties of man, in peace, that is, quietly, none troubling or opposing his dominion.

**V. 22. ***{come upon him}* At unawares subduing him.

*{all his armour}* *πανοπλία*, signifies all ammunition or furniture pertaining to complete arming, Ephes. 6. 13.

*{divideth his spoils}* As conquerors divide, or distribute the pillage to their soldiers: see Isa. 11. 14. Isa. 49. 24. his meaning is to show how he came to redeem mankind, which had no power to deliver itself from the captivity and tyranny wherein Satan held the greatest part of the world, until Christ came; and rescued all those impropriated vessels of honor, restoring God his elect, and to them the graces and state of holiness whereof Satan had despoiled them.

**V. 23. ***{He that is not with me}* Matt. 12. 30. he that declares not himself to be on my side; for there is no man, but he is either Christ's or Satan's: Christ can have no communion with Satan, (as they blasphemously imagined in his casting out devils) for Satan's work is only to destroy and scatter: but Christ, his work is to save and gather together the lost sheep of the house of Israel.

**V. 24. ***{when the unclean spirit}* Mat. 12. 43.

*{is gone out of a man}* Not that Satan ever leaves any, except the spirit of regeneration drive him out: but that in some vacations, and want of opportunities to precipitate them to sins, he seems to be gone for a time: the purpose is, to show the wretched condition of those who abused light and grace offered them; or who being illuminated with the knowledge of God, seeming to have escaped from the snares and power of Satan, do yet relapse to pride, envy, malice, lust, drunkenness, etc. for their last estate is far worse than the first: sin never stands at a stay, but grows worse and worse; 2 Peter 2. 20. Heb. 6. 4. and the judgment shall be proportionable.

*{seeking rest}* Meaning some other heart, where he might rule without any resistance: Satan's rest is in the worst of motions to hurt men, and to make them as damnable as himself.

**V. 25. ***{swept and garnished}* Mat. 12. 44. by a little formal repentance, or fruitless resolution to do some good.

**V. 26. ***{seven other, &c.}* That is, many worse, and so that man's state becomes more desperate, as v. 24.

**V. 27. ***{a certain woman of the company}* Admiring the power and wisdom of Christ; while the envious Scribes and Pharisees studied, and vented their blasphemy, she thus confidently professes his excellency, this seems a proverbial manner of speaking, importing a good and excellent person; and the reason thereof may seem to be, because it is a blessing of God to be the mother of prudent, holy, and excellent children; as the contrary is a great affliction and unhappiness.

**V. 28. ***{that hear the word, &c.}* Without which no carnal privilege can make happy; but we must keep it; for not the hearers, but the doers of God's word are justified. See Rom. 2. 13. Jam. 1. 23. Mat. 7. 26. it is the food (of the soul) which cannot nourish except the stomach retain it to a good digestion.

**V. 29. ***{and when the people}* Mat. 12. 38.

*{were gathered}* When the multitude increased, and were gathered to him.

**V. 30. ***{For as Jonah was a sign}* See Jon. 2. 1. as *Jonas'* deliverance out of the belly of the whale was a sufficient proof of his ministry, so Christ's resurrection the third day, was of his mission: and therefore as the *Ninevites* believed Jonas, after he had been three days in the whale's belly, so should these believe and repent after his resurrection the third day; Acts 2. 37. 41. 47. or else be left excuseless.

**V. 31. ***{The Queen of the south}* See 1 Kings 10. 1. the Queen of Saba, or Sheba: see Mat. 12. 42.

*{a greater than Solomon is here}* That is, Christ the Messiah, God and man: the eternal word and wisdom of God: it is true that God gave *Solomon* wisdom and understanding *exceeding much, and largeness of heart, even as the sand that is on the sea shore*: so that his wisdom excelled the wisdom of all the children of the East, and all the wisdom of Egypt: *he was wiser than all men*, 1 Kings 4. 29, 30, 31. but in Christ all fullness dwelt, Colos. 1. 19. the fullness of the Godhead, Colos. 2. 9. in him are hid *all the treasure of wisdom and knowledge*, Colos. 2. 3. so that (as *Bernard* says *sup. Cant. serm.* 27.) he, *Non tam sapiens, quam ipsa sapientia est*: is not only wise, but wisdom itself: and so CHRIST excels all creatures: no creature is its own essence.

**V. 32. ***{The men of Nineve shall rise up, &c.}* See Jon. 3. 5. their example shall be an argument of condemnation against those, who regarded not the preaching of a greater than *Solomon*, than all men, as *John Baptist* says, John 3. 31.

**V. 33. ***{no man when he hath lighted, &c.}* See Matth. 5. 15. Mark 4. 21. Luke 8. 16. his purpose is to show, that though his preaching found little good effect in that evil generation, yet would he not hide the light which he brought not in vain into the world: this similitude of light accommodate to the office of the ministry, is often used in holy Scripture, to teach us what we ought to endeavor to.

*{under a bushell}* See Mat. 5. 15.

**V. 34. ***{the light of the body is the eye}* See Matth. 6. 22. as a window is called a light, because it lets light into the room: these words seem to give a reason why that generation continued blind, in so clear a light of the gospel preached to them: because the eye of their mind was darkened with ambition, pride, envy, malice, covetousness, and other wicked affections; hence it came to pass, that they, not only understood not the truth which Christ showed them, but opposed the same, accounting it darkness and error.

*{single}* Clear without blemish, quick-sighted; if the crystal humor, and *pupilla oculi*, were colored, the eye could not see any other color, for *intus existens prohibet alienum*, that color of the eyeball would not suffer any other to be perceived; therefore has the God of wisdom (wonderful in all his works) made that (like a clear glass-window to let in light to the house) without any color in it, that through it the species of things seen, might be presented through the *optic nerves* to the common sense, and so we have a right apprehension of things sensible: and so proportionably is it with things intellectual.

*{whole body}* Meaning that the whole body is directed by a sufficient light.

**V. 35. ***{that the light}* That which seems to be light, take heed it be no darkness of error.

**V. 36. ***{no part dark}* He means this of true illumination by God's word and Holy Spirit, not as if any man (though regenerate) were free from all ignorance, but because in regeneration every part is sanctified and renewed (though not in this life perfected) so that we thereby walk as children of light.

**V. 37. ***{a certain Pharisee besought him, &c.}* Some of these invited him sometimes, though possibly with several intentions; he who came to save sinners, omitted no opportunity thereto. See Chap. 7. 36.

**V. 38. ***{Pharisee saw it}* See Mark 7. 3. etc.

*{first washed}* They washed often according to their superstitious tradition, and he wondered why Christ did not so also, Mark 7. 2, 3, etc.

**V. 39. ***{and the Lord said}* See Matth. 23. 25. See the admirable difference between Christ, and the men of this world. Christ, as a true friend, not so much endeavoring to please as to profit, plainly told the Pharisee his, and his fellows', faults; but the parasitical trencher-flies will rather betray their benefactors' soul by their silence, than venture their own interest in their favor by reproving them, and telling them the truth, that they might amend their faults.

**V. 40. ***{make that which is within also}* Both body and soul, that God may be served in both according to his revealed will, not your own tradition: you wash the hands, and neglect the heart; it is deep hypocrisy to be solicitous for that which man sees, and to neglect that which God sees, and principally requires. See Mat. 23. 26. 2 Cor. 7. 1. James 4. 8.

**V. 41. ***{But rather give alms of such things as you have}* *Τὰ* *ἐνόντα*, that is, *κατὰ* *τὰ* *ὄντα*, according to the things which you have: *τὰ* *ὑπάρχοντα*, as Chap. 12. 33. The Syriac interpreter gives it, *id quod adest*: as it is Genes. 32. 13. *מן* *הבא* *ביד* *min habba beiad*, of that which came to hand, which he had ready: the meaning is, as you are able and provided to give: it is not washing the body can make you clean or holy before God, but faith and a good conscience within: specially charity in giving according to your ability, wherein God will accept you, as clean.

**V. 42. ***{Wo unto you Pharisees}* See Mat. 23. 23.

*{these ought ye to have done}* As if he had said; you Pharisees do well to pay tithes of all the increase of the earth, for so the Law commands, Levit. 27. 30. and in *Abraham's* tithes paid to *Melchisedec*, it appears to be due by the law of nature, (the ground of the moral Law, and long before the Levitical Law commenced, in force) the transcript whereof is the Law written by God on the tables of stone. Next we must consider, that God (the best and surest interpreter of his own Law) interprets detention of tithes to be sacrilege, as it is written, Mal. 3. 8. *ye have robbed me—in tithes and offerings*: notwithstanding, men otherwise interpreted, who said, *Wherein have we robbed thee?* and so a breach of the eighth precept of the moral law, therefore standing still in force: for Christ came not to dissolve the Law, Matth. 5. 17. this speech then of Christ, at the period of the Levitical Law, affirming that they ought to pay tithes of all, even to the very herbs; ought not to be restrained as relating only to the priesthood of *Aaron*, it does as duly now concern the Ministers of the Gospel, as any other part or branch of the moral Law: it being sufficient for that, once to have said to all persons, times and places, *Thou shalt not steal*; the Prophet so expressly interpreting detention of tithes to be stealing and robbing; by so much more abominable, because God has vouchsafed to take tithes upon his own account in the Ministers' behalf, lest it should be thought a human interest, and in the power of man to alienate them, under any pretense or color whatsoever. It can be no less than impious ingratitude to detain the tenth from God, who gives us freely nine parts for our own use. *Quid faceres si novem partibus sibi sumptis, tibi decimam reliquisset?* says *Augustin. de Temp. ser.* 219. what would you do, if he should have taken nine parts to himself and left you only one? All is his: he requires but his own: he may dispose of it as he pleases.
