Neither does he require tithes for his own sake, but for our good: as for a maintenance which his wisdom laid out for the Ministers, which watch over our souls; so that he might bless the other nine parts to us as it is written, Proverbs 3. 9, 10. *Honour the Lord with thy substance, and with the first fruits of all thy encrease: so shall thy barnes be filled with plenty, and thy presses shall burst out with new wine.* And again, Mal. 3. 10. *Bring ye all the tithes into the store-house, and prove me now herewith, saith the Lord of hosts, if I will not open unto you the windows of heaven, and poure* *you out a blessing, that there shall not be room enough to receive it*, etc. the curse lies upon the detainers of tithes, not only in particular, but in the general: *Ye are cursed with a curse; for ye have robbed me, even this whole nation*, Mal. 3. 9. many fearful instances to this purpose do both our histories, and memories afford us, too large for our present purpose. Two things are considerable by those, who are of other opinion, and the third not contemptible: first, whereas they say, the Ministers shall have otherwise a competency; let them consider: how dangerously the wisdom of man assails the amendment or bettering of God's wisdom and decrees. Secondly, whereas they suppose tithes were due in the ceremonial Law only, not by the moral, let them consider. First, what could tithes prefigure, accomplished, (and so repealed) in Christ. And next, that (whereas all the ceremonies of the Law are repealed in particular, or in the general, so in the Gospel or new Testament, as that their abolishing necessarily concludes the abolition of all the particulars thereto pertaining) no one Text of the new Testament evidently, or by necessary consequence prohibiting tithes, can be produced: whereas this present text says, *these ought ye to have done, and not to leave the other undone*: and the forecited interprets the detention of tithes sacrilege, or robbing of God: denouncing the curse to the detainers, pronouncing the blessing to the payers thereof: now whatsoever was commanded in the Old Testament, having its ground in the law of nature, and being not repealed in the new; must yet stand in force, as a duty of the moral law. Thirdly, it is considerable, that every branch of the moral law, is indispensable: so that if Ministers and people should consent to any violation thereof: there can reasonably be expected none other sentence from the just God, than that forecited, Mal. 3. 9. *ye are cursed with a curse; for you have robbed me, even this whole nation*: Yet they said, *wherein have we robbed thee?* confident that detention of tithes was no sin. To conclude, that which Christ taxes here, is their not being as well careful for judgment, mercy, faith, (Matth. 23. 23.) and the love of God, as they seemed to be in paying small tithes.

**V. 43. ***{Ye love the uppermost seats}* See Matth. 23. 6. Mark 12. 38. Luke 20. 46.

**V. 44. ***{Ye are as graves}* See Matth. 23. 27. your filthy corruptions within you, appear not unto men, so that deceived by your shows of sanctity, they easily fall into a dangerous imitation of you.

**V. 45. ***{Then answered one of the lawyers}* One of the expounders of the law.

*{thou reproachest us also}* Thus he said, as taking it for granted, that the interpreters of the law were unreprovable; he censures Christ's just condemning their hypocrisy: so hard is reformation, where pride, hypocrisy, and opinion of learning and wisdom, prejudicate all instruction: this was the reason why so few Scribes, Pharisees, and Doctors of the Law were converted by the preaching and miracles of Christ, evidently enough speaking him the expected *Messiah*; and the same cause is there still, why so few are now converted, but many are perverted daily, the Spirit of pride, and a giddy influence of Satan has so possessed many, that they think themselves able to teach their Ministers, and themselves too good to be reproved: he injures them, who but speaks of their sins.

**V. 46. ***{And he said}* See Mat. 23. 4.

*{ye lade men}* See Acts 15. 10. though they charged the people with many burdensome traditions, yet here Christ seems not so much to speak of these, as their rigid and imperious exaction of obedience to the whole law of ceremonies (which Peter calls, *a yoke which neither we nor our fathers were able to bear*, Acts 15. 10.) and in the meantime they favored themselves, with ease and indulgence.

**V. 47. ***{wo unto you}* See Matth. 23. 29.

**V. 48. ***{ye allow the deeds}* Your fathers building the Prophets' tombs, whom their fathers slew, therein condemned their fathers' facts, by such an honoring their memory, declaring their disliking of their fathers' cruelty; you doing the like, show yourselves heirs also of your fathers' impiety. *Non igitur aedificatio, sed aemulatio loco criminis aestimatur. Ambros. in Luc.* l. 7. c. 11. not the building of the Prophets' tombs, but imitation of their fathers' wickedness is condemned: as if he had said, you adorn their tombs, but will no more obey their doctrine than your fathers did: you will also kill the messengers of God, under pretense of Religion and Godliness.

**V. 49. ***{Said the wisdom of God}* Matth. 23. 34. as if he said I say, who am the eternal subsisting wisdom of the father, Prov. 8. 12. or, the wisdom and goodness of God, thus disposed to send Prophets, etc.

**V. 50. ***{That the blood of all, &c.}* For in former ages they slew the Prophets of God, and these men of this generation continuing in their fathers' sins, and adding to the measure by crucifying Christ, and persecuting the Apostles and Ministers of the Gospel; God would now no longer bear, but pour down vengeance on the bloody nation, as was seen in the destruction of Jerusalem: and as Christ foretold them, Matth. 23. 31.

**V. 51. ***{From the blood of Abel}* See Genes. 4. 8. the meaning is, that God would call this nation to account for all the blood they shed; though in their blind fury, and ignorant zeal, they thought they had done God service in shedding it (as John 16. 2.) he names Abel, because his blood was the first that ever stained the earth; as if he said, no cruelty and effusion of innocent blood, when ever shed, shall escape unpunished: of *Zacharias* we read, 2 Chron. 24. 22. whose dying words, when they stoned him, were *the Lord look upon it, and require it*: which quickly came to pass: for the Assyrians invaded Joash (who commanded him to be slain) and destroyed Princes and people; and his servants conspired against him, and slew him in his bed, 2 Chron. 24. 25. concerning two named *Zacharias*, see the learned *Is. Casaubon. Exercit.* 1. *ad Annal. Eccles.* 17. An. 1. n. 57.

**V. 52. ***{wo unto you lawyers}* See Mat. 23. 13.

*{taken away the key, &c.}* By your artifices, traditions, and usurpation over men's consciences, subjecting them to your erroneous opinions, and keeping them from the means of knowing the true sense of holy scriptures, which is preaching the word; it being the key of heaven: as also in that you hinder them from believing in me, who am the end of the law, the way, truth, and life.

**V. 53. ***{to urge him to speak, &c.}* The word *ἀποστοματίζειν* here used signifies, *ex ore elicere*: to draw something out of one's mouth: which the Syriac significantly expresses by, *ומתכסין* *vamthacsin & aucupabantur*; as if it were said, they went *a birding*: or laid wait to catch him and ensnare him in his words. *תכס* is a Syriac word, which, as *Tremelius* notes, *inter alia significat aucupari, aut carpere verba alicujus*. Rabbi *Salomo* expounds *טפשף*, *visco avibus insidiari*: *Vatablus* interprets it, *captiose eum interrogare de multis*, they began captiously to question with him concerning many things. So they urged him to answer, by proposing several doubts and cavils, in hope that in many answers they should catch something whereof to accuse him: so it is expressed, verse 54. by *ζητοῦντες* *θηρεῦσαί* *τι*, seeking as it were by hunting to catch something out of his words, whereof to accuse him.

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1. ***{In the mean time}* See Matthew 16. 6. Mark 8. 15.

*{leaven of the Pharisees}* Leaven is Metaphorically put in Scripture; sometimes in the good part, as Matth. 13. 33. sometimes in the bad, as Mark 8. 15. 1 Cor. 5. 6, 7, 8. Gal. 5. 9. it relates sometimes to doctrine, sometimes to practice: in either of these, men use to receive a certain influence of qualities, from others whom they hear, or read, or with whom they converse, whether good or evil as the leaven conveys, and communicates its sourness to the meal in which it lies; so they interpret this word *ζύμη*, *farina subacta, quae rancorem contraxit*: Hebr. *חמץ* *chamets* and Syriac *חמע* *acris, acerbus fuit*, as 1 Cor. 5. 6. *מחמע* *machamah*, here the Syriac has *חמירא* *chamiro*, from the Hebrew word *חמר* *Chamar*, mixed or confused. So that Christ here warns them, that they be not infected with that same bitterness of hypocritical Pharisees, the bane of religion: therefore is that *πρῶτον* used, not distributively to prescribe an order of avoiding evil; but as a mark of eminency set upon that sin; as if he said chiefly (as Rom. 1. 8.) or above all evils, take heed of hypocrisy, in which there can be no truth of Religion.

**V. 2. ***{Nothing covered}* Matthew 10. 26. Mark 4. 12. Luke 8. 17.

**V. 3. ***{The house tops}* Publicly and openly: he respects the manner of their buildings, which were flat roof, whence they might speak to multitudes in the streets. See 2 Sam. 11. 3.

**V. 4. ***{I say unto you my friends}* He speaks thus to comfort them, and to encourage them to preach the Gospel boldly, see Mat. 10. 28. John 15. 14, 15. A man will save and relieve a friend, specially in that which he has employed him, and it were great dishonor, to desert him therein: therefore fear you not to preach the Gospel; stronger is he that is with you therein, than all that are, or can be against you.

**V. 5. ***{Into hell}* To hell fire, and eternal condemnation; of this no creature has power, therefore God only is to be feared: Christ calls it *γέενναν*, which some derive as a contract of *גי*-*הנם* *ge-hinnoi*, Josh. 15. 8. *the valley of Hinnom*: hence the Syriac *גהנא* *gihanno* or *gihannah*. Greek *γέεννα*: it was a pleasant and fruitful valley near Jerusalem upon the brook *Cedron*, wherein was *Topheth*, where they offered their sons and daughters to devils by the idol *Moloch* (as the heathens did to *Saturn* (*Minuc. Fel. Octavio.*) burning them between two fires, with hideous noises: for the abolishing whereof *Josias* commanded, that all manner of carrions from the city should be cast there; so that there was continual fire to burn and consume the same, lest they should stench the city, See 2 Kings 23. 10. hence by a metaphor the Hebrews used to call hell (the place for torments of the damned) *גיהינם* *gehinnum*, as a place most abominable, impious, and terrible. See *Joh. Drus. ad voc. N. T.* tom. 1. *Gehenna*.

**V. 6. ***{Are not five sparrows}* Matth. 10. 29. This he adds to confirm and comfort his disciples against persecutions. The poor birds cannot be destroyed, except God permit it; how much less shall you? it is injury to the divine providence, to distrust his care over us, who keeps the creatures provided for us: he forgets not one of these; can he forget you? certainly you being of more value, are more dear to him.

**V. 7. ***{The very hairs of your head}* See on Matthew 10. 30.

**V. 8. ***{Also I say unto you}* Matthew 10. 32. 2 Timothy 2. 12.

**V. 9. ***{Whosoever shall deny me}* To the foregoing argument he adjoins the mention of reward: Christ will own them, who suffer for his sake; and that before the holy Angels, who shall attend him in his judging the living and the dead. Mark 8. 38. as also the punishment of them, who (for fear, favor of men, or of this world, or the things thereof) shall any ways deny him before men: as if any deny him to be very God and man: to be our Savior, justifier, redeemer, and sole mediator: or if any deny the Gospel, or any part thereof to be the truth: if any out of malice oppose it, for fear of men be ashamed of it, or by an impious silence betray the truth thereof: To confess him relates, either to all Christians, of which 1 Pet. 3. 15. or to those who are called out before persecutors, to give testimony to the truth of Christ: he will deny and cast off all those who deny him: which is to be understood in case of final impenitency: otherwise Peter's denial of Christ, (through deep perturbation of mind in sudden fear) and his bitter tears of repentance following, show how far the elect, in some distempers are sometimes overborne in temptations; and yet by God's mercy are recovered again.

**V. 10. ***{Speak a word}* See Matth. 12. 32. Mark 3. 28. 1 John 5. 16.

*{it shall be forgiven him}* He that speaks against Christ, through ignorance and unbelief, it shall be forgiven him when he repents: but he that shall blaspheme the Holy Spirit of God, the Godhead, out of obstinate malice, against his knowledge and conscience, wittingly and willingly, persevering therein unto his end, it shall not be forgiven him: for otherwise we know Paul's blasphemy, was forgiven him, for he did it ignorantly. 1 Timothy 1. 13.

**V. 11. ***{When they shall bring you unto the Synagogues}* See Matth. 10. 19. Mark 13. 11. Luke 21. 14. the meaning is, when they shall convent you before the councils of Jews, High-Priests, Pharisees, etc. or the tribunals of heathen Princes, Magistrates, or Governors, fear not, you shall find a present assistance of God's Spirit, in such words and wisdom as your enemies shall not be able to overthrow, chap. 21. 15.

**V. 14. ***{Who made me a judge}* Christ would not intermeddle with secular government, when he was to redeem man, by subjecting himself to the judgment of man: not hereby disallowing the right of Magistracy and government by civil judicature, but intimating that God had now given him another calling: teaching everyone to contain himself within the limits of his own calling, and not to usurp and intermeddle with others; admonishing all men, to beware of covetousness the fountain of contention: and lastly, laying the ground for that which the Apostle urges, 2 Timothy 2. 4. that the state of a Minister should herein be proportionable to the condition of a soldier; disentangled from the affairs of this life, that he may please him, who has chosen him to another office: not that the Minister may not meddle with any affairs secular, but that he may not thereby be entangled, and hindered from the work of his Ministry, to which God has chosen him, and set him apart, as Romans 1. 1. nor be impropriated to the execution of civil offices of judicature or the like, because it can be no less than sacrilege to convert that, in whole or any part, to common uses (which is sacred by a peculiar dedication to God and his Religious service) incompatible with that consecration and ministration; therefore Christ refused this office of civil judicature; because he had no calling thereto, but to be *a Priest for ever after the order of Melchisedec* (Hebr. 5. 4, 5, 6.) though he were in his own person of infinite abilities to have performed both these offices: the fourth Council of *Carthage* chap. 20. decreed, that the Bishop should not so much as be troubled with the care *rei familiaris*, of domestic business, but, *lectioni, & orationi & verbi praedicationi tantummodo vacet*, attend only to reading, prayer, and preaching the word: the third Council of *Carthage*, chap. 15. citing those words. 2 Tim. 2. 2, 4. decreed that neither Bishops, Priests, etc. should be farmers, stewards, etc. it is not unlikely that they had also in their mind that of the Apostle; 1 Tim. 4. 15. that which some allege out of *Possidonius, vit. Augustin.* cap. 19. that he spent whole days in compounding controversies; and that which they bring out of *Augustin de opere Monach.* c. 29. To. 3. conclude nothing against us herein, seeing it appears not that *Augustin* executed any civil office of judicature; but that which was Ecclesiastical and immediately subordinate to Religion, as are peace, mercy, and charity; which the first Council of Antioch allowed and enjoined chap. 20. it was ordained that twice a year the Bishops should meet; *διὰ* *τὰς* *ἐκκλησιαστικὰς* *χρείας*, *καὶ* *τὰς* *τῶν* *ἀμφισβητουμένων* *διαλύσεις*, for Ecclesiastical causes, and to compose controversies, etc. and chap. 25. of the same: it was decreed, that the Bishop should have *τῶν* *τῆς* *ἐκκλησίας* *πραγμάτων* *ἐξουσίαν*, etc. power of things belonging to the Church; to give alms; and to relieve his own wants: now although these controversies, did not all arise from matters of faith and religion, but probably some, or many from secular interests (which might be the reason why *Augustin de op. Monach.* cap. 29. names those secular businesses, in composing whereof he was so much distracted) yet was the making of peace immediately subordinate to the duties and acts of Religion unto which none are, or can be well and duly fitted, without peace, unity, and charity. (Matth. 5. 23, 24. 1 Cor. 1. 10. 1 Cor. 3. 3. Phil. 2. 1. etc. Ps. 133. 3. etc.) and so, though this office well became all the children of God, Mat. 5. 9. yet questionless, best those who were, or are assigned to the ministry of the Gospel of peace: yet is it considerable, that *Augustin* esteemed this, such a distraction from his Ministry in the word, and such a burden unto him, that he calls God to witness, *multo mallem per singulos dies, &c. aliquid manibus operari*, etc. I had much rather every day some hours labor with my hands, that I might have others free to read, pray, and do something concerning divine Scriptures, and that he not only complained hereof, but obtained leave in an Ecclesiastical assembly of the people to transmit and put over this burden to *Eradius* his successor elect. See *Aug. ep.* 110. If to spend time to make peace, were a known distraction and heavy burden to good Bishops; we may securely conclude with *Ambros. ep.* 33. *ad Marcellin. tyrannis sacerdotis infirmitas est—nunquam sacerdotes tyranni fuerunt, sed tyrannos saepe sunt passi*, that is, the tyranny of a priest is an infirmity—priests (if good) were never tyrants, but have often suffered by tyrants.

**V. 15. ***{Beware of covetousness}* Beware of insatiable desire of having more: which the Apostle calls the root of *all evil*. 1 Tim. 6. 10. so it is indeed, of fraud, lying, oppression, murder, etc. *non ille liber qui servit avaritiae, Amb. of.* l. 2. ep. 7. he is no freeman who serves avarice: all abundance cannot preserve life; that is, it is in God's hand, to give or take away; as he presently shows in the parable of the secure rich man: and truly, *non census divitem sed animus facit: Ambros. lib.* 10. ep. 82. it is not so much wealth, as the mind which makes a man rich: the law of nature is rich enough to all: unto which you may quickly find abundance; but to desire, all abundance is scarcity: *nemo pauper nascitur sed fit*: no man is born, but becomes poor: therefore poverty is not by nature, but opinion: therefore you may quickly find nature’s rich man; hardly desire’s.

**V. 16. ***{Brought forth plentifully}* He teaches that God gives temporal blessings, even to the wicked, (as Matth. 5. 45.) many times for their greater destruction. Jer. 12. 3. Psal. 73. 18. therefore we may not set our hearts on them; but aspire to those riches which he gives to his elect only, as the riches of his grace, Ephes. 1. 7. Eph. 2. 7. *the unsearchable riches of Christ*. Ephes. 3. 8. See Col. 2. 2. 1 Tim. 6. 5, 6.

**V. 18. ***{Build greater}* This he says, not to condemn a holy providence of saving and laying up that which God gives us (for he commanded the broken meats to be gathered up, John 6. 12. See also 2 Cor. 12. 14. 1 Timothy 5. 8.) but to show us how evil confidence in riches is, how vain and foolish; seeing they may be so suddenly taken away from their securest owners, or they from them.

**V. 20. ***{Fool, this night}* He was foolish in reckoning upon many years to come, who could not be certain of one night: *longa nostra desideria increpat via brevis. Greg. hom. in Evang.* 32. so short a way of life, reproves our long desires.

*{Then whose shall these things be}* No man (says *Ambros. lib.* 8 ep. 10.) is rich, *qui quod habet secum hinc auferre non potest*: which cannot carry hence that which he has: that which we must leave behind us, is not ours, but some others.

**V. 21. ***{layeth up treasure}* He that heaps up riches to satisfy his worldly desires without the good provision of faith, and good works, shall be like this foolish rich man: see Mat. 6. 20. 1 Tim. 6. 19. Jam. 1. 5.

**V. 22. ***{Take no thought}* See Mat. 9. 25. 1 Pet. 5. 7. Psal. 55. 22. he forbids not *πρόνοιαν*, care for yourselves and your families, that we owe. 1 Tim. 5. 8. but this *μέριμναν* distracting and diffident care proceeding from a greedy desire and love of the world, distrust in God's providence, and undervaluing of the true riches of Godliness, 1 Tim. 6. 5, 6.

**V. 23. ***{The life is more}* Matth. 6. 25. as if he said: God has given the life and soul, which is far better than food and raiment; therefore he will not fail to give these for the preservation of the other, the Creator never ceases from the work of providence, John 5. 17.

**V. 24. ***{Consider the ravens}* Matth. 6. 26. God that provides for the most abject and vile creatures, as ravens, etc. Job 38. 41. Psalm 147. 9. will certainly much more feed, and provide for man, made to his own image, whom he redeemed with no less a price than the precious blood of Christ Jesus. See Rom. 8. 32.

**V. 25. ***{Which of you by taking thought}* Mat. 6. 27. this is another argument to dissuade from worldly care, from the vanity and unprofitableness thereof: as if he said, all your care cannot make you rich; God only gives or restrains. See 1 Kings 3. 13. 1 Chron. 29. 12. Hag. 1. 6, 9, 10, 11. Job 8. 18. it is in vain to rise up early, to sit up late, to eat the bread of carefulness, or sorrows: except the Lord bless it, you cannot thrive. Psal. 127. 2.

**V. 26. ***{For the rest}* For that which is of greater importance, the preservation of yourselves.

**V. 27. ***{Consider the lilies}* See on Mat. 6. 28, 29. this argument is, like the former, from God's liberality showed on the creatures made for man's delight, to assure us that he is more liberal to man himself: he that thus clothes the soon-fading flowers, will not let man want clothing: the scope of all is to teach us in a holy and prudent endeavor, to cast our care upon God. See Psal. 55. 23. 1 Pet. 5. 7.

**V. 28. ***{If then God so cloth the grasse}* Mat. 6. 30.

**V. 29. ***{Neither be ye of doubtful mind}* *μὴ* *μετεωρίζεσθε*, it signifies, be you not in suspense or of minds doubtful and wavering: so *Suidas* expresses it: *ἦσαν* *δὲ* *μετέωροι* *ταῖς* *ψυχαῖς*, *καὶ* *πρὸς* *τὸ* *μέλλον* *ἔτι* *σαλεύοντες* *erant autem animis suspensis, & ob incertum rerum futurarum eventum adhuc fluctuantes*. So *Thucydides*, by him also cited, says, that Greece was *μετέωρος*, that is, says he, *ἐσαλεύητο*, *καὶ* *οὐχ* *ἡσύχαζον*; *they were troubled, and did not rest*, So he calls ships at sea *μετεώρους*, that is, *πελαγίους*, *καὶ* *μὴ* *ἐπ᾽* *ἀγκυρῶν* *ἱσταμένας*: that which we may not improperly render *ships adrift*, or *at hull*, which are not come to an anchor: the interpreter of the Syriac gives this word, *neque distrahatur cogitatio vestra in istis*: the whole sense is, be you not of wavering and doubtful minds, about temporal necessaries, as they must needs be who, depending only on second causes, do not put their confidence in God's providence, which never fails those who trust in him. See Psal. 9. 10. Hebr. 13. 5.

**V. 30. ***{the nations, &c.}* This last argument is taken from the difference which ought to be between us Christians, and the profane and unbelieving heathens: we acknowledge and profess that God is our Father; we call on him who knows we have need of these things; and as a tender Father, he provides these things for us: but the Gentiles and worldly minded men, have not faith to depend on God: no wonder if they, who have no such confidence on God's favor toward them, depend on second causes, and endeavor to provide for themselves: a worldly mind discovers a man's diffidence in God, and gross unbelief, as to his relation to God's covenant, and interest therein.

**V. 31. ***{these things}* These temporal necessaries, common to good and bad.

*{shall be added}* See on Mat. 6. 33. Shall be added over and above that you seek for; as *Solomon's* riches were to his request for wisdom. 1 Kings 3. 9, 10, 11, 12, 13.

**V. 32. ***{the kingdom}* Of heaven, therefore fear not: he that gives an eternal interest in heaven; will not suffer you to want temporal and earthly necessaries, to fit you, and bring you thereto.

**V. 33. ***{sell that ye have}* Matth. 19. 21. Acts 2. 45. Acts 4. 34.

*{provide you bags}* See chap. 16. 9. Mat. 6. 20. 1 Tim. 6. 19. This command condemns not propriety of goods; neither reaches it to all times, places and persons; but respects cases of necessity: and teaches that charity much more safely lays up riches in God's hands (who will bountifully repay for the poor) than any care of worldly men can otherwise do: and lastly this shows which are true riches: *illa sunt verae divitiae, quas cum habuerimus perdere non possumus; August. de verb. Domini serm.* 35. those are true riches which we can never lose, when once we have them.

**V. 34. ***{where your treasure is}* Matth. 6. 21. Because your minds and affections will be commonly on that which is most loved, dear, and precious unto you: therefore be merciful, and so lay up treasure in heaven, that your hearts and conversations may be there: See Col. 3. 2. Phil. 3. 20.

**V. 35. ***{let your loins &c.}* See 1 Pet. 1. 13. Meaning that they should be always ready to execute their charge; alluding to their habit, and manner of girding their long loose garments unto them when they were to do any work; he wills them to contrive all worldly necessaries so, that they may not hinder them in the service of God.

**V. 36. ***{When he will return from the wedding}* The meaning couched under this metaphor is, that we should stand upon a spiritual watch, constantly expecting the coming of our Lord Jesus to judge; *ἀναλύσει* *return*, properly signifies to dissolve or break up: so *Athen* lib. 1. *ἀπὸ* *τῶν* *δείπνων* *ἀναλυόντες* which may be interpreted breaking up company, to go home: some take it translatively from ships breaking ground, to return whence they came.

**V. 37. ***{and serve them}* That is, God will abundantly requite their service in Christ, with eternal blessings.

**V. 38. ***{in the second watch}* They were wont to divide the night into four watches; so often they relieved and changed the turns of their watchmen or sentinels, from the courts of guard. See Matthew 14. 25. Exod. 14. 24. *Theophylact* understands here *τὰς* *διαφόρους* *ὥρας* *τῆς* *ζωῆς* *ἡμῶν*, the divers hours or times of our life. The meaning is, whether he come sooner or later, to take you out of this life; this know, *quos diu ut convertantur tolerat, non conversos, durius damnat. Greg. hom.* 12. *in Evang.* those whom he long tolerates, that they may be converted, he more heavily damns if they convert not.

*{or in the third}* The meaning is, because the hour of death or judgment is uncertain; therefore we must always be provided for it. See Job 14. 14. See verse 40.

**V. 39. ***{and this know}* See Mat. 24. 43. 1 Thess. 5. 2. 2 Pet. 3. 10. Rev. 3. 3, & 16. 15.

**V. 41. ***{this parable unto us}* Whether does this parable appertain to us Apostles, Ministers and watchmen over the house of Israel; or unto all men?

**V. 42. ***{Who then is that faithful, &c.}* Matthew 24. 45. He answers, not *Peter's quaere* directly, but so as that we may understand here, as Mark 13. 37. *What I say unto you, I say unto all, watch*: though it principally concern those to whom God has committed the charge of souls, yet all that will be saved, are also to watch.

**V. 43. ***Blessed are &c.* He exhorts to the duty by the reward proposed.

**V. 44. ***{ruler over all}* Mat. 24. 45. that is, he will highly honor and recompense his diligence; as in things secular *Joseph* was honored by *Pharaoh*, and set over his household Gen. 41. 43. what this honor shall be, *eye hath not seen*, etc. 1 Cor. 2. 9.

**V. 45. ***{But if that servant}* By punishment threatened, he dehorts from security, tyranny, oppression, and injury towards another.

**V. 46. ***{Cut him in sunder}* Or *cut him off*: *διχοτομήσει*. So Mat. 24. 51. *Tertul. adv. Marc.* l. 4. c. 29. gives it by *segregabitur*: so the Syriac gives it *ונפלג*, as *Martin Trostius* reads it; as *Tremelius* *ויפלג* *& dividet*: of *פלג* *phelag*. Hebr. *פלג* *Phalag*, signifying both; *divisit* or *separavit*, all to one purpose; for so will the Lord cut off the impious servant by separating him from the number and society of them that shall be saved: as Mat. 25. 46. thus he will deal with deceivers of so great a trust; this penalty is added to terrify the idle, injurious, and those who in the Church serve not the Lord Jesus, but their own bellies, Philippians 2. 21. to these will he once give their portion on fire with hypocrites and unbelievers in hell fire.

**V. 47. ***{which knew his Lords will}* As if he said; if the servant which offends out of ignorance be worthy of punishment; what is he, who knowing it, willfully, or negligently transgresses it? ignorance is less sin than willfulness, 2 Pet. 2. 21. it is vain to pretend ignorance (where all are bound to know, that cannot excuse) and most wretched to abuse knowledge.

**V. 48. ***{worthy of stripes}* That is, who has committed some fault worthy of punishment: he alludes to the custom of correcting servants.

**V. 49. ***{to send fire}* Mat. 10. 34. *Cyril. Hierosol. Catec.* 17. understanding this of the Holy Ghost: others more probably of the fiery trial, afflictions, to which they shall be exposed who will live godly in Christ. See verse 51. etc. he speaks this of that effect which the Gospel should produce, not of itself, but by reason of man's corruption and abuse of it.

*{and what will I?}* That is, though I desire unity, not dissension, yet I would not for wicked men's sakes, that the Elect should want the comfort, which should come by the publishing of the Gospel: hereby he incites them to diligence in preaching the Gospel, notwithstanding the tumults which they should see following the same.

**V. 50. ***{baptism to be baptized with}* Math. 20. 22. meaning that himself must first suffer: this in the Saints, is by some called the baptism of martyrdom: so by waters, the scriptures sometimes express many or great afflictions. Psal. 66. 12. & 69. 1.

*{how am I straitened}* Pained, or much pressed: as if he said, I much desire that it were accomplished, and the redemption of man thereby.

**V. 51. ***{suppose ye &c.}* Mat. 10. 34.

*{but rather division}* See on verse 49. He shows them before, what would follow by the impious perverseness of men, lest finding such unexpected effects, they might be scandalized, and impute those divisions to the preaching of the Gospel, which are only accidental thereto: so must we account all these intestine quarrels in or about religion here specified: See on Matthew 10. 34.

**V. 53. ***{the Father}* Matthew 10. 35. one near and dear friend against another.

**V. 54. ***{When ye see a cloud rising}* Mat. 16. 2. The sea bare westward from Judea, so that commonly clouds thence arising brought rain: so *Elias'* servant saw a little bank or cloud arising from the west, before that great rain which followed the drought of three years. 1 Kings 18. 43.

**V. 56. ***{Ye hypocrites}* See on chap. 13. 15.

*{that ye do not discern this time}* As if he said; you have wisdom to observe natural causes, and signs; why can you not consider spiritual things, of far greater consequence, that is, this accepted time, and day of salvation, wherein God offers you such abundant means thereto.

**V. 55. ***{why of yourselves judge ye not?}* He discovers the fountain of their impiety and security: they would not descend seriously to examine their own consciences, neither would they there inquire, as in the sight of God, what was right: therefore had they much more understanding for anything, than for matters divine and heavenly: if they were sued, or in danger of arrests, they would presently make means to compound with their adversary, lest they should be put to more charge, or fall into greater dangers: but for reconciliation with the holy Majesty of God offended, they cared not; *Prosp. cont. collar.* well observes here, that Christ did not speak this, as intimating that they had either freedom of will, or possibility thereby to that which was good of themselves: but that they might know that they had lost it *suo vitio*, by their own fault of sin, and that therefore God's exaction was not unjust, though they were not able to perform that which they ought; but that man, (*a litera occidente, confugiat ad Spiritum vivificantem, & facultatem, quam ante non invenit in natura, quaerat ex gratia*) might from the killing letter (of the law) fly to the quickening spirit, and seek that ability of grace which he could not before find in nature: which if he did, he should find, that great is the mercy of God: if he did not, that the punishment of his sin is just.

**V. 58. ***{When thou goest with thine adversary}* See on Mat. 5. 25. The meaning is, break off contentions with your adversaries, before you come to dangerous issues: repent quickly of all your sins, for which God has a controversy with you: believe in Jesus Christ, the only Mediator between God and man, and the price of our redemption; that being justified by faith, you may have peace with God, (Rom. 5. 1.) in this life abuse not the patience and longsuffering of a gracious God, lest he judge you according to the rigor of his justice, which you can never satisfy, which is here meant by the last mite. See on Mat. 1. 25.

*{mite}* See on Mark 12. 142. *Theophylact* on this place well shows, that the torments of the damned shall never end, because they can never by suffering, pay the last mite, or satisfy the justice of God.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1. ***{Whose blood Pilate}* We find not in any other scriptures, mention of this fact: it seems he caused them to be slain as they were sacrificing, so that their blood was mingled with the blood of the beasts which they offered: not that they were slain in Galilee, whereof *Pilate* was not Governor: but it may be that these were of the sect of *Judas* the Galilean, mentioned, Acts 5. 37. as *Anastasius sinait. quaest.* 146. affirms they were; Christ here corrects the rigid censures of hypocrites, over-quick and sharp in censuring others in their afflictions, forgetting that the same rod is over themselves for their security in sin: whereas we ought by such examples to examine our own corruptions, and by present repentance to prevent God's judgments: however we may not judge of men's final conditions, by their present calamities, for God many times so afflicts his dear servants, as thereby to bring them home to him in repentance, and newness of life; while he gives fullness and prosperity to others, who by their abusing the same, find the broad and easy way to destruction eternal.

**V. 2. ***{suppose ye that these, &c.}* As if he said, you may not think that God always lays the most heavy secular punishments, on the greatest sinners; he does not presently smite all great sinners: but he makes some exemplary, that thereby others may be moved to amend the like faults. See Numb. 16, 32. Acts 5. 3, 5. etc.

**V. 3. ***{I tell you nay}* Because men are too apt to condemn those as plagued of God, whom he exercises with temporal punishments, without regarding their own faults; he admonishes everyone to look home to his own defects.

**V. 4. ***{the tower in Siloam fell}* Neither do we read this history anywhere else in scripture: it seems to have been one of the towers on the walls of Jerusalem, which stood by the waters of *Siloe*. See John 9. 7. Isaiah 8. 6.

*{think ye they were sinners}* Gr. *ὀφειλέται* *Debtors*, that is *sinners*. See Mat. 23. 16. so the Syriac translates it; and so the word is used, Mat. 6. 12. Luke 11. 4.

**V. 6. ***{ye shall all likewise perish}* The word *ὁμοίως*, which the Syriac gives, *as they*, does not here signify the same kind of judgment; but is to be understood of punishment in general, hanging over the impenitent; as if he said, all impenitency is under the curse temporal and eternal; as may appear in the following parable of the barren fig-tree. See also Mat. 3. 12. Ephes. 5. 5. *ye shall all* (whether Jews, Galileans, or Samaritans) be judged alike, by the impartial God, who respects not persons. 1 Pet. 1. 17. Rom. 2. 5.

**V. 6. ***{this parable}* See Mat. 21. 19.

*{A certain man had a fig-tree}* This parable our Savior spoke as prepossessing a secret objection which a secure sinner might make against that which he said: the scope is to show that many impenitent sinners who are worthy of present destruction, are yet spared for some time; all which shall but add to their final ruin, except they truly and timely repent: continuance in sin is a high abuse of God's longsuffering, whose justice sleeps not; neither is he slack, but patient. See 2 Pet. 3. 9. Romans 2. 5. as the planter will try all means to make the barren tree fruitful: so the good God will not presently cut down and destroy men; but use all means to better them: when nothing will serve, then he lays the ax to the root. See Isaiah 5. 4. etc. Mat. 3. 10.

**V. 7. ***{these three years}* That is, these many years; it signifies the patience and longsuffering of God toward sinners: some understand the time before, and under the law, and Gospel. *Greg. hom.* 31. See *Irenae.* l. 4. c. 7.

*{why cumbreth it the ground?}* *καταργεῖ* signifies, among other things, to consume or work out the heart of the ground: the meaning is, that they who are not fruitful in good works, do but make others barren by their example; and as *Gregory* says, *aliis operandi occasionem negat*, deny others the occasion of working, and possess the place on which others might be planted, which would bring forth better fruit. See Rom. 6. 6. & 7. 8.

**V. 8. ***{He answering said}* This describes the affection of those who faithfully plant in God's vineyard; they do not forthwith despair when they see not present fruits of their labors, but take the more pains with their plants, their auditors, by how nearer they perceive God's judgments are on their barrenness; and pray the Lord to spare them. See 2 Tim. 2. 24, 25. Exod. 32. 32. Amos 7. 2, 5.

**V. 9. ***{then after that thou shalt cut it down}* The Syriac reads *and if not, why should it live? thou shalt cut it down*: Christ's purpose is hereby to show the Jews, and in them all others planted in God's vineyard, the Church, what God looks for, that is, our fruit. Isaiah 5. 2. and what they must look for, who after much pains and culture bestowed upon them by the Ministers, remain yet unbettered and unfruitful; that is, cutting up and destruction of body and soul. See 2 Chron. 36. 15, 16. Jer. 1. 10. Isaiah 5. 5, 6. Mat. 3. 10.

**V. 10. ***{on the Sabbath}* Signifying that this miracle was done publicly, when the multitude were assembled.

**V. 11. ***{a spirit of infirmity}* It was not any natural infirmity, but by the malicious working of the devil; who delights in the misery and infirmity of man, and therefore does not only infect and afflict the mind, but also the body; where and as far as he can obtain leave to hurt, as may appear in the history of *Job*, and many in the Gospel.

**V. 12. ***{thou art loosed}* It has reference to that which is said, verse 16. (*whom Satan hath bound*), and to show that he had power to dissolve the work of the devil, and to rescue his captives, and the key of the bottomless pit, with the chain to bind Satan. Rev. 20. 1, 2.

**V. 13. ***{immediately she was made straight}* Syr. *She stretched out herself*, *ἀνωρθώθη*, she stood up straight again: this shows that it was the immediate power of God, which perfectly cured her, without any ordinary means applied.

*{and glorified God}* This is an effect of faith, to give God the honor of all the blessings which we receive. See Chap. 17. 16, 17. and it shows that this was no obscure work, but an evidence of God's power and mercy.

**V. 14. ***{the ruler of the synagogue}* That is, one of the rulers: for it seems there were divers, Mark 5. 22. Acts 13. 15. the Syriac calls him *רב* *Rab*, which in the Chaldee and Syriac signifies *great, major, doctor, prince*, or *ruler*. See on Acts 18. 8.

*{there are six days}* Concerning the error of Pharisees prohibiting works of mercy on the Sabbath day, see chap. 6. 2. observe here, that there can be nothing free from the censure of malicious ignorance, the most sacred and Divine actions of Christ's mercy does Satan strive to black over with some imputation of evil, lest the spectators should believe and be saved.

**V. 15. ***{Thou hypocrite}* *ὑποκριτὴς* signifies also that which the Latins call, *histrio*, an actor or personator of others, as stage-players, in habit words and actions imitate or counterfeit that which they are not, but for present would by all means seem to be: so do hypocrites, counterfeit, seem, or resemble Saints; when they are indeed and truly no more such, than he that acts an Emperor on the stage, is an Emperor in power, Estate, and honor; the Syriac excellently expresses it, by *נסב* *באפא* *naseb baphe assumens vultum*, or *accipiens personam, faciem*, etc. you who assume the countenance, or take on you the person or face of another. Mat. 7. 5. Luke 6. 42. this dissembling of the ruler (who under pretense of much zeal to the sanctification of the Sabbath, calumniated Christ the Lord of the Sabbath) Christ here reproves: as also their perverse zeal, who did not think the sabbath violated, by works of mercy towards their beasts, but towards men: thus he convinces them by their own practice.

**V. 16. ***{daughter of Abraham}* She was such, either by nature, or imitation, (Rom. 2. 12. Luke 19. 9.) or both: in any of which respects they were extreme perverse, in denying that comfort to a Saint of God, bound 18 years together, with the bonds of Satan; in great affliction, and pain of body, which they would not deny to a beast, by themselves tied up with the bonds of men, for their own good, and for a convenient time only; malice is ever blind, and impiety unreasonable.

*{these eighteen years}* The time of her suffering which Christ well knew, he recites, as to convince their peevish malice; so to comfort those who suffer long: God measures all, and in due time will help. Psal. 9. 10. long this afflicted woman suffered, long the lame man. John 5. 5. 9. much longer Israel in Egypt; yet God delivered at last. Gen. 15. 13. Acts 7. 6. See Habac. 2. 3.

**V. 17. ***{All his adversaries were ashamed}* They were ashamed as convicted of calumny: it does not appear that they were converted: but the more happy effect of this miracle appeared in the rest who rejoiced for the power and mercy of God shining in these works of Christ.

**V. 18. ***{Unto what is the kingdom of God like}* Mark 4. 30. that kingdom of God which is now offered unto you which he governs by the scepter of his word, showing the way thereto.

**V. 19. ***{Like a grain of mustard seed}* This he speaks to declare the admirable increases and power of the Gospel, whose beginnings were so slender, and that the Church has divers states, sometimes it is small and hardly discerned for the paucity of professors and true believers (Revel. 11. 12.) sometimes glorious with multitudes, and by them very conspicuous, stretching out her branches. Psal. 80. 9, 10, 11. to the river, and her boughs to the sea.

**V. 20. ***{Whereunto shall I, &c.}* Mat. 13. 33. See Luke 7. 31.

**V. 21. ***{It is like leaven}* This similitude is to the same purpose. See chap. 12. 1. to show the admirable increase of God's kingdom of grace.

**V. 22. ***{Journeying towards Jerusalem}* Mat. 9. 35. the first part of this verse shows the unwearied industry of Christ in teaching the people the way to salvation, in the second appears his zeal to the ordinances of God: for in that he made journeys to Jerusalem, it seems to have this meaning, that however he preached constantly in the towns and villages, yet so often as their appointed festival approached, he went up to the solemn assemblies to worship at Jerusalem.

**V. 23. ***{Are there few that be saved?}* This seems a captious question, occasioned by Christ his pressing repentance: as if it had been said; what shall become of the Israel of God if so few as you pretend, shall be saved? therefore he answers not directly, but admonishes rather to be very careful for their own souls' health (Mat. 7. 13.) than inquisitive after the counsels of God concerning the number of the elect: and to avoid that common road to hell, following multitudes to sin. See Exod. 23. 2.

**V. 24. ***{Strive to enter in}* See on Mat. 7. 13. Christ according to his usual manner, answered not directly to their question, to satisfy their curiosity, but to that which more concerned them to know and do.

*{many, I say unto you will seek}* That is they will endeavor for salvation, by a blind desire thereof, but not using the right means, nor going the right way (by Christ the only door thereto) shall not attain it. See John 7. 34. & 8. 21. & 13. 33. Rom. 9. 31, 32. many shall strive in vain, as they who seek righteousness and salvation by their own merits and works of the Law, not by faith in Christ: or who continue willfully in known sins: yet must strive in that way of faith which works by love Gal. 5. which God has appointed, in whom our labor is not in vain. 1 Cor. 15. it is true, few shall be saved, in respect of the multitude of unbelievers which shall perish: there is little wheat in respect of the chaff, yet enough to fill God's granary: as many as are written in his book of life: that certain number, whose election is sure and immutable, having this foundation, *the Lord knoweth who are his*: our wisdom therefore shall be, not to trouble ourselves with those things, which are within the veil, and ark of God's secret counsel, but to strive to make our calling and election sure to our own consciences (which is sure in God's decree) by doing those things which may as happy fruits of our election, calling and regeneration, assure us that we shall never fall. 2 Pet. 1. 9, 10.

**V. 25. ***{And hath shut the door}* He means, that God affords us time and opportunity of salvation which if we omit, we shall be shut out like those foolish virgins. Mat. 25. 10. Psal. 32. 6. Isai. 55. 6.

*{I know you not}* That is, I own you not for mine.

**V. 26. ***{We have eaten}* He means here the Jews, who had opportunity of salvation offered them, by Christ's presence, and familiar conversing with them, and yet they neglected it, it shall not profit to have eaten and drunk with Christ on earth: nor to have heard him preach; except we believe, and forsake our sins: Judas, and the Pharisees heard him, yet were sons of perdition.

**V. 27. ***{But he shall say}* See Mat. 7. 23.

*{depart from me}* Mat. 25. 41.

**V. 28. ***{Weeping and gnashing of teeth}* Mat. 8. 12. & 13. 42. & 24. 51. This expresses the miserable and hopeless condition of the damned; who shall indeed rise again to eternal sorrows which the scripture expresses in several terms; everlasting torments in hell fire, unquenchable; the worm that dieth not; utter darkness; and here weeping and gnashing of teeth: that is the most intense sorrows and torments of soul and body, the *poena sensus* and *poena damni*, in that they shall be sensible of the blessedness of the Patriarchs, Prophets, and all the elect, in the presence of God, which they willfully lost. See Mat. 25. 11.

**V. 29. ***{Come from the east}* He seems to speak here principally concerning the Gentiles, alluding to Isai. 43. 5, 6. the sense being, God will call his elect out of all nations, and they shall have eternal life and blessedness with faithful Abraham, which he expresses by the similitude of a feast or banquet, as chap. 14. Mat. 22. Mat. 8. 11. Luke 22. 20.

**V. 30. ***{here are last, which shall be first}* Mat. 19. 30. & 20. 16. Mark 10. 31. in this speech he overthrows the vain confidence of the Jews, who so pretended to be the peculiar people of God that they would disinterest all other nations, whom they despised as castaways, themselves presuming on God's favors, as if he were bound to them, however they degenerated from holy Abraham; he tells them here, that shortly that order should be changed; the Gentiles being preferred before them and they through their invincible obstinacy and unbelief, being rejected, and becoming a scorn and reproach to all nations.

**V. 31. ***{Herod will kill thee}* *Herod Antipas*: This seems not advice proceeding of any good will to Christ, but in hope to rid him away, that he might not teach there. See Amos 7. 12.

**V. 32. ***{Tell that fox}* He calls him so, for his subtlety, and fraudulent disposition (See 2 Tim. 4. 17.) which Christ hereby intimates was well known to him, though Herod lurked like a fox, to find advantage to hurt.

*{I cast out devils}* Intimating that he had no cause to fear Herod's power, who commanded the powers of hell: and himself had taught, that in the service of God, they are not to be feared who can only kill the body: which must teach us constancy in our ministry. See on chap. 12. 4.

*{to day and to morrow}* That is, for a short time by God appointed; and therefore not in the power of man to prevent or abridge.

*{be perfected}* Namely in being offered up, and perfecting the work of man's redemption. See John 19. 28, 30.

**V. 33. ***{Perish out of Jerusalem}* He shows that he must suffer at Jerusalem, where so many of the Prophets had been murdered, that few, or none in respect of them, perished elsewhere; the sense is, as if he said, tell you me that Herod will kill me? nay but more bloody enemies are you and your high Priests.

**V. 34. ***{O Jerusalem, Jerusalem}* Mat. 23. 37.

*{how often would I have, &c.}* In this similitude he declares, as the tender love of God to them (John 11. 25.) sending them many Prophets to instruct them (Jer. 7. 2. Chron. 36. 15.) so also their obstinacy in contemning his counsel against themselves: here is no freedom of will to good, but only self-destruction by sin.

**V. 35. ***{behold your house is left unto you desolate}* See Matt. 23. 38. that is, the temple in which you glory. Jer. 7. 12. this city Jerusalem, and this whole people shall be ruined, here is used the present for the future, both for the certainty of that he says, and also because it was then hard at hand.

*{Ye shall not see me}* That is, after my death and resurrection, you shall not see me again until I come in glory. See chap. 19. 38. Psal. 118. 26. Mat. 23. 39.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1. ***{One of the chief Pharisees}* Either one of their *Sanhedrin*, or chief council in matters Ecclesiastical; or else one of the rulers of a Synagogue: for Mat. 9. 18. *Jairus* is called *ἄρχων*, a *Ruler*, who Mark 5. 22. is named *ἀρχισυνάγωγος*, a *Ruler of a Synagogue*: also there was a difference between these; Elders, Scribes, and Chief Priests; as between the offices of civil and Ecclesiastical rulers. See c. 24. 20. Acts 4. 5. as *Moses* also distinguishes them. Deut. 19. 17. for all rulers of Synagogues were not Pharisees, John 7. 48. for Pharisee was a name of a Sect, of *פרש* *pharash separavit*, or *exposuit* *פרושים* as it were, separated from others' society in sanctimony of life (at least in their own opinions) as Luke 18. 11. so *Theophylact* says they were separating from others. See *Epiphan. lib.* 1. 16. *Joseph. Antiq.* lib. 17. cap. 3. & lib. 18. cap. 2. so also *Suidas* interprets *φαρισαῖοι*, by *ἀφορισμένοι*, separates those who derive the name from interpreting, must consider, that their interpreters and doctors were called *סופרים* *Sopherim*, scribes, from the word *ספר* *saphar scripsit*, but the Pharisees observed the law and exposition thereof from tradition of their elders. See Ezra 7. 12. 21. 25.

*{to eat bread}* This known Hebrew phrase imports to dine or sup, the like History we have chap. 7. 36. why he would eat with such treacherous inviters, the cause might be, lest he should seem to hate their persons, or ecclesiastical order, and that in his affability, he might find occasion of converting or convincing them, and leaving them without excuse at the day of judgment.

*{they watched him}* They observed his words and behavior, that they might find something to accuse him of.

**V. 2. ***{Which had the dropsy}* Whether he came of his own accord, or was sent in by the Pharisees, for that frequent occasion of quarrel about the Sabbath, it is not certain: the disease is mentioned; he was *ὑδρωπικὸς*, ill of the dropsy, either a *Timpany*, or that which is the *Anasarcha*, or dropsy, universally spread over the whole body, the swelling flesh becoming spongy and senseless; and so much the more uncurable by how much longer time, the crude humor from the corruption of the liver, has prevailed, and settled an evil habit upon every part: whatever, how desperate soever to man; he that made man, can repair any ruin in man, and perfectly cure him; but here Christ provokes the Pharisees to a dispute concerning the Sabbath; first, because he knew their thoughts who watched what he would do, again because on the like occasions, he formerly had sundry contests with them.

**V. 3. ***{Jesus answering}* The Hebrew manner of speaking many times signifies, he began to speak.

*{is it lawful to heal on the Sabbath day?}* Mat. 12. 10. as if he had said, what is your opinion in this case? is it any breach of God's law to cure and heal the sick and disaffected on the Sabbath day? or is such a work of mercy to be accounted a Sabbath day's work? He asks this, not as doubting, for he best knew the intent of the law, who was the author thereof; but that by this prepossession he might correct their error who thought otherwise; that thereby he might show the unjustness of their calumny foreseen, who would be bitterly incensed at his doing such a work in their presence who were of a contrary judgment, and took themselves to be the only knowing interpreters of the law.

**V. 4. ***{they held their peace}* Not able to contradict as verse 6. nor daring to incur the hatred of the people, for whom Christ wrought so much good; or thinking there was no present danger of death in the sick man, and having also been so lately put to shame in the same argument. See chap. 13. 17.

**V. 5. ***{Which of you shall have an ox, &c.}* See chap. 13. 15. which of you is so unreasonably unjust, as that he would not, without making any scruple of conscience, relieve and save a poor beast on the Sabbath day? how much rather is man's life and safety (for whom God made all the creatures on earth) to be regarded and preserved? See Mat. 12. 11, 12.

**V. 7. ***{A parable}* Matthew 13. 3. 9. That is, a dark speech covertly to tax their ambition, and to teach them humility.

*{chief rooms}* Gr. *πρωτοκλισίας*, *κλισία*, as *Suidas* says, is *κλίνη*, or *κραββάτον* a bed on which they lay down: though that same *discubitorius lectus* in *Plinie* l. 33. c. 11. on which they used to sup, differed from the bed on which they slept, as also their *triclinia*, which were three beds or pallets set together to lie down and to eat on, specially at their feasts: the Syriac word here used is of *סמך* *Semach*: as John 13. 23. Mat. 26. 7. 20. Luke 7. 37. & 22. 27. 1 Cor. 8. 10. etc. the same which the Hebrew *סמך* *Samach*: in the pallets, it seems there was (as at our tables) some observation of places for guests respectively to be set, which some ambitiously affected, as thinking themselves more worthy than others present, this Christ reproves as an effect of pride, and occasion of many breaches of charity.

**V. 8. ***{Sit not down in the highest}* This he says to beat down ambition, and to persuade to modesty and humility, as the Apostle admonishes, Phil. 2. 3. that in lowliness of mind they esteem others better than themselves.

**V. 9. ***{He that bad thee, &c.}* See Prov. 25. 6, 7. that is, he that invited you to his house, or otherwhere to eat with him.

**V. 10. ***{Sit down in the lowest}* *ἀνάπεσον*, of *ἀναπίπτω* *decumbo*, according to the custom of those countries. See on chap. 7. 36. he gives not this as an universal rule to all men, but forbids ambition in all. See on chap. 11. 43. Mat. 12. 38.

**V. 11. ***{For whosoever exalteth himself}* Mat. 23. 12. Job 22. 29. Prov. 29. 23. Luke 18. 14. Jam. 4. 6. 1 Pet. 5. 5. whosoever arrogates to himself, or thinks too highly of his own worth.

*{he that humbleth himself}* To humble oneself, is to think lowly of oneself, attributing all the good he has to God: which Samuel expressed by *קטן* *בעיניך* *katon behenecha, little in thine own eyes*, 1 Sam. 15. 17. not overvaluing yourself, he says not, he that is humbled, etc. but he that humbles himself, it may be no fault to be exalted, and so no virtue to be humbled, therefore he speaks not here of that humility which is *ex tristitia* or *necessitate*, as *Bernard sup. Cant. serm.* 34. speaks, for that may be with pride and an unsubdued mind, but that which is, *ex voluntate*, of a will either meekly bearing afflictions, or humbly enjoying prosperity, the sum is, that which *Fulgentius, de praedest. ad Monim.* l. 1. says, *sicut—fit elatione humilis: sic fit humilitate sublimis*, as a man is made low by pride, so is he exalted by humility: for God resists the proud, and gives grace to the humble. 1 Pet. 5. 5. not that God finds any thus humble before he gives them grace, but because *dando gratiam, humiles facit*: by giving grace, he makes them humble.

**V. 12. ***{When thou makest a dinner or a supper}* This forbids not entertainment of parents, brethren or friends, but condemns the error of Pharisees, who thought thereby they satisfied the law of charity, which is indeed in relieving the poor and needy.

**V. 13. ***{Call the poor}* For these are the objects of charity: the good you do to these, Christ takes as done to him. Matthew 25. 35.

**V. 14. ***{For they cannot recompense thee}* Meaning, that in justice, a recompense shall be made, which, because the poor cannot, God certainly will.

*{at the resurrection of the just}* Where the just shall receive reward (the wicked shall rise again, but to eternal punishment) see Mat. 25. 34. Eccles. 11. 1. Prov. 19. 17. the poor man's hand is *gazophylacium Christi*, Christ's treasury, and a bill of exchange for heaven, whereby we may there lay up treasure, where we shall be sure to find and enjoy it, *in tuto haereditas ponitur, quae Deo custode servatur. Cyprian. de op. & misericord.* in safety is that inheritance laid up, which God keeps.

**V. 15. ***{Blessed is he}* Rev. 19. 9. It appears that this was one of the sect of the Pharisees who acknowledged the resurrection of the dead, and the blessedness of the just in the kingdom of God; and it seems that he was affected with some apprehension thereof, in that he took occasion from the present discourse to break out into these words: though whether he understood the state and condition of glorified bodies free from necessities of eating, drinking, etc. it appears not.

*{that shall eat bread in the kingdom of God}* Chap. 13. 29. the Hebrews by this manner of expression meant, to be partaker of the joys of heaven: possibly this speaker dreamed of some imaginary heaven in which men should eat and drink: but God's kingdom consists not in such things.

**V. 16. ***{Then said he unto him}* Mat. 22. 2. upon this speech, Christ takes occasion further to instruct him and others concerning the kingdom of God, and that it should indeed be happy for them who should enjoy the same, but that against the common opinion of their Doctors, who thought themselves and their sectaries the only heirs apparent of the kingdom of heaven, that the generality and chief of them, though thereto first invited, yet for their love and study of things secular, contemning, slighting, and neglecting God's merciful invitations thereto, should be excluded from that happiness, and that the despised party of Jews and Gentiles should enjoy the same. See verse 21. See 1 Cor. 1. 20, 26, 27, 28.

*{a certain man made a great supper}* That is, God rich in mercy, who by his servants invites man, proposing the bread of life, which remains to eternity. John 6.

*{bade many}* Mat. 22. 2. many were and are called to the knowledge of God, of the Jews and Gentiles: in respect whereof it is called a *great supper*, or feast, as also in regard of that which is set before them, which is eternal life and complete happiness in Christ. See Mat. 22. 14. Rom. 10. 18. this is a great feast which lasts not for some days only, as did Solomon's, 2 Chron. 7. 8. Nehemiah's, Nehem. 8. 18. or that of Ahasuerosh, Esther 1. 3, 5. and 2. 18. but to eternity: neither is it for the nation of the Jews only, Rom. 3. 29. or like *Belshazzar's* Dan. 5. 1. to a thousand lords, but to all nations and people, without acceptation of persons. Acts 10. 34, 35.

**V. 17. ***{Sent his servant}* Meaning his Prophets; who though many, yet having one commission, message and Spirit by which they all speak, are here signified by the name of a servant.

*{come, for all things}* Prov. 9. 2, 5. intimating speed, and undelayed apprehension of God's mercy offered in the Ministry of the Gospel, as Hebr. 3. 15.

**V. 18. ***{All with one consent}* With one accord, though they allege several causes, yet they all agree in refusing to come.

**V. 19. ***{Another said I have bought}* In these excuses, it appears, how dangerous a *Remora* and obstacle to the kingdom of Christ, the care of secular things is, and that all things and businesses of this world, however seasonably lawful and necessary, in their due times must yet give place to those, which concern God's honor, and our eternal life and salvation.

**V. 21. ***{Master of the house being angry}* Mat. 22. 7. is mentioned the injury done to the servants sent to invite the guests (meaning the persecutions of the Prophets and Ministers) and the just punishment inflicted; destruction of the foolish and ungrateful people who so requite the Lord; which Luke omits.

*{into the streets and lanes, &c.}* Hereby signifying the calling of the Gentiles, wretched unregarded sinners, intimated by the poor, lame and blind (See Rev. 3. 17.) and a people gathered as it were from the highways and hedges.

*{the poor}* The poor in spirit, Mat. 5. So, *Peccatores superbi respuuntur, ut peccatores humiles eligantur, Greg. hom.* 36. Proud sinners are refused that the humble may be chosen. God chooses those whom the world despises: the infirm and despicable of the World, commonly do more readily hearken to the voice of God, by how much less they have in the World to please and distract them: so hold all worldly things, that you be not thereby held from God, *ut terrena res possideatur, non possideat, Greg. qu. s.* that so you may possess earthly things, but not be possessed thereby.

**V. 22. ***{and yet there is room}* Many being collected of the Jews, yet was there place left for a greater fullness of the Gentiles, *August. de verb. Dom. ser.* 34. nicely distinguishes, the Gentiles came from the Streets, and Heretics from the Hedges. The meaning is, to show God's inexhaustible mercy to all sorts of penitent sinners.

**V. 23. ***{compel them}* Not by violence, but by the power of preaching; so *Theophylact* understands it, as Rom. 1. 16. God compels no man to sanctity, but inclines the will thereto: he forces none by violence, though he draw them by persuasions, promises, or threatenings, which is the spiritual compulsion here intended, when the rigor of the Law preached terrifies the sinners' conscience, and so fear of judgments forces him to seek salvation and comfort in Christ. So *John Baptist* compelled his hearers, by telling them, that the ax was laid to the root of the tree, Matt. 3. 10. So Ministers are commanded to do, 2 Tim. 4. 2. So God draws us to Christ, John 6. 44. which is never done. *Sine inclinatione mobilis*, without the inclination of him that is moved, it being an immediate effect of the will sanctified, moved, and enabled by the Spirit of God. This he does, says *Bernard, de gra. & lib. arbitr. Cum terret aut percutit ut faciat voluntarios, non salvet invitos*, when he terrifies or smites to make them willing, not to save them against their wills: when he changes the will from evil to good, he does but change, he does not take its liberty away: as for the coercive or compulsory power of Magistrates, it belongs not to this place, because, not to Ministers: certainly the civil Magistrate bears not the Sword in vain, Rom. 13. 4. Therefore ought he revenge all wickedness, specially, that wherein God's honor and truth are interested: yet is it most requisite, that a tender hand be used toward the weak, on the Minister's part, as is commanded 2 Tim. 2. 24. On the Magistrates, by providing all probable means to inform them better, ever considering the difference between him that errs out of that which they call invincible ignorance, and him, that not only errs willfully, in fundamentals, but also seduces others therein: See Deut. 13. 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 13, 14, 15. Deut. 17. 7. and it is by proportion considerable, if he that murders the body, may not be spared, (Numbers, 35. 31, etc.) much less may he, who murders bodies and souls to Eternity, by making their Proselytes children of Hell, Matthew 23. 15.

**V. 24. ***{none of those men which were bidden}* None of those who absolutely and finally refused the grace and mercy of God offered in the Gospel. He means not here, an absolute, but a general rejection of the Jews; as if he said, few of them (in respect of the multitudes of the Gentiles to be called) shall be saved. See Rom. 11. 1. etc.

**V. 25. ***{said unto them}* There were many followers of Christ present, but not all of one mind and resolution: he who saw all their thoughts, addresses this speech to some of them: and so prosecutes the former argument concerning men's excusing themselves from God's service, by secular businesses: showing, that except men can put off that mind and vain love of this world, they can never be any true Disciples of Christ.

**V. 26. ***{if any man come unto me}* Matt. 10. 37. Matt. 16. 24.

*{and hate not his father}* That is, removes not all carnal affections, contrary to the calling of God; or he who is not disposed (where these loves are incompatible) to hate Father, and all for the love of Christ, can be none of his: so that these things are by no means simply to be understood (as if the service of Christ required any violation of the Laws of God and Nature) but comparatively: I ought dearly and tenderly to love Father and Mother; but to prefer my Creator and Savior before all: as *Ambros. orat. funebr. de ob. Valent.* very well says, *Debeo affectum parenti, sed magis obsequium debeo salutis authori*. I owe affection to my Parent, but I rather owe obedience to the Author of my salvation.

**V. 27. ***{bear his crosse}* That is, (as may appear from the following comparison) when the Lord shall call men thereto to be ready and willing to endure afflictions for Christ's sake. The Cross here, as elsewhere, signifies torments, miseries, calamities, in allusion to those sorrows and torments which Christ on the Cross suffered for us, though all that we can suffer are incomparably less than those. And in this manner of speaking he intimates that we cannot reasonably expect, that the condition of us servants should be better than Christ our Lord and Savior. See Matthew 10. 38.

**V. 28. ***{sitteth not down first}* This shows the means how to bear the Cross patiently and constantly; that is, by resolving beforehand so to do. That which makes men startle away in afflictions, is principally their unpreparedness thereto, and vain hope of secular prosperity. Concerning preparation hereto, he proposes two similitudes: First, of the unconsiderate builder, who for want of forecast, lost much charge on that which he is not able to perfect: So shall they who do much in Christ's service if they reckon not upon the Cross if need require. Secondly, of the unadvised Soldier, who will not timely consider the power of those with whom he must encounter: this life is a warfare; and that truceless; it is great folly ever to be secure.

**V. 33. ***{forsaketh not all that he hath}* That is, when God's glory so requires, for otherwise, as appears in sundry instances, the Disciples of Christ, did not in their following him, leave all their proprieties in that they had; though they were ready ever so to have done, when God's honor, and the testimony of the truth should require them thereto, or to lay down life itself for the same: See on Matt. 8. 14. and 19. 27.

**V. 34. ***{Salt is good}* By Salt, he means the uncorrupted integrity and vigor of mind in the midst of afflictions (which seasons, hardens and keeps from corruption of flesh and blood, ever indulgent to its own ease) without which, we can be profitable for nothing: especially, if Ministers be not wise to season themselves, and others herewith, they are utterly unprofitable.

*{but if the salt hath lost his savour}* Matt. 5. 13. Mark 9. 50. The Disciple of Christ must be as Salt, not only good in himself, but also by his Ministry communicating that savor to others, that they may be sound: as it is written, Titus 1. 13. *Rebuke them sharply that they may be sound in the faith*.

**V. 35. ***{He that hath ears to hear}* He that hears, let him mark diligently. This admonition, as we have continually need of, whenever we hear any part of God's Word, so specially where this subject, the Doctrine of the Cross is preached; which we most unwillingly hear of.

## CHAPTER 15

**Verse 1. ***{Drew near unto him all the Publicans, &c.}* Who were odious to the Jews, because they gathered up the customs and taxes for *Caesar*: by *all*, he means many: or all that came to him to be converted: Of sinners there were (and are) two sorts: some acknowledged or were ashamed of their sin and misery; some were just in their own eyes. Of the first sort were those who came to hear Christ preaching mercy to penitent believers in him. Of the second sort were the Scribes and Pharisees, who highly valued their own merits, and pretended righteousness by the works of the Law; therefore they embraced not the faith in Christ for their justification. So that Christ came not to call them, but those other sinners, Mat. 9. 13. by sinners here understand notorious sinners; for all are sinners.

*{to hear him}* They came not to make their liberty from the curse of the Law, a cloak for sin, but to hear his Doctrine and to convert: they came as the sick to the Physician, to be healed and saved. Feeling the restless stings of their own consciences, against which the rigor of the Law could afford them no ease or relief, but rather cause of greater terror and unrest they came to Christ who preached to them the comfortable doctrine of justification by faith, repentance, and remission of sins, with deliverance from the severe curse and rigor of the law; which doctrine is therefore called *εὐαγγέλιον*, gladsome tidings.

**V. 2. ***{murmured, saying, this man, &c.}* They calumniated Christ among the people; which he perceiving, answered: as in the following Parables, they, being puffed up with vain opinion of their own righteousness, blamed him: first, for admitting sinners into his company: Secondly, for eating with them: this *οὗτος*, this Man, is *emphatical*: as if they had said; this your excellent Doctor, whom you count the *Messiah* and teacher of righteousness, look what company he keeps, Publicans and notorious sinners. Pride and Malice are ever censorious and blind. See cap. 7. 39. And in this example it appears, that it is more possible to be without fault than unjust censure.

**V. 4. ***{What man of you}* Here is a kind of appeal to their own consciences, as chap. 14. 5. The scope of all these Parables, is to teach the bounty of God, and his care of poor sinners' recovery, and how they that are truly sanctified, are so far from envying the conversion of other sinners, that they heartily rejoice thereat.

*{having an hundred sheep}* See Mat. 18. 12. He compares sinners to sheep going astray. So Psal. 119. 176. Isaiah 53. 6. and himself to a careful shepherd, as John 10. Ezek. 34. 15. Acts 20. 28. He leaves ninety and nine for a time; not meaning any desertion of the flock, but a solicitous intending the recovery of that which is strayed, speaking to man's sense. He compares Publicans and sinners to stray cattle, intimating his care to save the elect, and joy in their recovery from Sin and Death. Wherein he secretly taxes the envy of Pharisees for impious and Satanical, which so much differed from the joy of holy Angels in the presence of God at sinners' conversion: So lays he down a singular ground of comfort to the converted: for if God bear such love to the elect, that he would own them, and seek them, who had no hearts to seek him, being yet dead in trespasses and sins, how shall he not much more know them and hear them, when being found of him they seek him in truth. See Esay 65. 1. Rom. 5. 10. Rom. 8. 32. & 10. 20.

*{until he find it}* Christ the good shepherd came to seek that which was lost, and will never give over his work unperfected; the sinner is then prevented by God's grace, and enabled to seek him, as *Fulgentius de incarn. & grat. Dom. Jesu* says, *quando illuminatur ut credat*, when he is illuminated that he may believe.

**V. 5. ***{layeth it on his shoulder, rejoicing}* Hereby also Christ declares his love and care of sinners, who has borne all our infirmities, Matth. 8. 17. And the duty of Pastors which is to labor, as well to recover the lost, as to preserve the remainder of their flock. See 2 Tim. 2. 24, 25, 26. Matt. 18. 14, 15. etc.

**V. 7. ***{joy shall be in heaven}* Which he interprets v. 10. joy in the presence of the Angels of God; and thus he accommodates his speech to vulgar capacities: understanding by *just persons*, those who seem so to themselves, having no sense of their sins and need of repentance. See Prov. 30. 12. Eccles. 7. 22. or those who continue in the right way, Psalm 119. 1. Those who being justified by faith, yet daily pray, *forgive us our trespasses*. This understand, as joy is defined, *an affection arising from some fresh or new good*: Hence we may conceive how God and his good Angels are displeased at sins, whereas his Spirit is said to be grieved at them, Ephes. 4. 30. Ezek. 18. 23.

**V. 8. ***{ten pieces of silver}* This similitude is to the same purpose. *Drachma's*, here translated, pieces of silver, is the eighth part of an ounce, which comes to seven pence half penny, and is equal to the Roman penny, Matt. 18. 28.

*{if she lose one}* As if he said, you would all justify that care and pains which were bestowed in seeking that secular thing which is lost, abroad or at home: would you allow the seeking of lost beasts, and not of lost man? Is it justifiable to use all lawful means for the recovery of a little piece of silver with *Caesar's* Image upon it; and is it with you condemnable to seek a lost man, who has God's Image on him? By this reason he evidently convinces their malice: So does he admirably comfort the Elect. Intimating, that God will leave no means unattempted for the recovery of them.

**V. 9. ***{calleth her friends}* Here he proves by an Argument, from the less to the greater, that the Angels rejoice at the conversion of a sinner; because they have greater love and joy than man: also he shows what the captious Pharisees should have done, if they would imitate the Holy Angels, who were so far from envying any man's salvation, that they rejoice with one another for it: also here appears the great pains and care which ought to be taken for the recovery of lost sinners, contrary to the course of proud Pharisees, who swelling with a deluding conceit of their own Holiness, esteemed penitent sinners and Publicans no better than forlorn castaways.

**V. 11. ***{a certain man had two sons}* By this third similitude, he declares the wretched condition of those who live in sin and unbelief, which he proposes in the younger brother: as also how ready God is to forgive the penitent.

**V. 12. ***{Give me the portion of Goods}* That which you intend to give me for my portion: by this, *τὸ* *ἐπιβάλλον* *μέρος*, the portion of goods which falls unto me, he means that part, which *jure haeredis*, or *filii*, was to fall to his share: Syriac, *quae redit ad me ex domo tua*; as if he said, I am now of age to manage an estate; grant me now the free use thereof, that I may trade for myself.

*{he divided unto them his living}* *Τὸν* *βίον* imports here, as elsewhere, that by which life is maintained, which the Syriac interprets, *his possession*. See Mark 12. 44. Here is a Character of an evil son. First, in desiring a division of the estate, his Father being yet alive. Secondly, in that having possession of his portion given him, he presently forsakes his Father. Thirdly, in that he quickly consumed all in riotous living: in all which is seen an image of man fallen into sin; when ambitious (as it were) of sharing with God, and seeking happiness in himself, and his own foolish ways and inventions, rather than in God, the only Fountain of true Happiness, he departed from God, sin separating him: thus evilly using that freedom of Will which God gave him for good, he lost himself and all Will and ability to do good: Hence flowed in that Deluge of sin and misery on men, God permitting the Heathens to their own lusts and errors, Romans 1. 24. Hence also those miseries shadowed under this young man's poverty and extreme want.

**V. 15. ***{he sent him into his fields to feed swine}* Here in the letter appears the sad condition of ungoverned young Prodigals, to be vilified most by them, on whom they vainly spent their Estates: as also the just judgment on the rash and disobedient: He that would not cleave to a loving Father, that it might have been well with him, is compelled to adhere to an unkind stranger, and to be made a stranger, and to be made a servant of the vilest beasts, who undervalued the dignity of a son: He that would not understand the blessing of plenty, is better instructed by extreme want: and in the Moral, the base servitude which the Devil puts his servants to, in feeding and communicating with the most unclean affections.
