**V. 16. ***{and would fain have filled his belly with the husks}* By these *κεράτια*, *husks*, whether we understand those *siliquas* or pods of pulse, peas or beans: or, as *Theophylact* seems to understand, and others say, of the fruit of the *carob tree*, which bears a pleasant fruit in pods also, it skills not much; though the latter seem a very probable opinion, the *Syriac* reading here *חרובא* *charubo*, some give it *καρούβια*: *siliqua dulcis*, a sweet pod. *Strabo* cited by V. *Shindler, Penteglot.* says, *multum haec cognita Nicae, & maris Ligustici accolis, ubi eam esitant, pueri, etiamque porci*: this is much known in *Nicae*, and to those who dwell by the Sea of *Liguria*, where children, and also hogs use to eat thereof. *Galen* also mentions this tree bearing these *κεράτια*, by later Writers called the *Carob tree*, and the fruit thereof *Saint Johns bread*; (that which *John Baptist* eat, Matt. 3.) or Locusts. See *John Gerard. hist. plant.* lib. 3. cap. 82. *J. Drus. praeterit.* lib. 3. *Plin.* lib. 15. c. 24. whatever they were, *no man gave unto him*: as if he said, they who allowed their Swine a certain measure thereof would not allow him to fill his belly therewith. The Moral is, that in God's House his children are satisfied, as it were with fatness, Psalm 65. 5. there is distributed the bread or portion of sons, Matt. 15. 26. 32. but out of his Family, husks of unclean Swine, the lying fables of traditions, which do rather puff up with vain pride, than nourish to Eternal life: so are profane men compared to Swine, 2 Pet. 2. Of the famine of God's Word. See Amos 8. 11. See *Theophylact* hereon.

**V. 17. ***{when he came to himself}* Hitherto he was beside himself like a mad or drunken man, not knowing what he did, but necessity brought him to his right mind: Happy affliction which brings men to God by serious repentance and newness of life; truly may such a one say with *David*, Psalm 119. 71. *it is good for me that I have been afflicted, that I might learn thy Statutes*.

*{have bread enough}* The moral is, the meanest in God's family abound with spiritual comforts, and are in a condition incomparably better than any out of it. See Psalm 84. 11. *which God makes them at last to understand, to whom he gives a heart to know their own condition, and to convert*.

**V. 18. ***{I will arise and go, &c.}* Not that any sinner can prevent God's grace, see John 6. 44. Jer. 31. 33. Ezek. 11. 19, 20. God alone gives repentance, who in Christ forgives the penitent: the sinner indeed says in his heart, as this young man, *I will arise and go*, etc. but never until God works such a Resolution on him, see Phil. 2. 13. So that here are represented the effects of God's mercy in the conversion of a sinner. 1. Dislike and detestation of the former courses of life. 2. Arising from the drunken sleep and dotage of sin. 3. Certain purpose to leave sin, and to return to God in newness of life. 4. Confidence of Grace and merciful reception with our Heavenly Father. 5. Ardent and longing desire of the sweet and true comforts which are in God's House and Family. 6. Ingenuous confession of our sin before God. 7. Humble acknowledgment of our unworthiness (*I am no more worthy to be called thy son*) I have indeed forgotten the duty of a son, yet, O unchangeable God of mercy, forget not you the love of a Father. See Psalm 130. 3, 4.

*{against heaven}* Against God, *whose Throne is in Heaven, where his Divine Majesty and glory most evidently shines and is manifested to the creatures there being*.

**V. 19. ***{As one of thy hired servants}* Let me be anything, so I may be yours. See Psalm 84. 10. I am unworthy of that first dignity of your son, from which I willfully departed, of which I foolishly deprived myself, it shall be of your abundant mercy to forgive me, and to admit me to any share of that grace of yours, whereof the meanest of your servants partake.

**V. 20. ***{He arose and came}* This action describes an inseparable condition of true Repentance, which is not so much in resolving, or saying, as in doing according to Holy resolutions of turning to God.

*{yet a great way off}* This intimates God's preventing grace toward us. So are all sinners of themselves, far off from God, (*for their iniquities have separated between them and their God*,—Isa. 59. 2.) *and far from any thought of good*, (2 Cor. 3. 5.) *or resolution to leave their sins and impious courses of life to return unto the Lord in newness and sincerity of living*.

*{his father saw him and had compassion, &c.}* The meaning is, as if he said; if men naturally desirous of revenge, and too tenacious of privilege in punishing, can yet out of fatherly affection, so easily forget children's offenses, and be reconciled; how much more will God, who is of infinite mercy and pity, receive penitent sinners? certainly (says *Salvian. ep. Ipatio*, etc.) though Parents have just causes of being angry, yet nothing can more happily, or which they more wish, fall out, than that their children may so satisfy for their faults, *ne necesse habeant vindicare*, that they need not to revenge, the word *ἐσπλαγχνίσθη*, had compassion, signifies, a vehement moving to pity, such as proceeds *ex intimis visceribus* from the bowels and yearning of the heart. See Psalm 103. 13. Isa. 49. 15. Ezek. 16. 6. Jer. 2. 2.

**V. 22. ***{Bring forth the best robe}* Signifying the Robe of Christ's Righteousness; which is incomparably, and in infinite respects the best. *We all are as an unclean thing, and all our Righteousnesses are as filthy rags*—Isai. 64. 6. No wonder that *Paul* touching the Righteousness which is in the Law, blameless, yet counted *all things but loss, for the excellency of the knowledge of Christ Jesus,—and but dung, that he might win Christ, and be found in him not having his own righteousness which is of the Law, but that which is of the Faith of Christ, the Righteousness which is of God by Faith*, Phil. 3. 6, 7, 8, 9. this is the best robe; in which only the blessing of eternal life and salvation is to be attained. See Romans 9. 30, 31.

*{a ring on his hand}* It may import the riches of God's bounty, as also the Saint's honor and ornament, the pledge of God's love, or seal of faith.

*{and shoes}* The moral may be that which the Apostle expresses, Ephes. 6. 15. in all these things are signified God's goodness and our happiness in our conversion; wherein so much more is restored than was lost, by how much more excellent that state is, whence we shall never fall, than that from whence we all did fall.

**V. 23. ***{And kill it}* That is, Christ to be offered in sacrifice on the Cross, when the Gentiles (the younger brother *Japheth*) returned to the Tents of *Shem*, and to the God of the Fathers: and he is brought (as it were) to be slain for every believer, when the Gospel is preached to him. See Galat. 3. 1. *Augustine* To. 4. *quaest. sup. Evang.* lib. 2. cap. 33.

**V. 24. ***{this my son was dead and is alive}* The unregenerate is dead in trespasses and sins, Ephes. 2. 1. conversion and regeneration is a resurrection from the dead works of sin, to newness of life, Revel. 20. 6. Rom. 6. 11. 13. etc.

*{they began to be merry}* This is the same which is expressed, verse 7. 10.

**V. 25. ***{his elder son}* By which is meant, he that takes himself to be just, as verse 29. or is so accounted of others; who never ran out into any apparent exorbitancy, as the Gentiles did, and so the elder brother might signify the Jews, or those that continued in an upright course of life: indeed the proud Jews envied the mercy which God showed the penitent Gentiles, as Acts 10. 13. So *Augustin. q. s.* understands it: *Theophylact* of the Pharisees envying the reception of sinners.

**V. 27. ***{safe and sound}* *ὑγιαίνοντα*, in health and safety: In the moral it imports the safe and blessed condition of the Gentiles believing in Christ, who freely justifies sinners, and sanctifies those whom he justifies.

**V. 28. ***{would not go in}* That is, would not believe and partake with the Church of Christ, specially of the Gentiles, whom they counted desperate sinners unworthy of God's favor, as Rom. 11. 28, 31.

*{his father came out}* Signifying that God left no means unattempted to win the Jews to Christ: but sent his Ministers to preach the gospel to them first, until they put the Word of God from them, and judged themselves unworthy of everlasting life, Acts 13. 46.

**V. 29. ***{these many years}* This shadows out the arrogancy of the Jews, presuming on their merits and righteousness by the Law, not that they did not continually transgress the Law of God, and provoke him to anger, but because they had been many years his Church, and peculiar People; and because they were as high minded, as if they had never sinned and as unthankful, as if God never had done anything for them worthy of their service.

**V. 31. ***{all that I have is thine}* Intimating his abundant goodness toward that nation. See Psalm 147. 20. Isai. 5. 4. etc. Deut. 32. 7. Jer. 31. 9.

**V. 32. ***{was lost, and is found}* This imports the dangerous alienation of sinners specially of the Gentiles, from the Covenant of grace, until they were called by the Gospel according to the Prophecies; wherein *Noah's* prophetical prayer took effect, and *Japheth* (of whom came the Gentiles) was persuaded by the Word and Spirit of God, to dwell in the tent of *Shem*, Genes. 9. 27. that is, with that people of the Jews which believed in Christ.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1. ***{There was a certain rich man}* So verse 19. the drift of that which follows, is to show us, that they who bestow their riches or other gifts of God on their pleasures; or worldly interests only, must give account to God for them: and that Charity shall stand us in such stead in the day of judgment, that God will accept thereof, and abundantly requite it.

**V. 2. ***{give account of thy stewardship}* By this manner of speaking, he minds us, that we are God's stewards of all those temporal goods, which he puts into our hands for a time, to use them to his glory, others' good, and our own comfort: and that we must at last give a strict account hereof to God, See 1 Pet. 4. 9, 10. Hag. 2. 8. Psalm 50. 10.

**V. 4. ***{I am resolved what to do}* This perverse reasoning of his, is not proposed to our imitation, neither such robbing of his master, by deceiving trust, and making up false accounts: that being forbidden of God, and the contrary commanded. See Psalm 37. 3. Ephes. 4. 28. 2 Thes. 3. 10, 11, 12.

**V. 6. ***{an hundred measures of oil}* *ἑκατὸν* *βάτους*, Syr. Interpreter, *metretas*: the Hebrew word *בת* *bath*, signified a measure of liquid things, Oil, Wine, etc. Isai. 5. 10. 1 Kings 7. 26. 2 Chron. 4. 5. it contained nine gallons three quarters: the same in quantity with the Ephah, a common measure among the Hebrews: the *חמר* *homer*, contained ten *baths* (Ezek. 45. 14.) or *ephahs*; and the *ephah* contained ten *עמר* *omers*, Exod. 16. 36. so that it contained about nine gallons: some say much less, see *Godwin. Moses and Aaron*, lib. 6. cap. 9.

**V. 7. ***{an hundred measures of wheat}* *ἑκατὸν* *κόρους*, *Solomon's* provision for a day (1 Kings 4. 22.) of fine flour, was thirty *כורים* *chorim*, measures. See *Joseph. Antiq.* lib. 8. cap. 2. and lib. 15. cap. 12. where he says, *corus autem capit decem medimnos Atticos*. It contained as much as the *homer*, Ezek. 45. 14. which some reckon five bushels and five gallons (See *Godwin q. l. J. Drusius praeterit.* lib. 3. *in loc.*) Some about 14 bushels, some much more.

**V. 8. ***{commended the unjust steward}* We must not strain similitudes beyond the intent of the speaker to make every particular agree: the meaning is, that God commended them, who so used their temporal goods that thereby they might prepare them treasure for the life to come, Matthew 6. 19, 20. 1 Timothy 6. 18, 19.

*{are in their generation}* Meaning that worldly men commonly show more care and providence for secular things, than others for things eternal: *υἱοὶ* *τοῦ* *αἰῶνος* *τούτου* children of this world; is an Hebrew manner of speaking, signifying those that are wholly addicted to the care and study of things secular. So the Syr. has it, *filii seculi hujus*, those that love, serve and imitate the world and worldly men, not much caring or regarding what becomes of them in the next world, to whom he opposes in a contrary quality and condition—children of light.

*{children of light}* That is, the children of God illuminated by his Word and Holy Spirit: the sum is, that the master of that steward (commending the wit, not the fidelity of his servant) represents Christ stirring up our minds to a prudent and careful providence for our future safety (seeing that even the blind worldlings (who mind only secular things) are so circumspect for things which perish in using.

**V. 9. ***{make to yourselves}* This figurative manner of speaking is accommodated and fitted to the allegory: the meaning is, by your beneficence to the poor, give them occasion to pray for you, and make your advantage of being rewarded by God. See Proverbs 19. 17.

*{Mammon of unrighteousness}* The Greek calls that here *μαμμωνᾶ*, which the Syriac calls *ממונא* *mamuno*: so they said, *mammon, mamuna*, or *mamonas*: as *Satan, Satana*, or *Satanas*. See *Jo. Drus. ad voc. N. T.* tom. 1. and ib. tom. 2. cap. 14. *Irenaeus* lib. 3. cap. 8. *Augustin. de verb. Domini ser.* 35. and lib. 2. s. *serm. Domini in monte*, says, that *mamona* in the Punic tongue signifies gain: or as he *de verb. Dom. in Luc. serm.* 35. and *quaest. Evang.* lib. 2. cap. 34. *divitiae seculi*, riches of this world. *Suidas* interprets *μαμμωνᾶς*, *χρυσὸς*, *γήϊνος* *πλοῦτος*, *ὁ* *περιττὸς*, *καὶ* *ὑπὲρ* *τὴν* *χρείαν*, gold, earthly riches, *supervacaneus* or *more than necessary*: so *Anastas. Sinait.* q. 12. *M. [Grotius]* derives it from the Heb. *אמן* *aman*, among other things signifying *nutrire, sustentare*, and so *mamon* signifies food or sustenance for this life: as also it may come of the same root, as it signifies *credere*, because men usually trust therein: See Psal. 37. 3. and *Jo. Buxtorf. Jun. lex. chald. & Syr.* *ממון*, it is called here *mammon of unrighteousness*: not that we should give for alms or holy uses goods unjustly gotten: (for all such must be restored again to their right owners; and as *Basil* says well, *Reg. Fusius expl.* *ἐξ* *ἀδίκων* *κερδῶν*, alms given of goods or gains unjustly made,—are not acceptable to God, though alms be a sacrifice wherewith God is pleased, Heb. 13. yet as *Isidor. epist. ad Euseb. Episc.* notes: *οὐ* *χρήζει* *ὁ* *Θεὸς* *ἐξ* *ἀλλοτρίων* *θυσίας*, he has no need of sacrifice out of other men's goods, but abominates it, as him that offers a dog, or builds up Zion with blood) but riches are so called, because commonly either in their getting, using or keeping, they administer matter of sin, 1 Tim. 6. 9.

*{that when ye fail}* That is, when you die, or when your interest in, or further use of these things (of which you are for present appointed stewards) shall fail you: which may be in this life, in death it must be: the short time of this life, and uncertainty of secular dispensations of the things which God's providence allots us, should teach us to hold all that we have here with a loose hand; and not to be foolishly tenacious of that which may suddenly, and most certainly at last be taken from us.

*{everlasting habitations}* What manner of habitations these shall be in heaven whereof Christ speaks, John 14. 2. *potius debet credi, quam dici, Prosper. de vit. contempl.* lib. 1. cap. 2. See 1 Cor. 2. 9, 10. the meaning of this place is not, that we shall merit to find favor with God by our alms, for he was our friend, when we were his enemies, (Rom. 5. 10.) and of no strength to do any good: and merit is between them where there is no debt, or grace: but here where man can never discharge his debt of sin, all our merit is God's mercy: the meaning therefore is, if the unjust steward wanted not friends for giving his master's goods; how much less shall we, if we give ours to relieve God's poor, for whom God has promised repayment? Works of mercy shall both here testify to the giver's conscience, that he is the elect of God, who has given him so happy an use of those secular riches he gave him, and it shall be rewarded in the everlasting life to come. Mat. 25. 2 Pet. 1. 10. James 2. 22, 23.

**V. 10. ***{he that is faithful, &c.}* This seems a proverbial speech, commonly true: as if he said: he that is found unfaithful in a little, shall not be trusted with much: he that is true and trusty in a little, shall be trusted in much: if you use secular things well, whereof God has made you stewards for a time, he will give into your hands heavenly and eternal riches.

**V. 11. ***{unrighteous mammon}* If you abuse Temporal riches, whereof you are but stewards to an omniscient God, how can you hope that he will trust you with spiritual and heavenly, which are true riches.

**V. 12. ***{That which is another man’s}* He calls external goods another man's, because we are but stewards thereof, and must leave them to others: spiritual goods are our own, because they shall never be taken away; not but that God gave them all, nor that we may use them any otherwise than to God's honor.

*{who shall give you that which is your own?}* That is, God will not give you, in whose power alone it is to give: this manner of speaking by interrogation, was with the Hebrews very emphatical, and here is as if he had said, assure yourselves none will give you, that which is your own, imports here heavenly and eternal riches; not that we have them of ourselves, or not of God, but in regard of our eternal interest therein, for being once had and possessed, they shall never be lost again, because God who gives us grace, therewith ever gives us perseverance: so that the seeming grace which ever fails or falls quite away, was never true grace of God, whose gifts of this kind, are without repentance. Rom. 11. 28.

**V. 13. ***{can serve two masters}* See on Matt. 6. 24.

*{yee cannot serve God and Mammon}* You may possess and be served of riches, and withal serve God, and lay up a good foundation for yourselves, 1 Tim. 6. 19. treasures in Heaven: but you cannot serve riches, but with God's disservice. To serve riches is to be an idolater, and so, no servant of God. See Col. 3. 5.

**V. 14. ***{And they derided him}* As the manner of carnal men is, in anything which crosses their corrupt minds: these men thought none happy but the rich, *ἐξεμυκτήριζον*, *naso suspendebant. Clem. Roman. ep. ad corinth.* p. 51. says, *χλευάζουσιν* *ἡμᾶς* *καὶ* *μυκτηρίζουσι*, *Suidas* interprets it by *ἐξουθενεῖν*, to contemn as a thing of nought, and unconsiderable: therefore these men being rich, covetous, and making wealth their God, and derided him, as if he broached vain Paradoxes, eccentric, and far out of the way to the world's affections: and such esteem have all covetous men of the doctrine of alms, and a holy contempt of the world.

**V. 15. ***{ye are they which justify yourselves}* Who magnify or excuse yourselves: or you would fain seem just before men: here he touches the secret cause of their contempt of his Doctrine, their impious pride: and shows that he as it were convented them before God's Tribunal, by the testimony of their own Conscience, showing that such pride and hypocrisy, is abominable before God the searcher of hearts, however beguiled men applaud or canonize them. See Job 10. 4. 1 Sam. 16. 6. Matth. 23. 25. Isai. 5. 15. and on Matt. 6. 2.

**V. 16. ***{the Law and the Prophets}* Matt. 11. 11. when *John Baptist* showed Christ present, all prophecies concerning his coming ceased, and all the types prefiguring him in their accomplishment, *μετ᾽* *ἐκεῖνον* *γὰρ* *οὐδεὶς* *ὅλως* *προφήτης* *παρ᾽* *ὑμῖν* *γεγένηται* said *Justin Martyr*, to the Jew after him (that is, *John Baptist*) was there not one Prophet born among you, which is true, if he mean none speaking of a Messiah to come.

*{presseth into it}* See Matth. 11. 12. everyone who does believe zealously follows the Gospel: Greek *εἰς* *αὐτὴν* *βιάζεται*, presses as a man in a throng; so the Syr. *premit ut ingrediatur*; the word signifies, *vi contendere*: here as it were forcibly by might and main, all possible endeavors to enter into the Kingdom of God; See Matt. 11. 12.

**V. 17. ***{easier for heaven and earth to pass, &c.}* See on Matt. 5. 18. God's Decree for our salvation shall not fail.

**V. 18. ***{whosoever putteth away his wife}* See on Matt. 5. 32. and 19. 9. Mark 10. 5. 1 Cor. 7. 11. except in case of adultery: the Bill of Divorce was permitted for a time, because of the hardness of their hearts, and cruelty towards their Wives, Mal. 2. 15, 16. and that by their judicial Law, not by the Moral, which admits of no dispensation: and therefore Christ recalls all his to the first Institution.

**V. 19. ***{there was a certain rich man}* Having spoken concerning the right use of temporal goods; now he proceeds to show the horrible punishment of those who do not use them well: this was one of those rich men, who (as *Cyprian*, l. 2. ep. 2. says) *possident ad hoc tantum, ne possidere—alteri liceat*, possess goods only to keep others from possessing them.

*{in purple and fine linen}* By these things which were in use among them, he describes the luxury of worldly men, as Revel. 18. 16. also their unhappiness who abuse riches, and their happiness who make an Holy use of poverty; which he does by proposing one, as *Fulgentius* speaks, *ad Gal. ep.* 2. *in divitiis & sanitate miserum*; and another, *in egestate & vulnere multum beatum*, one unhappy in wealth and health, the other abundantly blessed in wants and sores. The abuse of his riches was, 1. in curious clothing: 2. in gluttony: 3. in neglect and contempt of the poor. *Clem. Alexandrinus, Paed.* l. 2. says the ancient Lacedaemonians, were wont to permit whores only to wear gorgeous apparel. See 1 Peter 3. 3, 4.

**V. 20. ***{a certain beggar named Lazarus}* Some write the name *Eleazarus, Tertul. de idolatr.* cap. 13. (as some copies have it) Hebr. *אלעזר* *Eleazar*; and the Syr. *לעזר* *Lahazar*, this was a poor diseased man, therefore not that *Lazarus*, John 11. the brother of *Mary* and *Martha*. In these we see an Image of the World, wherein the wicked flourish, and the righteous often want: the *antithesis* is remarkable; in the one riches, in the other, poverty and beggary; he clothed with purple and fine linen, this with rags; he feasting, this lying hungry at his gate, desiring but some crumbs or scraps falling from the rich man's table, or left after meals; this full of sores, a pitiful creature, he more pitiless than his dogs.

**V. 21. ***{dogs came and licked his sores}* *Sine cibo, sed non sine Christo: canibus expositum, sed socium angelorum*, says *Fulgent. q. s. ad Gallam.* ep. 2. without meat, not without Christ: exposed to the dogs, yet accompanied with Angels.

**V. 22. ***{into Abraham’s, bosom}* He means the communion of Saints and faithful, who walked in the steps of *Abraham's* faith and sanctity, and therefore called his sons, Rom. 4. 12. 16. Gal. 3. 7, 9. Luke 19. 9. *sinus Patriarcharum, recessus quidam est quietis aeternae, Ambros. orat. funebr. de obit. Valent.* the bosom of the Patriarchs is a certain retiring place of eternal rest.

*{the rich man also died}* This is common to all estates and conditions: poor and rich die alike: See Psal. 49. 10. the future makes no difference: to the one, whom men regarded not living, in his death the Angels are ministering spirits to convey him safe to Heaven: (a precious soul is sometimes lodged in a diseased body; the graces of God's Spirit, are so covered with the deformity of afflictions, as that carnal reason can sometimes discern nothing therein, but the appearance of the curse) he says no more of the rich man, than that he was buried, leaving his passage to be gathered from verse 23.

*{and was buried}* Possibly with great pomp; no funerals or tombs can shelter men from the wrath of God.

**V. 23. ***{in hell lift up his eyes}* Gr. *ἐν* *τῷ* *ᾅδῃ*, *in that lightless or dark place where nothing can be seen*, elsewhere called utter darkness, Matt. 5. 30. Syr. *בשיול* *basheiul*, Heb. *בשאול*, *in hell*, the place of the damned, Numb. 16. 33. sometimes it signifies horror of mind, Psalm 116. 3. sometimes the grave; so Gen. 37. 35. *Jacob* says, *I shall go down* *שאלה*, Chald. *לשאול* 70. *εἰς* *ᾅδου* *subaud.* *οἶκον* or *τόπον*, to the house or place of the grave. The Greek Fathers used this term, as the Latins, *apud inferos*, promiscuously for any state or condition of the dead, See *Irenaeus* lib. 5. c. ult. *Tertul. de Idolatr.* cap. 13, etc. *Chrysost. hom. de divite*, etc. (in that lifting up the eyes, is attributed to a spirit, it must either be *humanitus dictum*, as Phil. 2. 10. bowing the knee is spoken by things in Heaven: or it must conclude this rather a Parable than an History) sometimes the word imports an afflicted, wretched, hopeless, and irreparable condition. See on Matt. 11. 23. Act. 1. 27. here it expresses the torments of the damned, aggravated v. 25. by their remembrance of worldly prosperities, and the consideration of the Elect's happiness whom they formerly despised.

**V. 24. ***{and he cried}* The meaning is, that they who will not now hear and pity the cries of the poor, shall cry in hell and not be pitied; See Matt. 25. 11. Prov. 21. 13. James 2. 8. he that would not give the crumbs which fell from his Table, shall cry in hell for a drop of water, and not obtain it; that is, the least comfort to assuage his torments shall be denied him, though he eternally beg for it: *flammae infelicium mortuorum divitiis non refrigerantur haeredum*; the flames of the unhappy dead, are not cooled by the riches of their Heirs. *Tim. ad Ecclesiam Catholicam* lib. 3.

**V. 25. ***{Son remember}* This is a common manner of speaking; otherwise, the wicked are not to be accounted sons of *Abraham*, John 8. 39. Gal. 3. 9. This title, son, has in it a tacit reprehension, seeming to have this sense; you call *Abraham* Father, admit you are of his carnal seed, this prerogative can profit you nothing, who lived so unlike holy *Abraham*: he was commended for beneficence and hospitality, Gen. 18. 2, 3, etc. but you used to shut your gate against the poor and needy? If you were indeed a son of *Abraham*, you would have done the works of *Abraham*. See John 8. 39.

*{receivedst good things}* In the end, that appears true which *Chrysostom* proves, *conc.* 3. *de Lazaro*, prosperous impiety is very dangerous: There are two reasons of his denial. First, from Equity: you have had your part of comfort on Earth, where *Lazarus* has been afflicted; it is but equal, that now, you who so much abused prosperity, should be afflicted, and Holy *Lazarus* comforted. See 2 Thessalonians 1. 6. Secondly, from impossibility, there is a vast distance, so that none can pass from Heaven to Hell, nor from Hell to Heaven: as the blessedness of these, so the misery of the other, is unchangeable, and Eternal.

**V. 27. ***{send him to my fathers house}* Herein is described their vain desires, who tempt God in the neglect or contempt of his Ordinances, requiring miracles or testimony from the dead, to persuade them; whereas God (the only sufficient witness, and teacher of things Divine and Heavenly) sends all men to the Law, Prophets and holy Scriptures. See Isaiah 8. 19, 20. John 5.

**V. 28. ***{may testify unto them}* That he may testify to them what woeful torments are in this place, that they may not come hither by their sins and unbelief.

**V. 29. ***{Moses and the Prophets}* That is, the Law given by *Moses'* ministry: and the writings of the Prophets, and also preachers of the same word.

**V. 31. ***{neither will they be persuaded}* He that will not believe the Spirit of God speaking in the prophets, will not believe a man pretending that he came from the dead, and telling what the wicked suffer there; for who could believe a creature, who cannot rest assured in the infallible testimony of God himself? neither would the Jews believe *Lazarus*, who had been four days dead, and was raised again from the dead, John 11.

## CHAPTER 17

**Verse 1. ***{It is impossible}* Matth. 18. 7. It must needs be that offenses or scandals come: or, I foresee it will be so, because of men's wickedness, *σκάνδαλα*. *σκανδαλίζω* is of *σκάζω*, *claudico*: it imports *impingere facio*. See 1 Pet. 2. 8. *Theophylact* interprets it here, by *τὰ* *κωλύματα* *τῆς* *ἀγαθῆς* *καὶ* *θεοφιλοῦς* *πολιτείας*, hindrances to a good and godly life: and that which is pleasing to God. *Nazianzen orat.* 1. interprets it by *πρόσκομμα*, *offendiculum*, as *λίθος* *προσκόμματος* *a stone of stumbling*, 1 Peter 2. 8.

**V. 2. ***{offend one of these little ones}* That is, to make them halt, or fall away from God: he discovers the sin of those times (which was to think it little or no sin, by their pride and imperious importunity, to scandal an infirm, poor, or inferior brother) here he tells also of the punishment; speaking of scandals given by Heretics, Schismatics, apostates, or the like, whether in word or deed: (See Rom. 16. 17. Titus 1. 16.) for otherwise offenses are often taken, by perverse and ignorant men, from good and laudable things done or spoken, because they cross their blind and corrupt opinions: so were the Scribes and Pharisees; were scandaled, and offended at Christ's doctrine and works of mercy: but blessed are those, who are not offended in Christ, Matth. 11. 6. It were better, or more tolerable (as the Syriac has it) for a man thus scandaling one of the meanest of God's children, to be cast into the most desperate or helpless condition of this life, than to endure the eternal torments which await him in Hell.

**V. 3. ***{take heed to &c.}* This may be referred either to that he said of scandals, or that which he will now say concerning love and mercy, seeing there is the like hazard in both.

*{if thy brother trespass}* See Mat. 18. 21. This is spoken concerning private or personal offenses, of the public, See 1 Tim. 5. 20. Mat. 18. 15. are laid down certain degrees of admonition and Ecclesiastical discipline.

*{rebuke him}* Or *reprove him*: both are joined, 2 Timothy 4. 2. but it must be done respectively, or with regard had to the person. See 1 Tim. 5. 1. for charity must not connive at sin, but discreetly reprove it. See Lev. 19. 17. Ephes. 5. 11. 1 Cor. 5. 6.

*{if he repent forgive}* That is, call him not into question or sue him not: forgive as much as concerns you, yea, though he offend you not once only seven, but many times; if he do not acknowledge his fault to you, yet let your heart be ready to forgive: commit your cause to God: as often as he offends, forgive, which is meant by seventy times seven times. Mat. 18. 22.

**V. 4. ***{seven times}* Many times. See verse 3. as often as he offends and repents.

**V. 5. ***{increase our faith}* Herein they acknowledged that faith is his gift, as Ephes. 2. 8. and that their faith wanted confirmation: for, says *Fulgentius ad Prob. ep.* 4. though the Lord give new strength to his, in their conflicts, lest they should fail, *permittitur tamen fatigari, propriae infirmitatis onere, &c.* yet overladen mortality is permitted to be wearied with the burden of its own infirmity; that, finding no strength in itself, it may quickly address itself to implore aid and Divine assistance.

**V. 6. ***{and the Lord said}* Mat. 17. 20. Mark 11. 23.

*{it should obey you}* See Mat. 17. 20. This manner of speech he uses to show the admirable power of faith; to wit, that it should overcome all difficulties, as Mark 9. 23. All things are possible to him *that believeth*: certainly the effects of faith in fervent prayer have been more than removing trees, or mountains. See Hebrews 11. 29, 30, 33, etc. James 5. 15, 17, 18. 1 John 5. 16. John 10. 12, etc. Exod. 17. 11. By a grain of Mustard seed is meant the least measure, *Mat.* 13. 32. by *be thou plucked up by the root* he means that they should do wonderful things.

**V. 7. ***{which of you having a servant plowing}* By this similitude taken from the duty of servants to their masters, he prevents a conclusion, which haply the Apostles would have gathered from their merit, who had followed Christ, and therefore might expect so much favor of him, as to give them such faith to do great works: but he shows them that God is debtor to none, (Rom. 4. 4, 5.) that they must do all their duty, and expect God's leisure for the reward, as also to refer all to the good pleasure and providence of God concerning the measure of grace, which he best knows to proportion unto them, so as shall most make for the advancement of his glory and the good and salvation of his servants.

**V. 10. ***{we are unprofitable servants}* *ἀχρεῖοι*] this title is also given to evil servant, Matthew 35. 50. Phil. 11. to show us what our merit is if God should be severe, Job 22. 2. Psalm 143. 2. See also Job 35. 7. and Psalm 16. 2. as also by an argument from the lesser to the greater, to show that we cannot merit any good at God's hand, for if a servant be so bound in duty to a mortal and sinful master, that whatsoever he does, and how diligently soever he serves, yet shall he not deserve so much as thanks at his master's hands: much less can we, (that are not only servants, but unprofitable servants, continually falling short of our duties, and also creatures of God, with all our best endeavors) merit at God's hands: we owe ourselves, and all that we have, are, and possibly can do, to God's service, by whom we live and are: you therefore that dream of meriting, renounce your interest in, and claim to grace, (for grace and merit are incompatible, Rom. 4. 4. Rom. 11. 6.) and as *Ambros. de poenit.* l. 2. c. 8. says (if those books be his) *prius solve debitum, ut quod speraveris impetrare merearis*. First pay your debt, that you may deserve to obtain that you hope for. We are indeed faithful, good, and profitable servants, not of ourselves, or by any works which we can do, but by the free imputation of Christ's righteousness. See Heb. 13. 21.

**V. 11. ***{through the midst of Samaria}* *Omri* King of Israel, 1 King 16. 24. bought the hill *Samaria* of *Shemer*, and built thereon, and called the name of the city which he built, after the name of *Shemer* owner of the hill, *Samaria*, Hebr. *שמרון*, *Shomeron*, 70. *τὸ* *ὄρος* *τὸ* [unclear], See *Anastasius Sinait. qu.* 45.

**V. 12. ***{which stood afar off}* As unclean persons were to do, lest they should infect others. See Levit. 13. 45, 46. these lepers hearing of the fame of Jesus, that he cured all diseases, and was most ready to show mercy to all those who came to him for help, and possibly hearing that Jesus passed by that way, would not foreslow their opportunity of coming to entreat him to heal them. It seems that their disease brought them acquainted, and so they joined in petition, for otherwise, *the Jews have no commerce with Samaritans*, John 4. 9. and one of this company was a *Samaritan*, verse 16.

**V. 13. ***{and said, Jesus Master, have mercy}* It was a cause common to them all, and they joined in one and the same petition, short, but pithy: they say not wherein they desired him to instance his mercy: their misery was evident, and spoke their meaning, for a cure which they were confident he could work: however, they believed (as often it comes to pass) ingratitude in nine of them extinguished the sparks of grace, or miserably obscured and weakened them in these nine.

**V. 14. ***{shew yourselves unto the priests}* See Lev. 14. 2. to them it belonged to judge of the Leprosy, and hereby the unbelieving Priests might be convinced: it may also teach us humility and subjection to authority: *Sacerdotem adhuc appellabat quem sciebat sacrilegum esse, Cyprian* lib. 3. ep. 9. he yet called him Priest, whom he knew to be sacrilegious.

**V. 15. ***{turned back}* Whether he came back to Christ before he had showed himself to the Priest, as Christ enjoined him, is not certain. It is most probable that these were not sent to the Priest to know that they were clean, or to pronounce them so, for that evidently appeared, but to convince the Priests' unbelief, seeing that Christ had done such evident things to confirm his doctrine.

**V. 16. ***{fell down on his face, at his feet}* So adored he the divine power of Christ: and this shows that he was perfectly cured, otherwise he ought not to have come so near.

*{and he was a Samaritan}* These words condemn the Jews of high ingratitude, who being instructed in the Law of God, and having received the same favor from Christ to be cured, yet not so much as came back to thank Christ, which this *Samaritan* did, though he were a stranger from the Commonwealth of Israel, and of a Nation which they condemned as most impious, and vile, so that they held no commerce with them, John 4. 9.

**V. 17. ***{where are the other nine}* As if he said, I have healed ten of you, will none be thankful but this stranger? Not that he did not foresee their ingratitude, but that he might note and declare it unto others then present, to the intent, that they might learn to beware of that most ignoble sin of ingratitude to God, or man.

**V. 18. ***{there are not found}* He compares the Jews with this stranger: when the *Samaritan* showed himself not only thankful in his own person, but modest in not accusing the rest: Jesus thus speaks to his disciples and others present; there are not found, that is, no man appears thankful.

*{to give glory to God}* By confessing that Christ had cured them by a Divine power, for this mercy was showed them not for their sakes, but that the power, and mercy of God in Christ might therein be manifested and declared; therefore this their ingratitude was sacrilegious, for as much as their silence robbed God of his honor due; and Christ of the testimony of the truth of the gospel which he preached and confirmed by this and other like miracles;

*{save this stranger}* For the *Samaritans* were Colonies brought into those parts by *Salmanasar* King of *Assyria* out of divers Provinces. See 2 Kings 17. 24, etc. he calls him *stranger*, not to disgrace him but to set off the fault, and to condemn the ingratitude of the Jews; that no stranger might despair, nor any glory of holy parents, if themselves be degenerate.

**V. 19. ***{arise, go thy way}* He graciously raises him that was prostrate before him, neither does he dismiss him without commending his faith and gratitude, and it is most likely, that Christ, who showed him this mercy, would not leave his own work imperfect, dismissing him without further instructing him in the mysteries of eternal life and salvation.

**V. 20. ***{the Pharisees}* See on c. 14. 1.

*{not with observation}* Or *with outward show*: meaning that it should not be, as they dreamed, a kingdom temporal and glorious, with external pomp and Majesty; but spiritual, and so not to be discovered or observed by the world: for otherwise it appeared to the faithful by many evident signs and demonstrations, that Jesus was indeed that long expected *Messias*, and King of *Israel*, John 1. 49.

**V. 21. ***{within you}* Or *among you*, in the Gospel preached, which is the scepter of his kingdom; and the *Messias*, whose kingdom you have with ardent votes desired, is now among you in your houses and Synagogues, and you acknowledge him not, John 1. 27. and the spiritual kingdom of God is also in the hearts of the faithful, and so to be discerned by them only, who are subjected thereto, Rev. 2. 17. See Rom. 14. 14.

**V. 22. ***{when ye shall desire to see, &c.}* For the great afflictions which you shall suffer, you shall after my departure out of the world, desire this my bodily presence with you again, to comfort you. See Matthew 9. 15. John 17. 12. but I shall be no more conversant on earth, after my ascension, until I come again to judgment: therefore beware of seducers coming in my Name.

**V. 23. ***{and they shall say}* See Mat. 24. 23. Mark 13. 21.

*{go not after them}* He speaks here of seducers and false Apostles, whereof he warns. See also Deuter. 13. 3, 8. Jerem. 23. 16. Jerem. 27. 9. It is a dangerous thing to tempt God, by listening to the tempter: God oftentimes therefore gives them over to strong delusions, to believe lies and be damned, who believed not, neither rested contented with the truth, 2 Thes. 2. 11, 12.

**V. 24. ***{as the lightning}* Meaning, that it should be sudden, which Matth. 24. 27. is expressed by, *out of the east, and shineth even unto the west*. See the like manner of speaking, Deut. 4. 32.

*{so shall also the Son of man be in his day}* He speaks here of Christ's second coming, which verse 30. is called the day wherein the Son of man, Christ, shall be revealed: the Apostle calls it *the day of the Lord*, 1 Thes. 5. 2. and Jude 6. *the great day*, and *Paul*, 1 Cor. 1. 7. *the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ*. See also 2 Thes. 1. 7. 1 Thes. 4. 14, etc. Mat. 24. 30. this shall be sudden, as the appearance of lightning in the air, in the twinkling of an eye, 1 Cor. 15.

**V. 25. ***{first must he suffer}* See Mark 8. 31. Luke 9. 2. that is, Christ must first suffer.

*{and be rejected}* That is, be disallowed, contemned and despised of the Jews, as evil or unprofitable; which phrase seems to relate to Psal. 118. 22. See also Mat. 21. 42. 1 Pet. 2. 4. in these words Christ would remove the scandal of the cross, telling them beforehand what he must suffer, lest they should startle and be offended at it, when they should see him suffer: he would also teach us patience in his own example, and confute the vain dreams of Jews concerning his temporal kingdom and glory: for as the Prophets foretold of his sufferings, so did he enter into his kingdom of glory, through ignominy, sufferings, the Cross, and Death. See chap. 24. 26. 1 Pet. 1. 11. Mark 8. 31.

**V. 26. ***{in the days of Noe}* Gen. 7. Mat. 24. 37. 1 Pet. 3. 20. as if he said, no warnings could then persuade men, to leave their sins and turn to God, in amendment of life, but securely flattered themselves, until they all perished: so shall it be when I come to judgment. *Augustin* says truly, *de verb. Dom. Luc. ser.* 37. *Saeculi laetitia, est impunita nequitia*, the joy of this world, is unpunished wickedness: How short and beguiling must that needs be, seeing man is so short lived, and God so inevitably just? *magnæ misericordiæ est, nequitiæ impunitatem non relinquere*; it is God's great mercy not to leave wickedness unpunished here, lest he should be compelled at last to condemn to Hell, he now vouchsafes to correct with the rod, that he might by correction amend us, and not destroy us in the rigor of his justice.

**V. 27. ***{they did eat and drink}* Christ does not herein condemn any holy use of the creatures or the ordinance of God, but security in sinning, and contempt or neglect of God's warnings, as 2 Peter 3. 4, 9, 10, etc. 2 Peter 2. 5, 6, etc. there is a time and measure to eat and drink: a time and measure for all things, Eccles. 3. 1. 1 Cor. 7. 5. So that we suffer not our hearts to be surcharged thereby, and that day of the Lord come upon us unawares, chap. 21. 34.

**V. 28. ***{likewise also, &c.}* Genes. 19. by another example he shows the same which he had before taught them, that at his second coming he should find a secure world unprovided for judgment, the generality thereof thinking of nothing less than their destruction, which should suddenly come on them, as those showers of fire and brimstone upon *Sodom* and *Gomorra*; while they were eating, drinking, or following other worldly affairs.

**V. 30. ***{is revealed}* From heaven, coming in flaming fire to render vengeance to the ungodly, 2 Thes. 1. 8. 2 Pet. 3. 10. Matth. 24. 30.

**V. 31. ***{he which shall be on the house top}* Mat. 24. 17, 18. these things are related as spoken promiscuously concerning the destruction of *Jerusalem*, and the coming of Christ to judgment: this place mentions them as speaking of the general judgment; some things in Mat. 24. are to be understood as peculiar to the destruction of *Jerusalem*; some things as common to both: that is sudden destruction, and vain care to flee, resist, or save any temporal possession out of the flames.

**V. 32. ***{remember Lots wife}* Gen. 19. 26. A memorable example is proposed in her, who contrary to God's command, but looking back to that which she unwillingly left behind her, was thereby overtaken with the judgment: and the monument thereof remained after the time of Christ. *Clem. Rom. ep. ad Corinth.* p. 15. *σημεῖον* *ἐτέθη*, *ὡς* *γενέσθαι* *αὐτὴν* *στήλην* *ἁλὸς* *ἕως* *τῆς* *ἡμέρας* *ταύτης*, she was for a sign, being turned into a pillar of salt (which, says he) continues unto this day.

**V. 33. ***{whosoever shall seek to save, &c.}* See Mat. 16. 25.

**V. 34. ***{there shall be two}* Mat. 24. 40. he calls it night, in respect of the dreadful afflictions and horror of men's souls.

**V. 35. ***{two women}* Mat. 24. 41.

**V. 36. ***{two men shall be in the field}* Meaning, that no society of man in this world shall not then be dissolved, neither shall any be joined in their future condition, as in this present, in respect of temporal relations.

*{one shall be taken}* It imports a reception to safety, as Psal. 27. 10. the Lord *יאספני* *iaaspheni*, *will gather me*.

**V. 37. ***{they answered}* That is, the Disciples.

*{where Lord?}* Mat. 24. 28. in v. 22. he addresses his speech to the Disciples: now they having heard him speak of the taking of some, ask him where they shall be taken.

*{thither will the eagles}* Christ answers them by a similitude and sentence taken out of Job 39. 30. used also Mat. 24. 28. signifying by the flight of the Eagles, the sudden assembling of the Saints unto Christ coming unto judgment, whereof see 1 Thes. 4. 17. 1 Cor. 15. 52.

## CHAPTER 18

**Verse 1. ***{Always to pray}* That is, in every opportunity, *ἐν* *παντὶ* *καιρῷ*; as often as need and season require: this *πάντοτε*, is that which 1 Thes. 5. 17. the Apostle expresses by *ἀδιαλείπτως* without ceasing, which the Syriac gives by *omni tempore*, as Ps. 34. 1. Hebr. *בכל* *עת* *becol gheth*, *at all times*. 70. *ἐν* *παντὶ* *καιρῷ* in every opportunity: according to that of *Augustine ep.* 121. c. 10. the brethren in *Egypt* are wont, *Crebras quidem habere orationes, sed eas tamen brevissimas & raptim quodam modo jaculatas*, to have frequent prayers, but very brief, and in a manner suddenly ejaculated. The meaning here is, the next words interpret, *and not to faint*; not to be dismayed by the length of any afflictions, so as to let go their hope and exercise of prayer, but to watch therein. See on c. 2. 37.

**V. 2. ***{there was in a city a Judge}* The sum and scope of this place is to encourage us to fervency and constancy in prayer: by an argument from the lesser to the greater: if the unrighteous judge, which neither feared God nor reverenced man, was entreated by the importunity of the widow, to do justice to her whom he regarded not; how much more will God be entreated to relieve his dear children, whom he so loved, that he gave his only Son to death for them, whose necessities he knows before they ask? See Isaiah 65. 24.

*{neither regarded man}* He describes a wicked Judge, first by the cause of all such injustice, want of the fear of God. See 2 Chron. 19. 6, 7. next by a mischievous effect thereof come to such an height of impudency, and presuming to abuse power and law, that (all bonds of modesty broken down) he would not so much as vail or dissemble his injustice: but (as outdaring censure, and not caring who knew it) openly exercise it. So the *Syriac* gives this, *neither reverenced man*, and from the sons of men *לא* *מתכחד* *הוא* *non se abscondebat*, he concealed not himself. He was so openly wicked, that he feared not the judgment of God nor man. See Hos. 4. 18.

**V. 3. ***{and there was a widow}* In the description of this Widow are certain degrees of comfort to be gathered, and motives to perseverance in prayer. 1. She was a widow: by so much more helpless; therefore God cautioned in his law for such, Exod. 22. 22. Deut. 24. 17. Isa. 1. 17. 2. She was injured. 3. She had a wicked judge to appeal unto, and so little hope of relief. 4. she complained long without answer: yet her importunity at last prevailed; how much more shall God's children for mercy, which he is readier to show, than we to ask?

**V. 4. ***{though I fear not God, &c.}* Thus further encourages he us to prayer: the Judge, who could say to his own conscience, *that he neither feared God, nor reverenced man*; whom neither equity of the cause, pity to the widow, loathing of injury, zeal of justice, nor love of any good, could move; yet had another motive to make him judge for the widow, lest she should, by continual solicitation, weary him: the word *ὑπωπιάζῃ*, here used, is borrowed from combatants, and signifies, *lest she should*, as with continual strokes, *beat my ears*: the Latins express it by *obtundere*.

**V. 7. ***{though he beare long}* Though he long suffer the wicked to insult, and though he long delay the execution of his judgments; yet will he hear his elect, and be their refuge in due time. Many times, *quod dare vult, differt*, he defers that which he will give, that we may the more fervently desire that which is delayed, *ne vilescat cito datum*, lest that which is quickly given should be undervalued. *Aug. de verb. Dom. serm.* 9.

**V. 8. ***{he will avenge}* When he knows it most seasonable, Psal. 9. 9. Hebr. 10. 37. 2 Per. 3. 9. So is it on God's part, *μακροθυμεῖ* long-suffering, verse 7. (See Exod. 34. 6. Num. 14. 18. Rom. 2. 4. Rom. 9. 22. 1 Per. 3. 20. 2 Pet. 3. 9.) but on the sinners part *speedy vengeance*, verse 8. because it comes long before they would or did expect it. See Mat. 8. 29. 1 Thes. 5. 3. Prov. 24. 22. & 29. 1. Eccles. 9. 12. Isai. 29. 5. & 30. 13. Hab. 2. 7. Jer. 50. 24. Mat. 24. 44. Luke 12. 39. Rev. 3. 3.

*{when the Son of man cometh}* When Christ shall come to judge the world, he shall find few truly faithful on earth: and therefore wonder not at the evil of the times. See Mat. 24. 12. where the immediate causes of so general an apostasy seem to be rendered: that is, persecution of God's ministers: breaking out of false Apostles, or Prophets: malice, the grand nurse of all impiety: abundance of iniquity, and charity (without which faith is dead, Jam. 2. 20.) growing cold; it must needs be hard to find faith where these things are found: yet still God will have a reserve, his elect, here to be excepted, as also in judgment, Ezek. 9. 6. Rev. 7. 3.

**V. 9. ***{which trusted in themselves, &c.}* Or, *as being righteous*; that is, in their own merit and worth, as if for that God were bound to hear them; whereas indeed this is a certain effect and sign of their spiritual blindness, security, hypocrisy and pride, all which is abominable before God; for says *Salvian de gub. Dei* lib. 3. though one study to please God, yet this is a kind of the greatest wickedness, *si se justum praesumat*, if one presume that he is just: and again, *ib.* 4. he says, the greatest accuser of guilty men, is that arrogance which usurps (a show of) innocency; for among many who are guilty of the same crimes, *nullus est criminosior, quam qui se non putat criminosum*: none is more guilty than he who thinks not himself guilty: this only can we add to our sins, that we judge ourselves not sinners.

*{and despise others}* *ἐξουθενοῦντας* *ἐξουδενίζω*, *pro nihilo duco*: here is meant such a despising: as counts others as nothing worth; so do proud hypocrites undervalue all but themselves, the worst of all.

**V. 10. ***{into the temple to pray}* For that was, *בית* *תפלה* *beth tephilla*, a house of prayer, Isaiah 56. 7. *οἶκος* *προσευχῆς*, Matth. 21. 13. See Acts 3. 1. they went up because the temple stood on mount Moriah, 2 Chron. 3. 1. in the threshing floor of *Ornan*. See 2 Sam. 24. 16, 22. where *Abraham* would have sacrificed his only beloved son *Isaac* (Genes. 22. 2.) a type of Christ: hither came saints and hypocrites (as the clean, and unclean into *Noah's* ark) the just and wicked pray: but with much difference and effects. Prayer was ever esteemed a principal part of God's worship: see 1 Kings 8. 29, 30. Psalm 138. 2. here God is said to have put his name, 1 Kings 8. 16. not that God has confined his grace and mercy to that place. See 1 Tim. 2. John 4. 21, 22, 23.) but that the people of God might be restrained from the temples and service of idols: and that this temple might bear a representation of him who was to come thither, Mal. 3. 1. through whom alone the prayers of the faithful are heard, John 14. 13. John 16. 23. Matth. 7. 7. Mark 11. 24.

*{the one a Pharisee, the other a Publican}* The Pharisees being of a strict life, swelled with great opinion of their own sanctity: the Publicans were esteemed of the Jews, an impious, ungodly, and profane people: See concerning Pharisees, Chap. 14. 1. Chap. 15. 1. and of Publicans, Chap. 3. 12. Chap. 15. 1, 2. yet Christ said, *except your righteousnesse exceed the righteousnesse of Scribes and Pharisees, ye shall not enter into the kingdom of heaven*, Matth. 5. 20. and here the humble Publican is justified before the proud Pharisee: so much do God's judgments differ from man's.

*{prayed thus with himself}* *σταθεὶς* *πρὸς* *ἑαυτὸν*, standing or setting himself apart: or by himself: so the Syriac interpreter gives it, *he stood alone*: this scripture shows his proud heart, as far from the true fear of God, as his opinion had set him from the vulgar, whom he counted wicked and unclean: See Isa. 65. 5. This word *σταθεὶς* here determines not any posture or bodily gesture, (for they used also to kneel in prayer, 1 Kings 5. 24. Mic. 6. 6. Dan. 6. 10. Luke 22. 41. Acts 7. 60. and 9. 40. and 20. 36.) it also imports a commencement or beginning of an action, or perseverance therein: he stood (some think) alone, above in the temple, leaving the despised Publican in the utmost porch, or court of the people; so far it was lawful for reputed sinners to come, and strangers also. See *Joseph. contr. Appion.* lib. 2. 1 Kings 8. 41. *he prayed thus*, Greek *ταῦτα*, *these things*; so Syriac: instead of a prayer, he said these things.

*{God I thank thee}* Though he attributed to God's grace all the good he speaks of himself: yet because he placed not all his confidence in the free mercy of God through Christ, in humility accounting all that he had, *losse for Christ*, (Phil. 3. 7, 8.) that he might not be found in his own pretended righteousness, which was of the law but in that which is through the faith of Christ, he was not justified: though he did not here proclaim his merit unto men, but spoke to God: yet the searcher of hearts condemned that secret pride, and confidence in his own merit, which showed itself: 1. in contempt of others (whom he counted ungodly.) 2. in magnifying himself as free from the sins of others, and just in doing those things he spoke of, which probably he did, and possibly might be unreprovable herein before men. (See Phil. 3. 6.) how dangerous is spiritual pride, even in the most glorious and greatest excellencies of men? humility is a safe virtue: leave all to God, who is a plentiful rewarder of the holy, and will abundantly crown his own gifts: and be assured you shall lose nothing thereby: See Matth. 25. 34, etc. Hebr. 6. 10. Hebr. 10. 35. & 11. 26. 2 John 8. Rev. 22. 12.

*{as other men, extortioners, &c.}* As if other men's vices were his virtues; to justify himself, he contemns and condemns others: himself being by so much worse than others, by how much more proud than others: he confesses no faults, but as faultless casts stones at others: which is, as if the sick man should only tell what parts of his body were well affected and sound: it seems he praised God, (as hypocrites use) only to color his ostentation.

*{or even as this Publican}* Hypocrisy is blind at home, and too quick-sighted abroad: it is natural to the malicious, to judge all those evil, whom they hate; when proud hypocrites censure the just, God yet sees how much more damnable the judge is, than the condemned.

**V. 11. ***{I fast twice in the week}* That is, on the second day of the week, to which our Monday answers; and the fifth, which is on our Thursday, according to the tradition of the Pharisees as their writers say; he that had reckoned others faults, will be sure to put his own pretended virtues to account; as if he had perfectly learned that precept, Isai. 1. 16, 17. *Cease to do evil, learn to do well*; yea more than the Law required; as though he were better than the best: he mentions two things of which the Pharisees much gloried, fasting and paying tithes. See Matth. 9. 14. and 23. 23.

*{I give tithes of all}* See on Chap. 11. 42.

**V. 13. ***{the Publican standing afar off}* In that part of the temple, where all sorts of people, Publicans and sinners might come, 1 Kings 8. 41. here he stood in humility, and deep sense of his own unworthiness, far from the altar, and holy of holies, where God more evidently manifested his presence; yet not so far off but that the Pharisee had him in sight, when he said, *or even as this Publican*, verse 11.

*{not lift up}* This was also an argument of an humble and lowly heart, as a self-condemning sinner, he durst not look up toward the throne of God. (the courts of the temple were all open, as they say, *sub Dio*, whereof *Florus* lib. 3. c. 5. said—*vidit illud grande impiae gentis arcanum patens, sub aureo uti caelo*) now as this posture of his signified, dejection of mind and diffidence in his cause, so lifting up the head, or looking up imported boldness and confidence of favor and indemnity, as chap. 21. 28. *when these things begin to come to passe, then look up and lift up your heads, for your redemption draweth nigh*, but this Publican would not lift up his eyes to heaven, judging them, says *Theophylact*, *ἀναξίους*, unworthy to look up to heaven, who had set them so much on earthly things to enjoy them; viz. which contempt of the heavenly.

*{smote his breast}* See Chap. 23. 48. as it were correcting and punishing his heart *ὑπὲρ* *τῶν* *πονηρῶν* *ἐνθυμημάτων*, for its evil thoughts, and as to awaken it from its sleep and security in sin. *Theophylact*.

*{God be merciful to me a sinner}* He well makes haste to his sin's remission, who is displeased with himself; for with the just and merciful judge, *qui se accusat, excusat*; he excuses, who accuses himself. *Prosper*.

**V. 14. ***{justified rather than, &c.}* Acquitted of his sins, and received to mercy; so was not the other, because he justified himself, and was so far from forsaking his sins, that he did not so much as confess them. See Prov. 28. 13.

*{for every one that exalteth himself}* Mat. 23. 12. Luke 14. 11. so that it is true which *Prosper* says after *Augustine*, *Melior est in malis factis humilis confessio, quam in bonis superba gloriatio*; humble confession in evil deeds, is better than proud boasting in good.

**V. 15. ***{brought unto him also infants, &c.}* Mat. 19. 13. Mar. 10. 13.

*{touch them}* Lay his hands upon them, bless, or pray for them: for imposition of hands was anciently a sign thereof. See Gen. 48. Exod. 29. and a common *Symbolum* of blessing among the Jews.

*{they rebuked them}* That is, those that brought them: as Mark 10. 13.

**V. 16. ***{called them}* Those that brought the infants.

*{for of such is the kingdom &c.}* Meaning infants, and those who in simplicity, humility, contempt of the world, innocency, harmlessness, freedom from malice, soon forgetting injuries, and the like, become like little children; if the inheritance of the kingdom of heaven be theirs, who can reasonably bar them of the seal of adoption, baptism? of which it may not improperly be said (which *Jacob* said of the place he rested in) *this is the gate of heaven*, Genes. 28. 17. where did God ever forbid children to be baptized? It is a true speech of *Optatus*, lib. 7. *Quicquid enim Deus fieri noluit, ore suo prohibuit*; whatsoever God would not have done, he has forbidden with his own mouth: that is, the holy Scripture.

**V. 17. ***{whosoever shall not receive, &c.}* Mark 10. 15.

**V. 18. ***{a certain ruler asked him}* Mat. 19. 16. Mark 10. 17. See also on Chap. 14. 1.

**V. 19. ***{Why callest thou me good, &c.}* Because they commonly abused this word, Jesus shows him that he could not confess him to be good, except he also acknowledged him to be God.

**V. 20. ***{Thou knowest the commandments}* It was a very necessary question which he asked concerning a way to eternal life, and a blessed care of a young man: neither does he put it on good words, or anything else than good works really to be done: yet because he asks a legal question, verse 18. *What shall I do?* Christ accommodates his answer thereto: *thou knowest the commandments*: but when the terrified keeper of the prison asked *Paul* the like question, he answered, *believe in the Lord Jesus, and thou shalt be saved*; Acts 16. The scope of this speech is to show, that it is but vain to enquire for the way to heaven, while men trust in their own holiness, or have their hearts set on this world. Here good works and obedience to the Law are proposed, as Levit. 18. 5. Deut. 30. 19. as the whole Law, that showing us our sin, Rom. 7. 7. and miserable condition and disability to be justified by the Law, it may be our Schoolmaster to bring us to Christ. Gal. 3. 24. teaching us that we can no ways be saved, but by his merits: thus Christ would convict the dangerous confidence of this man by remitting him to the second Table of the Law, against which men's sins are more evident and apparent.

*{Do not commit adultery}* Exod. 20. 13, etc.

**V. 21. ***{All these have I kept}* This answer is full of youthful confidence, wherein he was neither sensible of the rigorous weight of the Law: nor his own disability to avoid the curse thereof, nor his own many sins whereof he was guilty; and stood charged before the tribunal of the all-seeing God; for there is no man that lives and sins not, 1 Kings 8. 46.

**V. 22. ***{Yet lackest thou one thing}* This he speaks, first by way of concession, admit it be as you say; next he tries him by this one precept, that he might make the corruption of his heart appear, who pretended unto so absolute an obedience to all the commandments.

**V. 23. ***{he was very sorrowful}* Because he loved the world, and yet would fain be saved in the world to come; it grieved him that he could not have both: carnal gospellers would fain go to Heaven, if they could find a cheap way thereunto: otherwise they will not purchase the hope of future blessedness, with a present price: the reason is, because they do not so much hope for salvation; as fear damnation.

**V. 24. ***{how hardly shall they that have riches, &c.}* That is, as Mark 10. 24. *they that trust in riches*, love them, set their hearts on them, serve them, and so give them that place in their hearts, which God alone should have.

**V. 25. ***{it is easier for a camel, &c.}* This seems to have been a proverb, whereby they signified some impossibility: this he says, *arguens cupiditatem ejus*, taxing his covetousness. *Irenaeus* lib. 4. cap. 26.

**V. 26. ***{Who then can, &c.}* That is, what rich man can be saved?

**V. 27. ***{are possible with God}* Who so governs the hearts of his, that their riches do not blind them, nor take away their hearts from God, Jer. 32. 17.

**V. 28. ***{then Peter said}* See Matthew 19. 27. Mark 10. 28.

**V. 30. ***{more in this present time}* For, the sweet fruit of the spirit, and assurance of eternal life, which the faithful here receive, is more to them than all the world, Phil. 3. 8.

**V. 31. ***{then he took unto him the twelve}* Matthew 20. 17. Mark 10. 3[unclear]. He spoke not concerning his Passion before the multitude, but to his disciples apart: for the multitude was not yet capable of this great mystery, neither did his disciples understand it, when he told it them plainly, verse 34. for two reasons says *Theophylact*: first that he might show them that he should suffer, *οὐκ* *ἄκων* *οὐδὲ* *ὡς* *ψιλὸς* *ἄνθρωπος*, neither unwillingly, neither as mere man, not knowing that he should be crucified: secondly, to teach them to bear patiently, that which was to come, being thereof foretold.

*{we go up to Jerusalem}* There he was to suffer, that the truth might answer the type, which so many ages before preceded in *Isaac*, Gen. 22. and here he foretells certain degrees of his passion: 1. how he should be delivered to the Gentiles, *Pilate* and the Roman Soldiers: 2. how he should be mocked and spitefully entreated, and spitted on: 3. how he should be put to death after scourging, etc. These things are fulfilled, and to balance these sad things, he proposes three things consolatory: 1. that he foresaw, and foretold what he was to suffer; so that it was neither fortuitous, compulsory, nor by the counsel of man; but voluntary, and by the determinate counsel of God for man's redemption. See Acts 2. 23. Acts 4. 28. 2. that herein should be fulfilled all that God had declared (concerning these sufferings of our Redeemer) by his Prophets. See Acts 3. 18. Psal. 22. 1. Matth. 27. 46. Psal. 69. 20, 21. Dan. 9. 26. Isa. 53. 3, 4, etc. 3. that he should rise again the third day according to the Scriptures; Hosea 6. 1. Psalm 16. 10. which was prefigured in *Jonah's* being three days and nights in the belly of the whale, Matth. 12. 39. Luke 11. 29. 30.

**V. 32. ***{the Gentiles}* See on verse 31.

*{spitted on}* See Mat. 26. 67.

**V. 33. ***{they shall scourge him}* Matthew 27. 26. John 19. 1.

**V. 34. ***{this saying was hid from them}* It was the great infirmity of the disciples still dreaming of Christ's temporal kingdom, and restitution of Israel's prosperity by some glorious victories over their enemies, which errors after Christ's resurrection, being cleared by the holy Ghost, better instructing them, they remembered what Christ had said unto them, John 2. 22. thus we see how blind carnal reason is, (specially being infected with some perverse opinion) until it be illuminated by the spirit of regeneration: the things here mentioned, were plainly preached without any trope; yet that which they had once fancied concerning a temporal kingdom, so prepossessed them, that they could not at present understand anything spoken concerning Christ's death and passion; but possibly they supposed that he spoke concerning some other person, or some other mystery.

**V. 35. ***{it came to pass}* Mat. 20. 29. Mark 10. 46.

*{nigh unto Jericho}* Jericho was a city in the tribe of Benjamin, Joshua 18. 21. once great and populous. See concerning it, Joshua 2. 1. and 6. 1. it was destroyed by *Joshua*, and there was a curse pronounced on the repairer thereof, Joshua 6. 16. it was re-edified by *Hiel*, 1 Kings 16. 34. in the siege of Jerusalem by the Romans, *propter perfidiam civium*, for the perfidiousness of the citizens (says *Jerom de loc. Hebraic.*) it was taken and destroyed, and repaired again, a third city being there built, which *Jerom* says, remained to his time.

*{a certain blind man}* *Bartimeus*, Mark 10. 46. this Evangelist names one blind man, (so does St. *Mark* also) who, it seems, was best known of the two mentioned by *Matthew* 20. 29. that which is here historified of *Bartimeus*, verse 35, 36, 37, 38. probably was at Christ's entrance into Jericho, but that which follows to the end of the Chapter, concerning restoring his sight, etc. was done at his going away from the city, as the other two Evangelists write.

**V. 36. ***{hearing the multitude pass by}* Matth. 20. 30. by the noise of their feet, and confused voices (such as use to be heard of men talking one with another) as they went along. So the Syriac expresses it, *& audivit* *קל* *כנשא* *vocem turbae transeuntis*. Some of the multitude telling him upon his enquiry, that it was Jesus of *Nazareth*, whom such a confluence of people attended, passing by.

**V. 38. ***{Jesus thou Son of David}* Matthew 20. 30. See on verse 39.

**V. 39. ***{rebuked him}* Matth. 20. 31.

*{but he cried so much the more}* The multitude reproved him, and enjoined him silence, as not willing that he should trouble Christ with clamors; but he cried so much the louder: not that his mercy needed to be awakened with such cries, *nec clamoribus admonendus est, qui cogitationes videt, Fulgentius de persona Christi* lib. 2. neither is he to be admonished with clamors, which sees the thoughts: but this was an effect of the petitioner's confidence: true faith (like water sources) by so much more strongly breaks over all lets, by how much more it is dammed up: all oppositions strive with it, as the wind with the fire, inflame it more: let not afflictions stop our mouths, but rather administer matter of more fervency in our prayers. See Chap. 22. 44.

*{thou Son of David}* They used to style the *Messias*, because they knew he should come of the stock of *David*, Psalm 132. 11. Acts 2. 30.

**V. 40. ***{Jesus stood and commanded}* Christ loves the importunity of faith, as verse 1. See Psalm 9. 18. See Mat. 20. 32.

**V. 41. ***{what wilt thou, &c.}* Thus he speaks, not because he was ignorant what the blind man desired, who could restore him sight (See Matth. 6.) but for the spectators' sake, that they might take the more notice of the miracle done: God best knows what we want, but will have us perform our duty in asking (*ut cor ad orationem excitet, Greg. hom.* 2. that he may stir up, and awake our heart to prayer) and as occasion serves, testify to others what we beg of him, that others may with us be moved to praise and glorify him.

**V. 42. ***{thy faith hath saved thee}* Here, as in many other like places, chap. 7. 50. chap. 17. 19, etc. he attributes that to faith, which is proper to Christ, whose power to save, faith apprehends, and applies to us for our salvation.

**V. 43. ***{and immediately he received his sight}* This shows that he is that eternal word, John 1. 1. which made all things, and who alone can repair that which is decayed: and thus he exemplifies his promise; *Ask and ye shall have*, Matth. 7.

*{glorifying God}* This is the double effect of the miracle: he ascribes the power and mercy which cured him to God: here was his gratitude. So did that Samaritan, chap. 17. 15. and the people which saw it, they praised God on his behalf, while their blasphemous doctors, attribute that which he did, to *Beelzebub*.

## CHAPTER 19

**Verse 2. ***{Chief among the Publicans}* That Christ vouchsafed his presence in Jericho (once subject to so grievous a curse, c. 18. 35.) exemplifies two notable things. First, that he who came to save sinners, and deliver from the curse, called and saved some out of the most infamous and despicable places, as *Samaria*, John 4. 39. *Galilee*, Mat. 4, etc. that we may know, that in Christ, there is neither *Jew* nor *Grecian*, bond nor free; all are one in him, who is rich in mercy to all that call upon him. See Rom. 10. 12. Gal. 3. 28. Secondly, that it was possible for rich men to be saved, he demonstrates in the example of *Zacheus*; because, though it be very rare, yet it is possible that God should touch the heart of rich men, causing them to give liberally to the poor, and to restore all that which they have evilly gotten, or detained from others; so repenting and believing they may be saved. *Zacheus* is here called *ἀρχιτελώνης*, a chief *Publican*, or chief Officer among them, or a *collector-general* for that place.

**V. 3. ***{who he was}* That is, what manner of person he was, of whom he had heard so much.

*{because he was of a low stature}* Having heard much of the fame of *Jesus*, and by the multitude attending him, that he was now come into *Jericho*, he might possibly out of a natural desire to satisfy his own mind, resolve to overcome the lets thereto: but God had another work in hand, which was to bring salvation to his house; and therefore by his secret providence, all hindrances wrought to the greater evidence of *Zache's* conversion: so his low stature sending him up into the *Sycamore-tree*: and his wealth (which was a common impediment to others) being the matter of alms and restitution rendered him the more exemplary to the multitude, and conspicuous, to God's glory, his own and others comfort, who can imitate him. So all things work together for good to those who love God, Rom. 8. because he loves them first, 1 Joh. 4. 19. yea those very things which to carnal reason seemed impediments thereto.

**V. 4. ***{into a Sycamore}* It concerns not much precisely to know what tree this was, concerning which there are so many opinions: It was not of that kind which is mentioned c. 17. 6. that was *συκάμινος*, a *Sycomine-tree*, a kind of *Mulberry-tree*. See *Dioscorid.* l. 1. c. 144. *Galen de aliment.* l. 2. But this is, *συκομορέα* (as *Suidas* and *Theophylact* write it) or *συκομωραία*. So *Greg. Nazianzen*, *orat.* 39. reads. The Syriac calls it *תאתא* *פכהתא* *tirbo phacchibtho*, a wild Fig, Hebr. *שקמים*, which grew plentifully in those champaign countries, 1 Kings 10. 27. 2 Chron. 1. 15. See *Chrysost. Tom.* 7. p. 404. *edit. Savil. Gregor. Nazianzen orat.* 29.

**V. 5. ***{to day I must abide at thy house}* Christ expected not his invitation, but in mercy prevented him, who probably durst not have invited him: he that was ambitious but to see Christ, obtains much more, to entertain him at his house, and to hear him open the mysteries of eternal life and salvation: God everywhere prevents us, says *Theophylact*, he is a plentiful Rewarder of them that seek him, giving them who but set themselves in the way, more than they could ask or think, Eph. 3. 20. See c. 1. 13. 1 King. 3. 12. 13. when Christ asks of us, he means to give some unvaluable blessing to us.

**V. 6. ***{and he made haste and came down}* Christ had made him willing: *praeparavit sibi ejus animum cujus elegit hospitium, Prosper. cont. collat.* he prepared his mind to receive him whose entertainment he chose. Thus *Zache's* faith broke out, in a ready and speedy obedience. So Gen. 22. 3. Mat. 4. 22. And secondly, in the joy which he conceived at Christ's calling him. See Acts 8. 39. Rom. 14. 17. Gal. 5. 22. true faith cannot be concealed, its own flame will discover it.

**V. 7. ***{they all murmured}* That is, the most part of those present, which were adversaries; neither entertaining him themselves, nor willing that others should, that he might show them mercy: the main cause of their murmuring was, their malice to him, and envy to others who believed in him: these murmuring speeches they cast on in hope to possess the people that he could not be the *Messias*, who frequented the company of sinners. So c. 5. 30. Mat. 9. 11. Luke 7. 39. This was upon the matter to murmur, *quod in domum aegroti intravit medicus, August. de verb. Apost. ser.* 8. that the Physician went into the sick man's house; they arranged Publicans with notorious sinners.

**V. 8. ***{And Zacchaeus stood}* Doubtless Christ spoke many things to instruct him: and possibly *Zache* might say many, which are not here recorded in the Gospel, whose scope is to set down that which may serve to bring us to faith and salvation, John 20. 30, 31. This was a sure sign of *Zache's* conversion, effected by the providence of God, to stop the mouths of murmuring and unbelieving Jews.

*{I restore him fourfold}* According to the Law Exod. 22. 1. Numb. 5. 7. And this is true repentance in case of injurious taking away, or detaining another's goods, which without restitution, were but counterfeit and vain.

**V. 9. ***{this day is salvation come to this house}* In his conversion and doing the works of holy *Abraham*, by God's mercy showed to him and his Family: as if he had said, heretofore *Zache* went with the perishing world in the broad way of destruction; his main care, study, and endeavor, being to get wealth by right or wrong: but this day God has showed mercy in his conversion, so that now he does loathe his former life and conversation: now he has chosen the better part; restoring that which he had by any means injuriously gained, that it may appear that his repentance is true, and so acceptable to the God of mercy; and giving so largely to the poor that he may lay up treasure in heaven, Mat. 6. 19. and a good foundation in store against the time to come, that he may lay hold on eternal life, 1 Tim. 6. 19. by faith (in Christ the only Savior) *which worketh by love*, Gal. 5. 6. Salvation is come to this house wherein the Savior of the Elect, *Christ*, is this day entertained, heard, and believed; this house whose master is become of a Publican, a Convert; of a sinner, a Saint: salvation; the means to, and assurance of eternal life and true blessedness, therein to be enjoyed; his sins free remission, his peace with God, and his justification, is this day sealed unto him by the spirit of regeneration.

*{is the son of Abraham}* To be a son of *Abraham*, is to be chosen freely, Rom. 9. 8. to walk in the steps and faith of *Abraham*, Rom. 4. 11, 12. and generally to do the good works of *Abraham*, John 8. 39. whereby we may be assured of our election to eternal life, Rom. 8. 29, 30. 2 Pet. 1. 10.

**V. 10. ***{the son of man is come to seek, &c.}* Mat. 18. 11. Hereby he confutes their murmuring; showing the end of his coming into the World which was to seek and save sinners, 1 Tim. 1. 15. Mark 2. 17. Mat. 9. 31. therefore among the rest he came to call *Zacheus*. That which was lost by original, as also actual sins, as *Augustine ep.* 106. understands. As also *Ambros. in Luc.* l. 7. 15. and *Prosper. advers. Collat.* c. 18. and so as *Fulgentius* says, *de incarnat. &c.* c. 8. *pro mortuis natus est ad mortem*: He was born to die for the dead, that we after the death of our first nativity which we have of the flesh, by the grace of the Spirit might be born again to life eternal.

**V. 11. ***{kingdom of God}* Meaning a temporal and earthly kingdom, such as they expected by the *Messias*, which they supposed should be manifested now in his approach toward *Jerusalem*. They might think that by doing so much good among the people in several divine miracles, he had gained so much honor and good will, that the people were generally his; and there remained little more now to be done, but going to the Imperial City, and ascending into the Throne and ancient honor of his Father *David*. Thus still dreamed they of a secular kingdom of Christ the *Messias*: to correct this error, he adds to that he had plainly said, this parable; wherein he teaches them, that he should indeed very shortly begin and enter into his Kingdom, but in heaven, into which he must go by his death and passion; so that the glory thereof should not appear until in the end of time, he should come again to judgment.

**V. 12. ***{he said}* See Mat. 25. 14.

*{went into a far country}* By this parable he describes his going up again into his kingdom of glory, and his return to judgment: by the servants are meant the faithful and Ministers of the Church: by Citizens, verse 14. the Jews who rejected them: by the money, their several gifts: by their trade, the usage of the same to God's honor, and the salvation of his people.
